Bloch’s cycle complex and coherent dualizing complexes in positive characteristic
Fei Ren

Abstract




=

Abstract.

Let
X
be a separated scheme of dimension
d
of finite type over a perfect field
k
of positive characteristic
p
.
In this work, we show that Bloch’s cycle complex
\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}
of zero cycles mod
p^{n}
is quasi-isomorphic to the Cartier operator fixed part of a certain dualizing complex from coherent duality theory. From this we obtain new vanishing results for the higher Chow groups of zero cycles with mod
p^{n}
coefficients for singular varieties.

Introduction

Let
X
be a separated scheme of dimension
d
of finite type over a perfect field
k
of positive characteristic
p
.
In this work, we show that Bloch’s cycle complex
\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}
of zero cycles mod
p^{n}
is quasi-isomorphic to the Cartier operator fixed part of a certain dualizing complex from coherent duality theory. From this we obtain new vanishing results for the higher Chow groups of zero cycles with mod
p^{n}
coefficients for singular varieties.

As the first candidate for a motivic complex,
Bloch introduced his cycle complex
\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}
in
[
3
]
under the framework of Beilinson-Lichtenbaum. Let
i
be an integer, and
\Delta^{i}=\operatorname{Spec}k[T_{0},\dots,T_{i}]/\penalty(\sum T_{j}-1)
.
Here
\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}:=z_{0}(-,-\bullet)
is
a complex of sheaves in the Zariski or the étale topology. The global sections of its degree
(-i)
-term
z_{0}(X,i)
is the free abelian group generated by dimension
i
-cycles in
X\times\Delta^{i}
intersecting all faces properly and the differentials are the alternating sums of the cycle-theoretic intersection of the cycle with each face
(cf.
Section

2
).
Let
\pi:X\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}k
be the structure morphism of
X
. Let
W_{n}X:=(|X|,W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X})
, where
|X|
is the underlying topological space of
X
, and
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is the sheaf of length
n
truncated Witt vectors. Let
W_{n}\pi:W_{n}X\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k
be the morphism induced from
\pi
via functoriality.
In this article, our aim is to arrive at a triangle

\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n}\rightarrow(W_{n}\pi)^{!}W_{n}k\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}-1}(W_{n}\pi)^{!}W_{n}k\xrightarrow{+1}

in the derived category
D^{b}(X,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
, in either the étale topology, or the Zariski topology with an extra
k=\overline{k}
assumption.
Here
(-)^{!}
is the extraordinary inverse image functor in the coherent setting as defined in
[
21
, VII.3.4]
[
9
, (3.3.6)]
, and
(W_{n}\pi)^{!}W_{n}k
is a dualizing complex for coherent sheaves on
W_{n}X
.
This is a generalization of the top degree case of
[
14
, 8.3]
,
which in particular implies the above triangle in the smooth case.
Our work is clearly inspired by Kato’s paper
[
30
]
, but the proofs in this article do not use any
results from
loc. cit.

Let us briefly recall Kato’s work in
[
30
]
and introduce our main object of interest, the complex
K_{n,X,log}
.
According to Grothendieck’s coherent duality theory, there exists an explicit Zariski complex
K_{n,X}
of quasi-coherent sheaves representing the dualizing complex
(W_{n}\pi)^{!}W_{n}k
(such a complex
K_{n,X}
is called a residual complex, cf.
[
21
, VI.3.1]
).
There is a natural Cartier operator
C^{\prime}:K_{n,X}\rightarrow K_{n,X}
, which is compatible with the classical Cartier operator
C:W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}
in the smooth case via Ekedahl’s quasi-isomorphism (see
Theorem

1.9
). Here
W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X}
denotes the degree
d:=\dim X
part of the
p
-typical de Rham-Witt complex. We define the complex
K_{n,X,log}
to be the mapping cone of
C^{\prime}-1
.
Kato considered in
[
30
]
the
{\mathrm{FRP}}
counterpart, where
{\mathrm{FRP}}
is the "flat and relatively perfect" topology (this is a topology with étale coverings and with the underlying category lying in between the small and the big étale site).
He showed that
K_{n,X,log}
serves in the
{\mathrm{FRP}}
topology as a dualizing complex in a rather big triangulated subcategory of the derived category of
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-sheaves, containing all coherent sheaves and the logarithmic de Rham-Witt sheaves
[
30
, 0.1]
.
Kato also showed that in the smooth setting,
K_{n,X,log}
is concentrated in one degree and its only nonzero cohomology sheaf is the top degree logarithmic de Rham-Witt sheaf
[
30
, 3.4]
.
An analogoue of the latter statement holds naturally on the small étale site.
Rülling later observed that with a trick from
p^{-1}
-linear algebra,
[
30
, 3.4]
can be done on the Zariski site as well, as long as one assumes
k=\overline{k}
(cf.
Proposition

1.15
). Comparing this with the Kato-Moser complex
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X}
(cf.
Section

4
), which is precisely the Gersten resolution of the logarithmic de Rham-Witt sheaf in the smooth setting, one gets an identification in the smooth setting
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X}\simeq K_{n,X,log}
in the Zariski topology. Similar as in
[
30
, 4.2]
(cf.
Proposition

1.21
),
Rülling also built up the localization sequence for
K_{n,X,log}
on the Zariski site in his unpublished notes (cf.
Proposition

1.22
).
Compared with the localization sequence for
\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}

[
2
, 1.1]
and for
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X}
(which trivially holds in the Zariski topology), it is reasonable to expect a chain map relating these objects in general.

The aim of this article is to construct a quasi-isomorphism
\overline{\zeta}_{log}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log}
, for a possibly singular
k
-scheme
X
.
When pre-composed with Zhong’s quasi-isomorphism
\overline{\psi}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n}\rightarrow\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X}

[
44
, 2.16]
, we therefore
obtain another perspective of Bloch’s cycle complex with
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-coefficients in terms of coherent dualizing complexes. More precisely, we prove the following result.

Theorem 0.1

(
Theorem

5.10
,
Theorem

6.1
)
.

Let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over a perfect field
k
of positive characteristic
p
. Then there exists a chain map

\overline{\zeta}_{log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},

which is a quasi-isomorphism. If moreover
k
is algebraically closed, then this chain map induces a quasi-isomorphism on the Zariski site

\overline{\zeta}_{log,{\operatorname{Zar}}}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{Zar}}}.

Composition with Zhong’s quasi-isomorphism
\overline{\psi}
, yields the chain map

\overline{\zeta}_{log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\circ\overline{\psi}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

which is a quasi-isomorphism. If moreover
k=\overline{k}
, then the composition

\overline{\zeta}_{log,{\operatorname{Zar}}}\circ\overline{\psi}_{{\operatorname{Zar}}}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}/p^{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{Zar}}}

is a quasi-isomorphism as well.

We explain more on the motivation behind the definition of
K_{n,X,log}
. For a smooth
k
-scheme
X
, the logarithmic de Rham-Witt sheaves can be defined in two ways: either as the subsheaves of
W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}
generated by log forms, or as the invariant part under the Cartier operator
C
.
In the singular case, these two perspectives give two different (complexes of) sheaves.
The first definition can also be done in the singular case, and this was studied by Morrow
[
35
]
.
For the second definition one has to replace
W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}
by a dualizing complex on
W_{n}X
:
for this Grothendieck’s duality theory yields a canonical and explicit choice, and this is what we have denoted by
K_{n,X}
. And then this method leads naturally to Kato’s and also our construction of
K_{n,X,log}
. Now with our main theorem one knows that
\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n}
sits in a distinguished triangle

\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n}\rightarrow K_{n,X}\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}-1}K_{n,X}\xrightarrow{+1}

in the derived category
D^{b}(X,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
, in either the étale topology, or the Zariski topology with the extra assumption
k=\overline{k}
.
In particular, if
X
is Cohen-Macaulay of pure dimension
d
, then the triangle above becomes

\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n}\rightarrow W_{n}\omega_{X}[d]\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}-1}W_{n}\omega_{X}[d]\xrightarrow{+1}.

where
W_{n}\omega_{X}
is the only non-vanishing cohomology sheaf of
K_{n,X}
(if
n=1
,
W_{1}\omega_{X}=\omega_{X}
is the usual dualizing sheaf on
X
), and
\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n}
is concentrated at degree
-d
(cf.
Proposition

8.1
).

As corollaries, we arrive at some properties of the higher Chow groups of
0
-cycles with
p
-primary torsion coefficients. (We have specialized several statements here in the introduction part. Please see the main text for more general statements.)

Corollary 0.2

(
Proposition

8.2
,
Corollary

8.6
,
Corollary

8.3
,

Corollary

8.4
,
Corollary

8.9
,
Corollary

8.12
,

Corollary

8.14
)
.

Let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
and
\pi:X\rightarrow k
be the structure map of
X
.

(1)

(Cartier invariance)
Assume
k=\overline{k}
. Then

{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,q;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})=H^{-q}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X,{\operatorname{Zar}}})^{C^{\prime}_{\operatorname{Zar}}-1}.

(2)

(Semisimplicity)
Assume
k=\overline{k}
.
Let
X
be proper over
k
. Then for any
q
,

H^{-q}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X})_{\mathrm{ss}}={\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,q;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}}W_{n}k.

(We refer to
Definition

A.4
and
Remark

A.5
(2) for the definition of the
semi-simple part.)

(3)

(Relation with
p
-torsion Poincaré duality)
There is an isomorphism in
D^{b}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})

K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\simeq R\pi^{!}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}),

where
R\pi^{!}
is the extraordinary inverse image functor defined in
[
41
, Exposé XVIII, Thm 3.1.4]
.

(4)

(Affine vanishing)
Assume
k=\overline{k}
.
Suppose
X
is affine and Cohen-Macaulay of pure dimension
d
. Then

{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,q,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})=0

for
q\neq d
.

(5)

(Étale descent)
Assume
k=\overline{k}
.
Suppose
X
is Cohen-Macaulay of pure relative dimension
d
. Then

R^{i}\epsilon_{*}(\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n})=R^{i}\epsilon_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}=0,\quad i\neq-d.

(6)

(Invariance under rational resolution)
Assume
k=\overline{k}
.
For a rational resolution of singularities
f:\widetilde{X}\rightarrow X
(cf.
Definition

8.10
) of an integral
k
-scheme
X
of pure dimension, the trace map induces an isomorphism

{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(\widetilde{X},q;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})\xrightarrow{\simeq}{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,q;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})

for each
q
.

(7)

(Galois descent)
Assume
k=\overline{k}
.
Let
f:Y\rightarrow X
be a finite étale Galois map with Galois group
G
. Then

{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,d;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})={\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(Y,d;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})^{G}.

Now we give a more detailed description of the structure of this article.

The general setting is that
X
is a separated scheme of finite type over a perfect field
k
of positive characteristic
p
(except in
Section

1.1
, where a scheme refers to a noetherian scheme of finite Krull dimension).
In Part 1, we review the basic properties of the chain complexes to appear.
Section

1
is devoted to the properties of the complex
K_{n,X,log}
, the most important object of our studies. We study the Zariski version in Sections

1.2
-
1.5
. Following an idea in
[
30
]
, we define the Cartier operator
C^{\prime}
for the residual complex
K_{n,X}
, and then define the complex
K_{n,X,log}
to be the mapping cone of
C^{\prime}-1
in
Section

1.2
. We compare our
C^{\prime}
with the classical definition of the Cartier operator
C
for top degree de Rham-Witt sheaves in
Section

1.3
.
The necessary computations are presented in the subsections
1.3.2
and
1.3.3
. The localization sequence is discussed in
Section

1.4
.
The main ingredients are a surjectivity result for
C^{\prime}-1
, which needs the base field
k
to be algebraically closed, see
Proposition

1.15

(see also
Appendix

A
for a short discussion of the necessary semilinear algebra),
the trace map of a nilpotent thickening (cf.
Proposition

1.21
), and the localization sequence (cf.
Proposition

1.22
). They were already observed by Rülling and are only re-presented here by the author. After a short discussion on functoriality in
Section

1.5
, we move to the étale case in
Section

1.6
.
Most of the properties mentioned above continue to hold in a similar manner, moreover
the surjectivity of
C_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-1:W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}^{d}
over a smooth
k
-scheme
X
only requires
k
to be perfect.
This enables us to build the quasi-isomorphism
\zeta_{log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
for any perfect field
k
in the next part.
In the remaining sections
2
-
4

of Part 1 we recall Bloch’s cycle complex
\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}
, Kato’s complex of Milnor
K
-theory
C^{M}_{X,t}
,
and the Kato-Moser complex of logarithmic de Rham-Witt sheaves
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}
.
There are no new results in these three short sections.

In Part 2 we construct the quasi-isomorphism
\overline{\zeta}_{log}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log}
and study its properties in
Section

5
. We first construct a chain map
\zeta:C^{M}_{X}\rightarrow K_{n,X}
and then show that it induces a chain map
\zeta_{log}:C^{M}_{X}\rightarrow K_{n,X,log}
. This map actually factors through the chain map
\overline{\zeta}_{log}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X}\rightarrow K_{n,X,log}
via the Bloch-Gabber-Kato isomorphism
[
4
, 2.8]
. We prove that
\overline{\zeta}_{log}
is a quasi-isomorphism for
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
, and also for
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
with an extra
k=\overline{k}
assumption. In
Section

6
, we review the main results of
[
44
, §2]
and compose Zhong’s quasi-isomorphism
\overline{\psi}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n}\rightarrow\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X}
with
\overline{\zeta}_{log}
.
This composite map enables us to use tools from the coherent duality theory in the calculation of certain higher Chow groups of
0
-cycles.

In Part 3 we discuss the applications.
Section

7
mainly serves as a preparatory section for
Section

8
. In
Section

8
we arrive at several results for higher Chow groups of
0
-cycles with
p
-primary torsion coefficients: affine vanishing, finiteness (reproof of a theorem of Geisser), étale descent, and invariance under rational resolutions.

.

.

Part I
The complexes

1.
Kato’s complex
K_{n,X,log,t}

1.1.
Preliminaries: Residual complexes and Grothendieck’s duality theory

The general references for this topic are
[
21
]
and
[
9
]
.
All schemes in
Section

1.1
will be assumed to be noetherian of finite Krull dimension.

1.1.1.
Residual complexes

A
residual complex
(
[
9
, p.125]
,
[
21
, p.304]
) on a scheme
X
is a complex
K
such that

•

K
is bounded as a complex,

•

all the terms of
K
are quasi-coherent and injective
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules,

•

the cohomology sheaves are coherent, and

•

there is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules

\bigoplus_{q\in\mathbb{Z}}K^{q}\simeq\bigoplus_{x\in X}i_{x*}J(x),

where
i_{x}:\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\rightarrow X
is the canonical map and
J(x)
is the quasi-coherent sheaf on
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
associated to an injective hull of
k(x)
over
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
(i.e. the unique injective
\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
-module up to non-unique isomorphisms which contains
k(x)
as a submodule and such that, for any
0\neq a\in J(x)
, there exists an element
b\in\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
with
0\neq ba\in k(x)
.)

Given a residual complex
K
on
X
and a point
x\in X
, there is a unique integer
d_{K}(x)
, such that
i_{x*}J(x)
is a direct summand of
K^{q}
, i.e.,

K^{q}\simeq\bigoplus_{d_{K}(x)=q}i_{x*}J(x).

The assignment
x\mapsto d_{K}(x)
is called the
codimension function on
X
associated to
K
(cf.
[
21
, IV, 1.1(a)]
[
9
, p.125]
).
We define the
associated filtration

Z^{\bullet}(K)=\{x\in X\mid d_{K}(x)\geq p\}.

On each irreducible component of
X
, this filtration equals the shifted codimension filtration.
By the
codimension filtration
of a scheme
X
we refer to the filtration
Z^{\bullet}
with

Z^{p}=\{x\in X\mid\dim\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\geq p\}.

If
Z^{\bullet}
is a filtration on
X
, we denote by
Z^{\bullet}[n]
the shifted filtration with
Z^{\bullet}[n]^{p}=Z^{p+n}
.

Let
Z^{\bullet}
be a filtration on
X
such that when restricted to each irreducible component, it is the shifted codimension filtration.
For any bounded below complex
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
, choose a bounded below injective resolution
\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}
of
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
.
Denote by
\uline{\Gamma}_{Z^{p}}
the sheafified local cohomology functor with support in
Z^{p}
, cf.
[
21
, p223 5.]
.
Then one has a natural decreasing exhaustive filtration by subcomplexes of
\mathcal{I}^{\bullet}
:

\dots\supset\uline{\Gamma}_{Z^{p}}(\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})\supset\uline{\Gamma}_{Z^{p+1}}(\mathcal{I}^{\bullet})\supset\dots.

This filtration is stalkwise bounded below. Now consider the
E_{1}
-spectral sequence associated to this filtration

E_{1}^{p,q}\Rightarrow H^{p+q}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}).

The
Cousin complex
(
[
9
, p.105]
)
E_{Z^{\bullet}}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
associated to
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
is defined to be the
0
-th line of the
E_{1}
-page, namely

E_{Z^{\bullet}}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}):=(E_{1}^{p,0}=\mathcal{H}^{p}_{Z^{p}/Z^{p+1}}(\mathcal{F}),d_{1}^{p,0}).

Here
\mathcal{H}^{p}_{Z^{p}/Z^{p+1}}(\mathcal{F}):=R^{p}\uline{\Gamma}_{Z^{p}/Z^{p+1}}(\mathcal{F})
and
\uline{\Gamma}_{Z^{p}/Z^{p+1}}(\mathcal{F}):=\uline{\Gamma}_{Z^{p}}(\mathcal{F})/\uline{\Gamma}_{Z^{p+1}}(\mathcal{F})
(cf.
[
21
, p.225 Variation 7]
).
We will also use the shortened notation
E
for
E_{Z^{\bullet}}
when the filtration
Z^{\bullet}
is clear from the context.
Note that
E_{Z^{\bullet}}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
is indeed a Cousin complex in the sense of
[
9
, p.105]
by the canonical functorial isomorphism
[
21
, p.226]
[
9
, (3.1.4)]

\mathcal{H}^{i}_{Z^{p}/Z^{p+1}}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\bigoplus_{x\in Z^{p}-Z^{p+1}}i_{x*}(H^{i}_{x}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})),

where
i_{x}:\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{X,x}\rightarrow X
is the canonical map, and
H^{i}_{x}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
is the local cohomology groups at
x
as defined in
[
21
, p.225 Variation 8]
. By slight abuse of notation we denote by the same notation
H^{i}_{x}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
the quasi-coherent sheaf on
\operatorname{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{X,x}
associated to this local cohomology group, and it is a sheaf supported on the closed point if it is nonzero.

Let
X
be a scheme and
Z^{\bullet}
be a filtration on
X
which is a shift of the codimension filtration on each irreducible component of
X
.
Denote by
Q
the natural functor from the category of complexes of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules to the derived category of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. Then
E_{Z^{\bullet}}
and
Q
induce quasi-inverses (
[
9
, 3.2.1]
)

(1.1.1)

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\text{dualizing complexes whose}\\
\text{associated filtration is $Z^{\bullet}$}\end{array}\right){\mathrel{\to}^{Q}_{E_{Z^{\bullet}}}}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\text{residual complexes whose}\\
\text{associated filtration is $Z^{\bullet}$}\end{array}\right).

For the definition of a dualizing complex (as an object in the derived category) we refer the reader to
[
9
, p.118]
.
Since we have assumed that
X
is noetherian and of finite Krull dimension, there always exists a residual complex on
X
.

1.1.2.
The functor
f^{\triangle}

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a finite type morphism between noetherian schemes of finite Krull dimension and let
K
be a residual complex on
Y
with associated filtration
Z^{\bullet}:=Z^{\bullet}(K)
and codimension function
d_{K}
.
Define the function
d_{f^{\triangle}K}
on
X
to be (
[
9
, (3.2.4)]
)

d_{f^{\triangle}K}(x):=d_{K}(f(x))-\mathrm{trdeg}(k(x)/k(f(x))

(so far the subscript
f^{\triangle}K
is simply regarded as a formal symbol), and define
f^{\triangle}Z^{\bullet}
accordingly

f^{\triangle}Z^{\bullet}=\{x\in X\mid d_{f^{\triangle}K}(x)\geq p\}.

Notice that if
f
has constant fiber dimension
r
,
f^{\triangle}Z^{\bullet}
is simply
f^{-1}Z^{\bullet}[r]
.

Following
[
21
, VI, 3.1]
[
9
, 3.2.2]
, we list some properties of the functor
f^{\triangle}
below.

Proposition 1.1
.

There exists a functor

f^{\triangle}:\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\text{residual complexes on $Y$}\\
\text{with filtration $Z^{\bullet}$}\\
\end{array}\right)\rightarrow\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\text{residual complexes on $X$}\\
\text{with filtration $f^{\triangle}Z^{\bullet}$}\\
\end{array}\right)

having the following properties (we assume all schemes are noetherian schemes of finite Krull dimension, and all morphisms are of finite type).

(1)

If
f
is finite, there is an isomorphism of complexes (
[
21
, VI.3.1]
)

\psi_{f}:f^{\triangle}K\xrightarrow{\simeq}E_{f^{-1}Z^{\bullet}}(\overline{f}^{*}R{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X},K))\simeq\overline{f}^{*}{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X},K),

where
\overline{f}^{*}:=f^{-1}(-)\otimes_{f^{-1}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is the pullback functor associated to the map of ringed spaces
\overline{f}:(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\rightarrow(Y,f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})
. Since
\overline{f}
is flat, the pullback functor
\overline{f}^{*}
is exact. The last isomorphism is due to the fact that
\overline{f}^{*}{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X},K)
is a residual complex with respect to filtration
f^{-1}Z^{\bullet}
(see
[
21
, VI.4.1]
,
[
9
, (3.4.5)]
).

(2)

If
f
is smooth and separated of relative dimension
r
, there is an isomorphism of complexes (
[
21
, VI.3.1]
)

\varphi_{f}:f^{\triangle}K\xrightarrow{\simeq}E_{f^{-1}Z^{\bullet}[r]}(\Omega_{X/Y}^{r}[r]\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}^{L}Lf^{*}K)=E_{f^{-1}Z^{\bullet}[r]}(\Omega_{X/Y}^{r}[r]\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}f^{*}K).

The last equality is due to the flatness of
f
and local freeness of
\Omega_{X/Y}^{r}
.

If
f
is étale (or more generally residually stable, see (
5
) below), this becomes

\varphi_{f}:f^{\triangle}K\xrightarrow{\simeq}E_{f^{-1}Z^{\bullet}}(f^{*}K)\simeq f^{*}K.

The last isomorphism is due to
[
21
, VI.5.3]
.
In particular, if
f=j:X\hookrightarrow Y
is an open immersion,
j^{\triangle}K=j^{*}K
is a residual complex with respect to filtration
X\cap Z^{\bullet}
(
[
9
, p.128]
).

(3)

If
f
is finite étale, the chain maps
\psi_{f},\varphi_{f}
are compatible. Namely, for a given residual complex
K
on
Y
, there exists an isomorphism of complexes

\overline{f}^{*}{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X},K)\xrightarrow{\simeq}f^{*}K

as defined in
[
9
, (2.7.9)]
, such that the following diagram of complexes commutes

\textstyle{\overline{f}^{*}{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X},K)}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{f^{\triangle}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{f}}
\textstyle{f^{*}K.}

(4)

(Composition) If
f:X\rightarrow Y
and
g:Y\rightarrow Z
are two such morphisms, there is an natural isomorphism of functors (
[
9
, (3.2.3)]
)

c_{f,g}:(gf)^{\triangle}\xrightarrow{\simeq}f^{\triangle}g^{\triangle}.

(5)

(Residually stable base change) Following
[
9
, p.132]
, we say a (not necessarily locally finite type) morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
between locally noetherian schemes is
residually stable
if

•

f
is flat,

•

the fibers of
f
are discrete and for all
x\in X
, the extension
k(x)/k(f(x))
is algebraic, and

•

the fibers of
f
are Gorenstein schemes.

As an example, an étale morphism is residually stable. For more properties of residually stable morphisms, see
[
21
, VI, §5]
. Let
f
be a morphism of finite type, and
u
be a residually stable morphism. Let

(1.1.2)

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{u^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{Y}

be a cartesian diagram. Then there is an natural transformation between functors (
[
21
, VI.5.5]
)

d_{u,f}:f^{\prime\triangle}u^{*}\xrightarrow{\simeq}u^{\prime*}f^{\triangle}.

(6)

f^{\triangle}
is compatible with translation and tensoring with an invertible sheaf. More precisely, for an invertible sheaf
\mathcal{L}
on
Y
and a locally constant
\mathbb{Z}
-valued function
n
on
Y
, one has canonical isomorphisms of complexes
[
9
, (3.3.9)]

f^{\triangle}(\mathcal{L}[n]\otimes K)\simeq(f^{*}K)[n]\otimes f^{\triangle}K\simeq(f^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes f^{\triangle}K)[n].

More properties and compatibility diagrams can be found in
[
9
, §3.3]
and
[
21
, VI, §3, §5]
.

1.1.3.
Trace map for residual complexes

Proposition 1.2
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a
proper
morphism between noetherian schemes of finite Krull dimensions and let
K
be a residual complex on
Y
. Then there exists a map of complexes

\operatorname{Tr}_{f}:f_{*}f^{\triangle}K\rightarrow K,

such that the following properties hold (
[
9
, §3.4]
).

(1)

If
f
is finite,
\operatorname{Tr}_{f}
at a given residual complex
K
agrees with the following composite as a map of complexes (
[
9
, (3.4.8)]
):

(1.1.3)

\operatorname{Tr}_{f}:f_{*}f^{\triangle}K\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\psi_{f}}{\mathcal{H}om}_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X},K)\xrightarrow{\text{ev. at 1}}K.

(2)

If
f:\mathbf{P}^{d}_{Y}\rightarrow Y
is the natural projection, then the trace map
\operatorname{Tr}_{f}
at
K
, as a map in the derived category
D_{c}^{b}(Y)
, agrees with the following composite (
[
9
, p.151]
)

f_{*}f^{\triangle}K\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\varphi_{f}}Rf_{*}(\Omega_{\mathbf{P}^{n}_{X}/X}^{n}[n])\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}K\xrightarrow{}K.

The first map is induced from
\varphi_{f}
followed by the projection formula (
[
9
, (2.1.10)]
), and the second map is induced by base change from the following isomorphism of groups(
[
9
, (2.3.3)]
)

\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\simeq}H^{d}(\mathbf{P}^{d}_{\mathbb{Z}},\Omega_{\mathbf{P}^{d}_{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathbb{Z}}^{d})=\check{H}^{d}({\mathfrak{U}},\Omega_{\mathbf{P}^{d}_{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathbb{Z}}^{d}),\quad 1\mapsto(-1)^{\frac{d(d+1)}{2}}\frac{dt_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dt_{d}}{t_{1}\dots t_{d}},

where
{\mathfrak{U}}=\{U_{0},...,U_{d}\}
is the standard covering of
\mathbf{P}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{d}
and the
t_{i}
’s are the coordinate functions on
U_{0}
.

(3)

(Functoriality,
[
9
, 3.4.1(1)]
)

\operatorname{Tr}_{f}
is functorial with respect to residual complexes with the same associated filtration.

(4)

(Composition,
[
9
, 3.4.1(2)]
)
If
g:Y\rightarrow Z
is another proper morphism, then

\operatorname{Tr}_{gf}=\operatorname{Tr}_{g}\circ g_{*}(\operatorname{Tr}_{f})\circ(gf)_{*}c_{f,g}.

(5)

(Residually stable base change,
[
21
, VI.5.6]
)
Notations are the same as in diagram (
1.1.2
), and we assume
f
proper and
u
residually stable. Then the diagram

\textstyle{u^{*}Rf_{*}f^{\triangle}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u^{*}\operatorname{Tr}_{f}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{u^{*}}
\textstyle{Rf^{\prime}_{*}u^{\prime*}f^{\triangle}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{Rf^{\prime}_{*}(d_{u,f})}
\textstyle{Rf^{\prime}_{*}f^{\prime\triangle}u^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{f^{\prime}}}

commutes.

(6)

\operatorname{Tr}_{f}
is compatible with translation and tensoring with an invertible sheaf (
[
9
, p.148]
).

(7)

(Grothendieck-Serre duality,
[
9
, 3.4.4]
) If
f:X\rightarrow Y
is proper, then for any
\mathcal{F}\in D^{-}_{\mathrm{qc}}(X)
, the composition

Rf_{*}R{\mathcal{H}om}_{X}(\mathcal{F},f^{\triangle}K)\rightarrow R{\mathcal{H}om}_{Y}(Rf_{*}\mathcal{F},Rf_{*}f^{\triangle}K)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{f}}R{\mathcal{H}om}_{Y}(Rf_{*}\mathcal{F},K)

is an isomorphism in
D^{+}_{\mathrm{qc}}(Y)
.

More properties and compatibility diagrams can be found in
[
9
, §3.4]
and
[
21
, VI, §4-5; VII, §2]
.

1.2.
Definition of
K_{n,X,log}

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p
. Let
W_{n}k
be the ring of Witt vectors of length
n
of
k
.
Notice that
W_{n}k
is an injective
W_{n}k
-module by Baer’s criterion. So
\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k
is a Gorenstein scheme by
[
21
, V. 9.1(ii)]
, and its structure sheaf placed at degree 0 is a residual complex
(with codimension function being the zero function and the associated filtration being
Z^{\bullet}(W_{n}k)=\{Z^{0}(W_{n}k)\}
, where
Z^{0}(W_{n}k)
is the set of the unique point in
\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k
)
by
[
21
, p299 1.]
and the categorical equivalence (
1.1.1
) (note that in this case the Cousin functor
E_{Z^{\bullet}(W_{n}k)}
applied to
W_{n}k
is still
W_{n}k
). This justifies the symbol
(W_{n}F_{k})^{\triangle}
to appear.
To avoid possible confusion we will distinguish the source and target of the absolute Frobenius by using the symbols
k_{1}=k_{2}=k
. Absolute Frobenius is then written as
F_{k}:(\operatorname{Spec}k_{1},k_{1})\rightarrow(\operatorname{Spec}k_{2},k_{2})
, and the
n
-th Witt lift is written as
W_{n}F_{k}:(\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k_{1},W_{n}k_{1})\rightarrow(\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k_{2},W_{n}k_{2})
.
There is a natural isomorphism of
W_{n}k_{1}
-modules (the last isomorphism is given by
Proposition

1.1
(1))

(1.2.1)

\displaystyle W_{n}k_{1}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\overline{W_{n}F_{k}}^{*}\operatorname{Hom}_{W_{n}k_{2}}((W_{n}F_{k})_{*}(W_{n}k_{1}),W_{n}k_{2})\simeq(W_{n}F_{k})^{\triangle}(W_{n}k_{2}),

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto a\otimes[(W_{n}F_{k})_{*}1\mapsto 1]\quad(=[(W_{n}F_{k})_{*}a\mapsto 1]),

where
\overline{W_{n}F_{k}}:(\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k_{1},W_{n}k_{1})\rightarrow(\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k_{2},(W_{n}F_{k})_{*}(W_{n}k_{1}))
is the natural map of ringed spaces, and the Hom set is given the
(W_{n}F_{k})_{*}(W_{n}k_{1})
-module structure via the first place.
In fact, it is clearly a bijection: identify the target with
W_{n}k_{2}
via the evaluate-at-1 map, then one can see that the map (
1.2.1
) is identified with
a\mapsto(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(a)
.

Let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over
k
of dimension
d
with structure map
\pi:X\rightarrow k
.
Since
W_{n}k
is a Gorenstein scheme as we recalled in the last paragraph,

K_{n,X}:=(W_{n}\pi)^{\triangle}W_{n}k

is a residual complex on
W_{n}X
, associated to the codimension function
d_{K_{n,X}}
with

d_{K_{n,X}}(x)=-\dim\overline{\{x\}},

and the filtration
Z^{\bullet}(K_{n,X})=\{Z^{p}(K_{n,X})\}
with

Z^{p}(K_{n,X})=\{x\in X\mid\dim\overline{\{x\}}\leq-p\}.

In particular,
K_{n,X}
is a bounded complex of injective quasi-coherent
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules with coherent cohomologies sitting in degrees

[-d,0].

If
n=1
, we write
K_{X}:=K_{1,X}
.
Now we turn to the definition of
C^{\prime}
.
Denote the level
n
Witt lift of the absolute Frobenius
F_{X}
by
W_{n}F_{X}:(W_{n}X_{1},W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}})\rightarrow(W_{n}X_{2},W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X_{2}})
.
The structure maps of
W_{n}X_{1},W_{n}X_{2}
are
W_{n}\pi_{1},W_{n}\pi_{2}
respectively. These schemes fit into a commutative diagram

\textstyle{W_{n}X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}F_{X}}
\textstyle{W_{n}X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}F_{k}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k_{2}.}

Denote

K_{n,X_{i}}:=(W_{n}\pi_{i})^{\triangle}(W_{n}k_{i}),\quad i=1,2.

Via functoriality, one has a
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X_{1}}
-linear map

(1.2.2)

\displaystyle K_{n,X_{1}}=(W_{n}\pi_{1})^{\triangle}(W_{n}k_{1})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\simeq]{(W_{n}\pi_{1})^{\triangle}(\ref{(1.0.1)})}(W_{n}\pi_{1})^{\triangle}(W_{n}F_{k})^{\triangle}(W_{n}k_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\simeq(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}(W_{n}\pi_{2})^{\triangle}(W_{n}k_{2})\simeq(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X_{2}}.

Here the isomorphism at the beginning of the second line is given by
Proposition

1.1
(4).
Then via the adjunction with respect to the morphism
W_{n}F_{X}
, one has a
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X_{2}}
-linear map

(1.2.3)

\displaystyle C^{\prime}:=C^{\prime}_{n}:(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}K_{n,X_{1}}\xrightarrow[\simeq]{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(\ref{(1.0.2)})}(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X_{2}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}F_{X}}}K_{n,X_{2}},

where the last map is the trace map of
W_{n}F_{X}
for residual complexes.
We call it the (level
n
)
Cartier operator
for residual complexes.
We sometimes omit the
(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}
-module structure of the source and write simply as
C^{\prime}:K_{n,X}\rightarrow K_{n,X}
.

Now we come to the construction of
K_{n,X,log}
(cf.
[
30
, §3]
). Define

(1.2.4)

K_{n,X,log}:=\operatorname{Cone}(K_{n,X}\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}-1}K_{n,X})[-1].

This is a complex of abelian sheaves sitting in degrees

[-d,1].

If
n=1
, we set
K_{X,log}:=K_{1,X,log}
.
Writing more explicitly,
K_{n,X,log}
is the following complex

(K_{n,X}^{-d}\oplus 0)\rightarrow(K_{n,X}^{-d+1}\oplus K_{n,X}^{-d})\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow(K_{n,X}^{0}\oplus K_{n,X}^{-1})\rightarrow(0\oplus K_{n,X}^{0}).

The differential of
K_{n,X,log}
at degree
i
is given by

\displaystyle d_{log}=d_{n,log}:K_{n,X,log}^{i}

\displaystyle\rightarrow K_{n,X,log}^{i+1}

\displaystyle(K_{n,X}^{i}\oplus K_{n,X}^{i-1})

\displaystyle\rightarrow(K_{n,X}^{i+1}\oplus K_{n,X}^{i})

\displaystyle(a,b)

\displaystyle\mapsto(d(a),-(C^{\prime}-1)(a)-d(b)),

where
d
is the differential in
K_{n,X}
.
The sign conventions we adopt here for shifted complexes and the cone construction are the same as in
[
9
, p6, p8]
. And naturally, one has a distinguished triangle

(1.2.5)

K_{n,X,log}\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X}\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}-1}K_{n,X}\xrightarrow{+1}K_{n,X,log}[1].

Explicitly, the first map is in degree
i
given by

\displaystyle K_{n,X,log}^{i}=K_{n,X}^{i}\oplus K_{n,X}^{i-1}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X}^{i},

\displaystyle(a,b)

\displaystyle\mapsto a.

The "
+1
" map is given by

\displaystyle K_{n,X}^{i}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{}(K_{n,X,log}[1])^{i}=K_{n,X,log}^{i+1}=(K_{n,X}^{i+1}\oplus K_{n,X}^{i}),

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle\mapsto(0,b).

Both maps are indeed maps of chain complexes.

1.3.
Comparison of
W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,log}
with
K_{n,X,log}

Recall the following result from the classical Grothendieck duality theory
[
21
, IV.3.4]
[
9
, 3.1.3]
and Ekedahl
[
10
, §1]
(see also
[
7
, proof of 1.10.3 and Rmk. 1.10.4]
).

Proposition 1.3

(Ekedahl)
.

If
X
is smooth and of pure dimension
d
over
k
, then there is a canonical quasi-isomorphism

W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}[d]\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X}.

Remark 1.4
.

Suppose
X
is a separated scheme of finite type over
k
of dimension
d
. Denote by
U
the smooth locus of
X
, and
suppose that the complement
Z
of
U
is of dimension
e
. Suppose moreover that
U
is non-empty and equidimensional (it is satisfied for example, if
X
is integral).
Then Ekedahl’s quasi-isomorphism
Proposition

1.3
gives a quasi-isomorphism of dualizing complexes

(1.3.1)

W_{n}\Omega_{U}^{d}[d]\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,U}.

Note that the associated filtrations of quasi-isomorphic dualizing complexes are the same (cf.
[
21
, V.3.4]
).
Let
Z^{\bullet}
be the codimension filtration of
U
.
As explained above, the associated filtration of
K_{n,U}
is the shifted codimension filtration,
i.e.,
Z^{\bullet}[d]
.
Apply the Cousin functor associated to the shifted codimension filtration
Z^{\bullet}[d]
to the quasi-isomorphism (
1.3.1
) between dualizing complexes, we have an isomorphism of residual complexes

E_{Z^{\bullet}[d]}(W_{n}\Omega_{U}^{d}[d])\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,U}

with the same filtration
Z^{\bullet}[d]
by (
1.1.1
).
Since
W_{n}j
is an open immersion, we can canonically identify the residual complexes
(W_{n}j)^{*}K_{n,X}\simeq K_{n,U}
by
Proposition

1.1
(2).
Since
K_{n,X}
is a residual complex and in particular is a Cousin complex (cf.
[
9
, p. 105]
), the adjunction map
K_{n,X}\rightarrow(W_{n}j)_{*}(W_{n}j)^{*}K_{n,X}\simeq(W_{n}j)_{*}K_{n,U}
is an isomorphism at degrees
[-d,-e-1]
. Thus the induced chain map

K_{n,X}\rightarrow(W_{n}j)_{*}E_{Z^{\bullet}[d]}(W_{n}\Omega_{U}^{d}[d])

is an isomorphism at degrees
[-d,-e-1]
.

1.3.1.
Compatibility of
C^{\prime}
with the classical Cartier operator
C

We review the absolute Cartier operator in the classical literature (see e.g.
[
5
, Chapter 1 §3]
,
[
25
, §0.2]
,
[
31
, 7.2]
,
[
26
, III §1]
).
Let
X
be a
k
-scheme. The
(absolute) inverse Cartier operator
\gamma_{X}
of degree
i
on a scheme
X
is affine locally, say, on
\operatorname{Spec}A\subset X
, given additively by the following expression (
\mathcal{H}^{i}(-)
denotes the cohomology sheaf of the complex)

(1.3.2)

\displaystyle\gamma_{A}:\quad\qquad\Omega_{A/k}^{i}\quad\quad

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{}\quad\quad\mathcal{H}^{i}(F_{A,*}\Omega_{A/k}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle ada_{1}\dots da_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto a^{p}a^{p-1}_{1}da_{1}\dots a_{i}^{p-1}da_{i},

where
a,a_{1},\dots a_{i}\in A
. Here
\mathcal{H}^{i}(F_{A,*}\Omega_{A/k}^{\bullet})
denotes the
A
-module structure on
\mathcal{H}^{i}(\Omega_{A/k}^{\bullet})
via the absolute Frobenius
F_{A}:A\rightarrow A,a\mapsto a^{p}
(note that
F_{A,*}\Omega_{A/k}^{\bullet}
is a complex of
A
-modules in positive characteristics). For each degree
i
,
\gamma_{A}
thus defined is an
A
-linear map. These local maps patch together and give rise to a map of sheaves

(1.3.3)

\gamma_{X}:\Omega_{X}^{i}\xrightarrow{}\mathcal{H}^{i}(F_{X,*}\Omega_{X}^{\bullet})

which is
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-linear. If
X
is smooth of dimension
d
,
\gamma_{X}
is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules, which is called the
(absolute) Cartier isomorphism
. See
[
5
, 1.3.4]
for a proof (note that although the authors there assumed the base field to be algebraically closed, the proof of this theorem works for any perfect field
k
of positive characteristics).

This can be generalized to the de Rham-Witt case.

Lemma 1.5

(cf.
[
29
, 4.1.3]
)
.

Denote by
W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{\prime i}
the abelian sheaf
F(W_{n+1}\Omega_{X}^{i})
regarded as a
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-submodule of
(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{i}
.
If
X
is smooth of dimension
d
, the map

\overline{F}:W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{i}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{\prime i}/dV^{n-1}\Omega_{X}^{i-1}

induced by Frobenius
F:W_{n+1}\Omega_{X}^{i}\rightarrow R_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{i}
is an isomorphism of
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules.

In particular, if
i=d
,

\overline{F}:W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}\rightarrow(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}/dV^{n-1}\Omega_{X}^{d-1}

is an isomorphism of
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules.

Proof.

Since

\operatorname{Ker}(R:W_{n+1}\Omega^{i}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega^{i})=V^{n}\Omega^{i}+dV^{n}\Omega^{i-1},

FV^{n}\Omega^{i}=0
and
FdV^{n}\Omega^{i-1}=dV^{n-1}\Omega^{i-1}
,
F:W_{n+1}\Omega^{i}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega^{i}
reduces to

\overline{F}:W_{n}\Omega^{i}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega^{i}/dV^{n-1}.

The surjectivity is clear. We show the injectivity. Suppose

x\in W_{n+1}\Omega^{i}
,
y\in\Omega^{i-1}
, such that
F(x)=dV^{n-1}y
. Then
F(x-dV^{n}y)=0
, which implies by
[
25
, I (3.21.1.2)]
that
x-dV^{n}y\in V^{n}\Omega^{i}
.

The second claim follows from the fact that
F:W_{n+1}\Omega^{d}\rightarrow R_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{d}
is surjective on top degree
d

[
25
, I (3.21.1.1)]
, and therefore
W_{n}\Omega^{\prime d}=(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{d}
as
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules.
∎

Definition 1.6

((absolute) Cartier operator)
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme of dimension
d
over
k
.

(1)

The composition

(1.3.4)

\displaystyle C:=C_{X}:

\displaystyle Z^{i}(F_{X,*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{i}(F_{X,*}\Omega_{X}^{\bullet})\xrightarrow{(\gamma_{X})^{-1}}\Omega_{X}^{i}

\displaystyle\big(\text{with }

\displaystyle Z^{i}(F_{X,*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X}):=\operatorname{Ker}(F_{X,*}\Omega^{i}_{X}\xrightarrow{d}F_{X,*}\Omega^{i+1}_{X})\big)

is called the
(absolute) Cartier operator
of degree
i
, denoted by
C
or
C_{X}
.

(2)

(cf.
[
29
, 4.1.2, 4.1.4]
)
More generally,
for
n\geq 1
, define the
(absolute) Cartier operator
C_{n}:=C_{n,X}
of level
n
to be the composite

(1.3.5)

C_{n}:W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{\prime i}\twoheadrightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{\prime i}/dV^{n-1}\Omega_{X}^{i-1}\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\overline{F}^{-1}}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{i},

where

\overline{F}:W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{i}\xrightarrow{\simeq}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{\prime i}/dV^{n-1}\Omega_{X}^{i-1}

is the map in
Lemma

1.5
.
If
i=d
is the top degree we obtain the
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-linear
map

(1.3.6)

C_{n}:(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}\twoheadrightarrow(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}/dV^{n-1}\Omega_{X}^{d-1}\xrightarrow{\overline{F}^{-1}}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}.

Remark 1.7
.

(1)

According to the explicit formula for
F
, we have
C=C_{1}

[
25
, I 3.3]
. For this reason we will simply write
C
for
C_{n}
sometimes.

(2)

C_{n}
(for all
n
) are compatible with étale pullbacks. Actually any de Rham-Witt system (e.g.
(W_{n}\Omega^{\bullet}_{X},F,V,R,\uline{p},d)
) is compatible with étale base change
[
7
, 1.3.2]
.

(3)

The
n
-th power of Frobenius
F
induces a map

\overline{F}^{n}:W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{i}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{H}^{i}((W_{n}F_{X})^{n}_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{\bullet}),

which is the same as
[
26
, III (1.4.1)]
.

(4)

Notice that on
\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k
,
C_{n}:W_{n}k\rightarrow W_{n}k
is simply the map
(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}
, because
F:W_{n+1}k\rightarrow W_{n}k
equals
R\circ W_{n+1}F_{k}
in characteristic
p
.

(5)

We sometimes omit "
(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}
" in the source. But one should always keep that in mind and be careful with the module structure.

Remark 1.8
.

Before we move on, we state a remark on étale schemes over
W_{n}X
.

(1)

Notice that every étale
W_{n}X
-scheme is of the form
W_{n}g:W_{n}U\rightarrow W_{n}X
, where
g:U\rightarrow X
is an étale
X
-scheme. In fact, there are two functors

\displaystyle F:\{\text{\'{e}tale $W_{n}X$-schemes}\}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrows\{\text{\'{e}tale $X$-schemes}\}:G

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\mapsto V\times_{W_{n}X}X

\displaystyle W_{n}U

\displaystyle\mapsfrom U

The functor
F
is a categorical equivalence according to
[
19
, Ch. IV, 18.1.2]
. The functor
G
is well-defined (i.e. produces étale
W_{n}X
-schemes) and is a right inverse of
F
by
[
23
, Thm. 1.25]
. We want to show that there is a natural isomorphism
GF\simeq id
, and this is the consequence of the following purely categorical statement: If
F:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}
and
G:\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
are two functors satisfying both
F
being a categorical equivalence and
FG\simeq id
, then
G
is a quasi-inverse of
F
, i.e., there exists a canonical natural isomorphism
GF\simeq id
. We leave this as an easy exercise for the reader.

(2)

The square

\textstyle{W_{n}U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}g}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}F_{U}}
\textstyle{W_{n}U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}g}
\textstyle{W_{n}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}F_{X}}
\textstyle{W_{n}X.}

is a cartesian square. This is because for any étale map
g:U\rightarrow X
, the relative Frobenius
F_{U/X}
is an isomorphism by
[
11
, 10.3.1]
. Thus
W_{n}F_{U/X}
is an also isomorphism and the claim follows.

We shall now state the main result in this subsection, which seems to be known by experts (cf. proof of
[
30
, 3.4]
) but we cannot find a proof in the literature.
To eliminate possible sign inconsistency of the Cartier operator with the Grothendieck trace map calculated via residue symbols
[
9
, Appendix A]
, we give a proof by explicit calculations (see
Section

1.3.2
-
Section

1.3.3
).
At the same time, this result justifies our notation for
C^{\prime}
: The classical Cartier operator
C
is simply the
(-d)
-th cohomology of our
C^{\prime}
in the smooth case.

Theorem 1.9

(Compatibility of
C^{\prime}
with
C
)
.

Suppose that
X
is a smooth scheme of dimension
d
over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
. Then the top degree classical Cartier operator

C:(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X/k}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X/k}

as defined in
Definition

1.6
, agrees with the
(-d)
-th cohomology of the Cartier operator for residual complexes

C^{\prime}:(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X/k}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X/k}

as defined in (
1.2.3
) via Ekedahl’s quasi-isomorphism
Proposition

1.3
.

Proof.

The Cartier operator is stable under étale base change, i.e., for any étale morphism
W_{n}g:W_{n}X\rightarrow W_{n}Y
(which must be of this form according to
Remark

1.8
(1)), we have

C_{X}\simeq(W_{n}g)^{*}C_{Y}:(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}.

We claim that the map
C^{\prime}
defined in (
1.2.3
) is also compatible with étale base change. That is, whenever we have an étale morphism
W_{n}g:W_{n}X\rightarrow W_{n}Y
, there is a canonical isomorphism

C^{\prime}_{X}\simeq(W_{n}g)^{*}C^{\prime}_{Y}:(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}K_{n,X}\rightarrow K_{n,X}.

First of all, the Grothendieck trace map
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}F_{X}}
for residual complexes is compatible with étale base change by
Proposition

1.2
(5), i.e.,

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}F_{X}}\simeq g^{*}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}F_{Y}}:(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\rightarrow K_{n,X}.

Secondly, because of the cartesian square in
Remark

1.8
(2) and the flat base change theorem

(W_{n}g)^{*}(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}\simeq(W_{n}F_{X})^{*}(W_{n}g)_{*},

we are reduced to show that (
1.2.2
) is compatible with étale base change. And this is true, because we have

(W_{n}g)^{*}\simeq(W_{n}g)^{\triangle}

by
Proposition

1.1
(2), and the compatibility of
(-)^{\triangle}
with composition by
Proposition

1.1
(4). This finishes the claim.

Note that the question is local on
W_{n}X
.
Thus to prove the statement for smooth
k
-schemes
X
, using the compatibility of
C
and
C^{\prime}
with respect to étale base change, it suffices to prove it for
X=\mathbf{A}^{d}_{k}
. That is, we need to check that the expression given in
Lemma

1.14
for
C^{\prime}
agrees with the expression for
C
given in
Lemma

1.10
. This is apparent.
∎

1.3.2.
Proof of
Theorem

1.9
:
C
for the top Witt differentials on the affine space

Let
k
be a perfect field of positive characteristic
p
. The aim of this subsection is to provide the formula for the Cartier operator on the top degree de Rham-Witt sheaf over the affine space (
Lemma

1.10
).

Consider the polynomial ring
k[X_{1},...,X_{d}]
.
Let
h:\{1,...,d\}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}[\frac{1}{p}]\setminus\{0\}
be a function such that
\mathrm{Im}(p^{n-1}h)\subset\mathbb{N}
.
Write
h_{i}:=h(i)
.
Let
\{i_{1},...,i_{d}\}
be a reordering of
\{1,...,d\}
, such that

v_{p}(h_{i_{1}})\leq v_{p}(h_{i_{2}})\leq...\leq v_{p}(h_{i_{d}}).

This order depends on
h
.
Since
\{1,...,d\}
is a finite set, we can also choose a uniform order for elements in
{\operatorname{Supp}\,}h
and
{\operatorname{Supp}\,}p^{a}h
for any integer
a
and any function
h
.
If
\mathrm{Im}(h)\not\subset\mathbb{N}
, let
r\in[1,d]
be the unique integer such that

v_{p}(h_{i_{1}})\leq...\leq v_{p}(h_{i_{r}})<0\leq v_{p}(h_{i_{r+1}})\leq...\leq v_{p}(h_{i_{d}}).

For all
j\in[1,d]
, write

v_{j}:=v_{p}(h_{i_{j}}),\quad h_{j}^{\prime}:=h_{i_{j}}p^{-v_{j}}.

According to
[
34
, 2.17]
, any element in
W_{n}\Omega_{k[X_{1},...,X_{d}]}^{d}
is uniquely written as a sum of (
1.3.7
) and (
1.3.8
):

•

h
is a function such that
\mathrm{Im}(h)\not\subset\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in W_{n+v_{1}}k
,

(1.3.7)

\displaystyle dV^{-v_{1}}(\alpha[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}})\cdots dV^{-v_{r}}([X_{r}]^{h_{r}^{\prime}})\cdot F^{v_{r+1}}d[X_{r+1}]^{h_{r+1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{v_{d}}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}},

and

•

h
is a function such that
\mathrm{Im}(h)\subset\mathbb{N}
,
\beta\in W_{n}k
,

(1.3.8)

\displaystyle\beta F^{v_{1}}d[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{v_{d}}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}.

Lemma 1.10

(
C_{n}
on
\mathbf{A}^{d}
)
.

The Cartier operator (cf.
Definition

1.6
)

C:=C_{n}:W_{n}\Omega_{k[X_{1},...,X_{d}]}^{d}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{k[X_{1},...,X_{d}]}^{d}

is the map uniquely determined by the following assignment by taking products:
for
j\in[1,d]
,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\\
dV^{-v_{j}}(\alpha[X_{j}]_{n+v_{j}}^{h_{j}^{\prime}})\\
(\alpha\in W_{n+v_{j}}k,v_{j}<0)\end{array}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}dV^{1-v_{j}}(R(\alpha)[X_{j}]_{n-1+v_{j}}^{h_{j}^{\prime}}),&\hbox{$-v_{j}\in[1,n-2]$;}\\
0,&\hbox{$-v_{j}=n-1$.}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\beta\\
(\beta\in W_{n}k)\end{array}

\displaystyle\mapsto(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\beta),

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{c}\\
F^{v_{j}}d[X_{j}]_{n+v_{j}}^{h_{j}^{\prime}}\\
(v_{j}\geq 0)\end{array}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}F^{v_{j}-1}d[X_{j}]_{n+v_{j}-1}^{h_{j}^{\prime}},&\hbox{$v_{j}\geq 1$;}\\
dV[X_{j}]_{n-1}^{h_{i_{j}}},&\hbox{$v_{j}=0$.}\end{array}\right.

Proof.

For any
\gamma,\delta\in W_{n}\Omega_{k[X_{1},...,X_{d}]}^{\bullet}
,
C(F(\gamma)\cdot F(\delta))=C(F(\gamma))\cdot C(F(\delta)).

Hence it suffices to check the formulae on each factor.
For
\alpha\in W_{n+v_{j}}k,-v_{j}\in[1,n-2]
, the formulae
CF=R
,
d=FdV
imply

\displaystyle C\Big(dV^{-v_{j}}(\alpha[X_{j}]_{n+v_{j}}^{h_{j}^{\prime}})\Big)

\displaystyle=C\Big(FdV^{1-v_{j}}(\alpha[X_{j}]_{n+v_{j}}^{h_{j}^{\prime}})\Big)

\displaystyle=dV^{1-v_{j}}(R(\alpha)[X_{j}]_{n-1+v_{j}}^{h_{j}^{\prime}}).

For
\alpha\in k,-v_{j}=n-1
, the formulae
CF=R
,
d=FdV
and
\operatorname{Ker}R=V^{n}+dV^{n}
imply

\displaystyle C\Big(dV^{-v_{j}}(\alpha[X_{j}]_{n+v_{j}}^{h_{j}^{\prime}})\Big)

\displaystyle=C\Big(FdV^{n}(\alpha X_{j}^{h_{i_{j}}})\Big)

\displaystyle=R\Big(dV^{n}(\alpha X_{j}^{h_{i_{j}}})\Big)

\displaystyle=0.

For
\beta\in W_{n}k
,

\displaystyle C(\beta)=(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\beta).

For
v_{j}\geq 1
, the formula
CF=R
implies

\displaystyle C\Big(F^{v_{j}}d[X_{j}]_{n+v_{j}}^{h_{j}^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle=R\Big(F^{v_{j}-1}d[X_{j}]_{n+v_{j}}^{h_{j}^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle=F^{v_{j}-1}d[X_{j}]_{n+v_{j}-1}^{h_{j}^{\prime}}

For
v_{j}=0
, the formulae
CF=R
and
d=FdV
imply

\displaystyle C\Big(F^{v_{j}}d[X_{j}]_{n+v_{j}}^{h_{j}^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle=C\Big(FdV[X_{j}]_{n}^{h_{i_{j}}}\Big)

\displaystyle=R\Big(dV[X_{j}]_{n}^{h_{i_{j}}}\Big)

\displaystyle=dV[X_{j}]_{n-1}^{h_{i_{j}}}

∎

1.3.3.
Proof of
Theorem

1.9
:
C^{\prime}
for the top Witt differentials on the affine space

The aim of
Section

1.3.3
is to calculate
C^{\prime}
for the top de Rham-Witt sheaves on the affine space (
Lemma

1.14
). To do this, one needs to first calculate the trace map of the canonical lift of the absolute Frobenius.

1.3.3.1
Trace map of the canonical lift
\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}
of absolute Frobenius
F_{X}

Before we start with the computation we recall some properties of the residue symbol from
[
9
, §A]
. Let
X\hookrightarrow P
be a closed immersion of affine schemes with the sheaf of ideals generated by
t_{1},...,t_{d}\in\Gamma(P,\mathcal{O}_{P})
. Let
P\rightarrow Y
be a separated smooth morphism of affine schemes with pure relative dimension
d
.
Suppose
X\rightarrow Y
is finite flat.
For any
\omega\in\Gamma(P,\omega_{P/Y})
, there is a well-defined element

\operatorname{Res}_{P/Y}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\omega\\
t_{1},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]\in\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})

which is called the residue symbol (cf.
[
9
, (A.1.4)]
). It satisfies the following properties (we use the same numbering as in
[
9
, §A.1]
):

•

Suppose
h:Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
is any morphism of schemes, and
P^{\prime}=P\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
. Then

(R5)

\operatorname{Res}_{P^{\prime}/Y^{\prime}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}h^{*}\omega\\
h^{*}t_{1},\dots,h^{*}t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]=h^{*}\operatorname{Res}_{P/Y}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\omega\\
t_{1},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]

•

For any
\varphi\in\Gamma(P,\mathcal{O}_{P})
,

(R6)

\operatorname{Res}_{P/Y}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\varphi\cdot dt_{1}\dots dt_{d}\\
t_{1},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]=\operatorname{Tr}_{X/Y}(\varphi|_{X}).

Here the notation
\operatorname{Tr}_{{X}/{Y}}
denotes the classical trace map associated to the finite locally free ring extension
\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y})\rightarrow\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
.

•

For
\eta\in\Gamma(P,\Omega_{P/Y}^{n-1})
, and
k_{1},\dots,k_{d}
positive integers,

(R9)

\operatorname{Res}_{P/Y}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}d\eta\\
t_{1}^{k_{1}},\dots,t_{d}^{k_{d}}\\
\end{array}\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}\cdot\operatorname{Res}_{P/Y}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}dt_{i}\wedge\eta\\
t_{1}^{k_{1}},\dots,t_{i}^{k_{i}+1},\dots,t_{d}^{k_{d}}\\
\end{array}\right].

Let
k
be a perfect field of positive characteristic
p
. Let
X=\mathbf{A}^{d}_{k}
, and denote by
\widetilde{X}:=\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}(k)[X_{1},\dots,X_{d}]
the canonical smooth lift of
X
over
W_{n}(k)
.
To make the module structures in the following discussion explicit, we distinguish the source and the target of the absolute Frobenius of
\operatorname{Spec}k
and write it as

F_{k}:\operatorname{Spec}k_{1}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}k_{2}.

Similarly, write the absolute Frobenius on
X
as

F_{X}:X=\operatorname{Spec}k_{1}[X_{1},\dots,X_{d}]\rightarrow Y=\operatorname{Spec}k_{2}[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{d}].

There is a canonical lift of
F_{X}
over
\widetilde{X}

\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}:\widetilde{X}=\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}(k_{1})[X_{1},\dots,X_{d}]\rightarrow\widetilde{Y}:=\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}(k_{2})[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{d}].

given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}^{*}:\quad\Gamma(\widetilde{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{Y}})=W_{n}(k_{2})[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{d}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow W_{n}(k_{1})[X_{1},\dots,X_{d}]=\Gamma(\widetilde{X},\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}}),

\displaystyle W_{n}k_{2}\ni\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto W_{n}(F_{k})(\alpha),

\displaystyle Y_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto X_{i}^{p}.

Let

\pi_{X}:X\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}k_{1},\quad\pi_{Y}:Y\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}k_{2},\quad\pi_{\widetilde{X}}:\widetilde{X}\rightarrow W_{n}k_{1},\quad\pi_{\widetilde{Y}}:\widetilde{Y}\rightarrow W_{n}k_{2}

be the structure maps.
The composition
\pi_{\widetilde{Y}}\circ\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}:\widetilde{X}\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k_{2}
gives
\widetilde{X}
a
W_{n}k_{2}
-scheme structure, and the map
\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}
is then a map of
W_{n}k_{2}
-schemes. Therefore the trace map

\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}}:\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X},*}\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\triangle}K_{\widetilde{Y}}\rightarrow K_{\widetilde{Y}}

makes sense. Consider the following map of complexes

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X},*}K_{\widetilde{X}}\simeq\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X},*}\pi_{\widetilde{X}}^{\triangle}W_{n}k_{1}\xrightarrow[\sim]{\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X},*}\pi_{\widetilde{X}}^{\triangle}(\ref{(1.0.1)})}

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X},*}\pi_{\widetilde{X}}^{\triangle}W_{n}F_{k}^{\triangle}W_{n}k_{2}\simeq

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X},*}\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\triangle}\pi_{\widetilde{Y}}^{\triangle}W_{n}k_{2}\simeq\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X},*}\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}^{\triangle}K_{\widetilde{Y}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}}}K_{\widetilde{Y}}.

Taking the
(-d)
-th cohomology, it induces a map

(1.3.9)

\widetilde{F}_{X,*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k_{1}}^{d}\rightarrow\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k_{2}}^{d}

Lemma 1.11
.

The notations are the same as above. The map (
1.3.9
) has the following expression:

(1.3.10)

\displaystyle\quad\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k_{1}}^{d}\quad

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{(\ref{eq:trace of lift Frob on Coh})}\quad\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k_{2}}^{d}

\displaystyle\alpha\bm{X}^{\bm{\lambda}+p\bm{\mu}}d\bm{X}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\alpha)\bm{Y}^{\bm{\mu}}d\bm{Y},&\hbox{if $\lambda_{i}=p-1$ for all $i$;}\\
0,&\hbox{if $\lambda_{i}\neq p-1$ for some $i$.}\end{array}\right.

Proof.

Consider the closed immersion
i:\widetilde{X}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{P}=\mathbf{A}^{d}_{\widetilde{Y}}
associated to the following homomorphism of rings:

\displaystyle\Gamma(\widetilde{P},\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{P}})=W_{n}(k_{2})[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{d},T_{1},\dots,T_{d}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow W_{n}(k_{1})[X_{1},\dots,X_{d}]=\Gamma(\widetilde{X},\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}}),

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto(W_{n}F_{k})(\alpha),\quad\alpha\in W_{n}(k_{2}),

\displaystyle Y_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto X_{i}^{p},\quad i=1,\dots,d,

\displaystyle T_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto X_{i},\quad i=1,\dots,d.

Its kernel is

I=(T_{1}^{p}-Y_{1},\dots,T_{d}^{p}-Y_{d}).

Denote

t_{i}=T_{i}^{p}-Y_{i},\quad i=1,\dots,d.

Obviously the
t_{i}
’s form a regular sequence in
\Gamma(\widetilde{P},\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{P}})
, and hence
i
is a regular immersion. Then one has a factorization of
\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}
:

(1.3.13)

\textstyle{{\widetilde{X}=\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}(k_{1})[X_{1},\dots,X_{d}]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{P}=\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}(k_{2})[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{d},T_{1},\dots,T_{d}]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{{\widetilde{Y}=\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}(k_{2})[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{d}]}.}

Regarding
\widetilde{X}
as a
W_{n}k_{2}
-scheme via the composite map
\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}\circ\pi_{\widetilde{Y}}
, the diagram (
1.3.13
) is then a diagram in the category of
W_{n}k_{2}
-schemes.

A general element in
\Gamma(\widetilde{X},\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k_{1}}^{d})
is a sum of expressions of the form

(1.3.14)

\alpha\bm{X}^{\bm{\lambda}+p\bm{\mu}}d\bm{X},\quad\alpha\in W_{n}k_{1},\bm{\lambda}\in[0,p-1]^{d},\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}.

Here
\bm{\lambda}=\{\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d}\}
,
\bm{\mu}=\{\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{d}\}
are multi-indices, and
\bm{X}^{\bm{\lambda}}:=X_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots X_{d}^{\lambda_{d}}
(similar for
\bm{Y}^{\bm{\mu}}
,
\bm{X}^{\bm{\lambda}+p\bm{\mu}}
, etc.),
d\bm{X}:=dX_{1}\dots dX_{d}
(similar for
d\bm{T}
, etc.).
The element (
1.3.14
) in
\Gamma(\widetilde{X},\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k_{1}}^{d})
corresponds to

(1.3.15)

(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\alpha)\bm{X}^{\bm{\lambda}+p\bm{\mu}}d\bm{X},\quad\alpha\in W_{n}k_{2},\bm{\lambda}\in[0,p-1]^{d},\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{N}^{d},

in
\Gamma(\widetilde{X},\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k_{2}}^{d})
under
(-d)
-th cohomology of the map
\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X},*}\pi_{\widetilde{X}}^{\triangle}(\ref{(1.0.1)})
, and

(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\alpha)\bm{T}^{\bm{\lambda}}\bm{Y}^{\bm{\mu}}d\bm{T},\quad\alpha\in W_{n}k_{2},\bm{\lambda}\in[0,p-1]^{d},\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}

is a lift of (
1.3.15
) to
\Gamma(\widetilde{P},\Omega_{\widetilde{P}/W_{n}k_{2}}^{d})
.
Write

\displaystyle\beta:

\displaystyle=dt_{d}\wedge\dots\wedge dt_{1}\wedge(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\alpha)\bm{T}^{\bm{\lambda}}\bm{Y}^{\bm{\mu}}d\bm{T}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{d}dY_{d}\wedge\dots\wedge dY_{1}\wedge(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\alpha)\bm{T}^{\bm{\lambda}}\bm{Y}^{\bm{\mu}}d\bm{T}

in
\Gamma(\widetilde{P},\omega_{\widetilde{P}/W_{n}k_{2}})
, where
\omega_{\widetilde{P}/W_{n}k_{2}}
denotes the dualizing sheaf with respect to the smooth morphism
\widetilde{P}\rightarrow W_{n}k_{2}
.
Recall that there is a natural isomorphism (
[
9
, p.30 (a)]
)

\displaystyle\omega_{\widetilde{P}/W_{n}k_{2}}

\displaystyle\simeq\omega_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{P}}}\pi^{*}\omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k_{2}},

where
\omega_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}
and
\omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k_{2}}
denote the dualizing sheaves with respect to the smooth morphisms
\pi:\widetilde{P}\rightarrow\widetilde{Y}
and
\widetilde{Y}\rightarrow W_{n}k_{2}
.
This isomorphism maps

\beta
to

(-1)^{\frac{d(3d+1)}{2}}(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\alpha)\bm{T}^{\bm{\lambda}}d\bm{T}\otimes\pi^{*}\bm{Y}^{\bm{\mu}}d\bm{Y}.

It is easily seen that
\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}
is a finite flat morphism between smooth
W_{n}k_{2}
-schemes. Applying
[
6
, Lemma A.3.3]
, one has

\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}}((W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\alpha)\bm{X}^{\bm{\lambda}+p\bm{\mu}}d\bm{X})=(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\alpha)\operatorname{Res}_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\bm{T}^{\bm{\lambda}}d\bm{T}\\
t_{1},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]\bm{Y}^{\bm{\mu}}d\bm{Y},

where
\operatorname{Res}_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\bm{T}^{\bm{\lambda}}d\bm{T}\\
t_{1},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]\in\Gamma(\widetilde{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{Y}})

is the residue symbol defined in
[
9
, (A.1.4)]
, and
\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}}
is the trace map on top differentials of the
W_{n}k_{2}
-morphism
\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}

[
9
, (2.7.36)]
.

We consider the following cases:

•

If
(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d})\neq(p-1,\dots,p-1)
,
\bm{T}^{\bm{\lambda}}d\bm{T}=d\eta
for some
\eta\in\Omega_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}^{d-1}
. Suppose without loss of generality that
\lambda_{1}\neq p-1
. Then we can take

\eta=\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}+1}T_{1}^{\lambda_{1}+1}T_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\dots T_{d}^{\lambda_{d}}dT_{2}\dots dT_{d}.

Noticing that

dt_{i}=d(T_{i}^{p}-Y_{i})=pT_{i}^{p-1}dT_{i}

in
\Omega^{1}_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}
, and that
\lambda_{1}+mp+1
(
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
) is not divisible by
p
if
\lambda_{1}+1
is so. Now we calculate

\displaystyle\operatorname{Res}_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\bm{T}^{\bm{\lambda}}d\bm{T}\\
t_{1},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}+1}\operatorname{Res}_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}d(T_{1}^{\lambda_{1}+1}T_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\dots T_{d}^{\lambda_{d}}dT_{2}\dots dT_{d})\\
t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{\lambda_{1}+1}\operatorname{Res}_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}T_{1}^{\lambda_{1}+p}T_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\dots T_{d}^{\lambda_{d}}dT_{1}dT_{2}\dots dT_{d}\\
t_{1}^{2},t_{2},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{p}{(\lambda_{1}+1)(\lambda_{1}+p+1)}\operatorname{Res}_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}d(T_{1}^{\lambda_{1}+p+1}T_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\dots T_{d}^{\lambda_{d}}dT_{2}\dots dT_{d})\\
t_{1}^{2},t_{2},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2p^{2}}{(\lambda_{1}+1)(\lambda_{1}+p+1)}\operatorname{Res}_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}T_{1}^{\lambda_{1}+2p}T_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\dots T_{d}^{\lambda_{d}}dT_{1}dT_{2}\dots dT_{d}\\
t_{1}^{3},t_{2},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2p^{2}}{\prod_{i=0}^{2}(\lambda_{1}+ip+1)}\operatorname{Res}_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}d(T_{1}^{\lambda_{1}+2p+1}T_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\dots T_{d}^{\lambda_{d}}dT_{2}\dots dT_{d})\\
t_{1}^{3},t_{2},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{6p^{3}}{\prod_{i=0}^{2}(\lambda_{1}+ip+1)}\operatorname{Res}_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}T_{1}^{\lambda_{1}+3p}T_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\dots T_{d}^{\lambda_{d}}dT_{1}dT_{2}\dots dT_{d}\\
t_{1}^{4},t_{2},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=\dots

\displaystyle=\frac{(\prod_{i=1}^{n}i)\cdot p^{n}}{\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(\lambda_{1}+ip+1)}\operatorname{Res}_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}T_{1}^{\lambda_{1}+np}T_{2}^{\lambda_{2}}\dots T_{d}^{\lambda_{d}}dT_{1}dT_{2}\dots dT_{d}\\
t_{1}^{n+1},t_{2},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=0.

We have used (R9) on the second, the fourth, the sixth, and the eighth equality signs.
The last equality is because
p^{n}=0
in
\Gamma(\widetilde{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{Y}})
.

•

If
(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{d})=(p-1,\dots,p-1)
,
consider

(1.3.16)

\textstyle{X^{\prime}:=\operatorname{Spec}\frac{\mathbb{Z}[Y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,Y^{\prime}_{d},T^{\prime}_{1},\dots,T^{\prime}_{d}]}{({T^{\prime}_{1}}^{p}-Y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{T^{\prime}_{d}}^{p}-Y^{\prime}_{d})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[Y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,Y^{\prime}_{d},T^{\prime}_{1},\dots,T^{\prime}_{d}]}=:P^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{Z}[Y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,Y^{\prime}_{d}]}=:Y^{\prime}.}

The map
f
is given by
f^{*}(Y^{\prime}_{i})=Y^{\prime}_{i}={T^{\prime}_{i}}^{p}
in
\Gamma(X^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}})
. This is a finite locally free morphism of rank
p^{d}
.
Consider the map
h:\widetilde{Y}\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
given by

\displaystyle\Gamma(Y^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{Y^{\prime}})=\mathbb{Z}[Y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,Y^{\prime}_{d}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow W_{n}(k_{2})[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{d}]=\Gamma(\widetilde{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{Y}}),

\displaystyle Y^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle\mapsto Y_{i}\text{ for all $i$,}

that relates the two diagrams (
1.3.16
) and (
1.3.13
).
In
\Gamma(Y^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{Y^{\prime}})
, we have

\displaystyle p^{d}\cdot\operatorname{Res}_{P^{\prime}/Y^{\prime}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}{T^{\prime}_{1}}^{p-1}\dots{T^{\prime}_{d}}^{p-1}dT^{\prime}_{1}\dots dT^{\prime}_{d}\\
{T^{\prime}_{1}}^{p}-Y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{T^{\prime}_{d}}^{p}-Y^{\prime}_{d}\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Res}_{P^{\prime}/Y^{\prime}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}d({T^{\prime}_{1}}^{p}-Y^{\prime}_{1})\dots d({T^{\prime}_{d}}^{p}-Y^{\prime}_{d})\\
{T^{\prime}_{1}}^{p}-Y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{T^{\prime}_{d}}^{p}-Y^{\prime}_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(R6)}}}{{=}}\operatorname{Tr}_{{X^{\prime}}/{Y^{\prime}}}(1)

\displaystyle=p^{d}.

The notation
\operatorname{Tr}_{{X^{\prime}}/{Y^{\prime}}}
denotes the classical trace map associated to the finite locally free ring extension
\Gamma(Y^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{Y^{\prime}})\rightarrow\Gamma(X^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}})
.
As for the last equality,
\operatorname{Tr}_{{X^{\prime}}/{Y^{\prime}}}(1)=p^{d}
because
f
is a finite locally free map of rank
p^{d}
. Since
p^{d}
is a non-zerodivisor in
\Gamma(Y^{\prime},\mathcal{O}_{Y^{\prime}})
, one deduces

\operatorname{Res}_{P^{\prime}/Y^{\prime}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}{T^{\prime}_{1}}^{p-1}\dots{T^{\prime}_{d}}^{p-1}dT^{\prime}_{1}\dots dT^{\prime}_{d}\\
{T^{\prime}_{1}}^{p}-Y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{T^{\prime}_{d}}^{p}-Y^{\prime}_{d}\end{array}\right]=1.

Set

\bm{T}^{\bm{p-1}}=T_{1}^{p-1}\dots T_{d}^{p-1},

which is the canonical lift of
\bm{X}^{\bm{\lambda}}
via the map
i:\widetilde{X}\hookrightarrow\widetilde{P}
in our current case.
Pulling back to
\Gamma(\widetilde{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{Y}})
via
h
, one has

\displaystyle\operatorname{Res}_{\widetilde{P}/\widetilde{Y}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\bm{T}^{\bm{p-1}}d\bm{T}\\
t_{1},\dots,t_{d}\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(R5)}}}{{=}}h^{*}\operatorname{Res}_{P^{\prime}/Y^{\prime}}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}{T^{\prime}_{1}}^{p-1}\dots{T^{\prime}_{d}}^{p-1}dT^{\prime}_{1}\dots dT^{\prime}_{d}\\
{T^{\prime}_{1}}^{p}-Y^{\prime}_{1},\dots,{T^{\prime}_{d}}^{p}-Y^{\prime}_{d}\end{array}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}1.

Altogether, we know that the map (
1.3.9
) takes the following expression

\displaystyle\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k_{1}}^{d}\quad

\displaystyle\rightarrow\quad\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k_{2}}^{d}

\displaystyle\alpha\bm{X}^{\bm{\lambda}+p\bm{\mu}}d\bm{X}

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\alpha)\bm{Y}^{\bm{\mu}}d\bm{Y},&\hbox{if $\lambda_{i}=p-1$ for all $i$;}\\
0,&\hbox{if $\lambda_{i}\neq p-1$ for some $i$.}\end{array}\right.

∎

1.3.3.2
C^{\prime}
for top Witt differentials

Now we turn to the
W_{n}
-version. The aim of this subsection is to calculate
C^{\prime}
for top Witt differentials on
\mathbf{A}^{d}_{k}
(
Lemma

1.14
).

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a finite
morphism between smooth, separated and equidimensional
k
-schemes of dimension
d
. Same as before, we denote by
\pi_{X}:X\rightarrow k
and
\pi_{Y}:Y\rightarrow k
the respective structure maps.
The complexes
K_{n,X}:=(W_{n}\pi_{X})^{\triangle}W_{n}k,K_{n,Y}=(W_{n}\pi_{Y})^{\triangle}W_{n}k
are residual complexes on
X
and
Y
. Then we define the trace map

(1.3.21)

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}:(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X})\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{Y}

to be the
(-d)
-th cohomology map of the composition

(1.3.22)

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}:(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X}\simeq{\mathcal{H}om}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{Y}}((W_{n}f)_{*}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X},K_{n,Y})\xrightarrow{\text{ev. at 1}}K_{n,Y}

via Ekedahl’s isomorphism
W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}\simeq\mathcal{H}^{-d}(K_{n,X})
in

Proposition

1.3
.

It suffices to compute the trace map locally on
Y
. Thus by possibly shrinking
Y
we can assume that
Y
and (therefore also
X
) is affine.
In this case, there exist smooth affine
W_{n}k
-schemes
\widetilde{X}
and
\widetilde{Y}
which lift
X
and
Y
. Denote the structure morphisms of
\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}
by
\pi_{\widetilde{X}}
and
\pi_{\widetilde{Y}}
, respectively. Then there exists a finite
W_{n}k
-morphism

\widetilde{f}:\widetilde{X}\rightarrow\widetilde{Y}

lifting
f:X\rightarrow Y
by the formal smoothness property of
\widetilde{Y}
.

Consider the map of abelian sheaves
[
10
, I (2.3)]

(1.3.23)

\displaystyle\varrho_{Y}^{*}:\quad W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{Y}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\vartheta_{Y}}\mathcal{H}^{0}(\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{Y}},\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}V^{i}([a_{i}])

\displaystyle\mapsto\widetilde{a}_{0}^{p^{n}}+p\widetilde{a}_{1}^{p^{n-1}}+\dots+p^{n-1}\widetilde{a}_{n-1}^{p},

where
a_{i}\in\mathcal{O}_{Y}
, and
\widetilde{a}_{i}\in\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{Y}}
are arbitrary liftings of
a_{i}
.
The map
\vartheta_{Y}
appearing above is the
i=0
case of the canonical isomorphism defined in
[
26
, III. 1.5]

(1.3.24)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{Y}:W_{n}\Omega_{Y}^{i}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{H}^{i}(\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet}).

Note that the map
\varrho_{Y}^{*}:W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{Y}}
is a morphism of sheaves of rings, and it induces a finite morphism
\varrho_{Y}:W_{n}Y\rightarrow\widetilde{Y}
(cf.
[
10
, I, paragraph after (2.4)]
).
Altogether we have the following commutative diagram of schemes (cf.
[
10
, I. (2.4)]
)

\textstyle{\widetilde{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{X}}
\textstyle{W_{n}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}f}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{i}_{X}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{i_{X}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\widetilde{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\varrho_{Y}}
\textstyle{W_{n}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}\pi_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{i}_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{i_{Y}}
\textstyle{W_{n}k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}(F_{k}^{n})}
\textstyle{W_{n}k.}
\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{F_{k}^{n}}
\textstyle{k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Lemma 1.12
.

Set
K_{\widetilde{X}}=\pi_{\widetilde{X}}^{\triangle}W_{n}k
, and
K_{\widetilde{Y}}=\pi_{\widetilde{Y}}^{\triangle}W_{n}k
.
The
(-d)
-th cohomology of the map
\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{f}}:\widetilde{f}_{*}K_{\widetilde{X}}\rightarrow K_{\widetilde{Y}}
gives a map
\widetilde{f}_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k}^{d}\rightarrow\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{d}
, which we again denote by
\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{f}}
.
Then by passing to quotients, this map
\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{f}}
induces a well-defined map

\tau_{\widetilde{f}}:\mathcal{H}^{d}(\widetilde{f}_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{d}(\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet}).

Moreover, the map
\tau_{\widetilde{f}}
is compatible with
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}
defined in (
1.3.22
), i.e., the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}\vartheta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{Y}^{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(\varrho_{Y}\widetilde{f})_{*}\mathcal{H}^{d}(\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\tau_{\widetilde{f}}}
\textstyle{(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\mathcal{H}^{d}(\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet})}

Proof.

We argue the other way around, namely we define the map
\tau_{\widetilde{f}}:\mathcal{H}^{d}(\widetilde{f}_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{d}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet})
via

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}:(W_{n}f)_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{Y}^{d}
,
and then show that this is the reduction of
\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{f}}:\widetilde{f}_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k}^{d}\rightarrow\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{d}
.

First of all, via isomorphisms
\vartheta_{X}
,
\vartheta_{Y}
, the map
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}:(W_{n}f)_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{Y}^{d}
defined in (
1.3.22
) induces a well-defined map
\tau_{\widetilde{f}}:\mathcal{H}^{d}(\widetilde{f}_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{d}(\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet})
.
To show the compatibility with
\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{f}}
, one needs the observation of Ekedahl
that the composite map

\displaystyle t_{Y}:(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{d}[d]\xrightarrow{\simeq}(\varrho_{Y})_{*}K_{\widetilde{Y}}

\displaystyle\simeq(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\pi_{\widetilde{Y}}^{\triangle}W_{n}k\xrightarrow[\simeq]{(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\pi_{\widetilde{Y}}^{\triangle}(\ref{(1.0.1)})^{n}}

\displaystyle(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\pi_{\widetilde{Y}}^{\triangle}(W_{n}F_{k}^{n})^{\triangle}W_{n}k\simeq(\varrho_{Y})_{*}(\varrho_{Y})^{\triangle}(W_{n}\pi_{Y})^{\triangle}W_{n}k\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{\varrho_{Y}}}K_{n,Y}

factors through
\overline{t}_{Y}:(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\mathcal{H}^{d}(\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet})[d]\rightarrow K_{n,Y}
(cf.
[
10
, §1 (2.6)]
). Then he defined the map
W_{n}\Omega_{Y}^{d}[d]\rightarrow K_{n,Y}
to be the composite

(1.3.25)

s_{Y}:W_{n}\Omega_{Y}^{d}[d]\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\vartheta_{Y}}\mathcal{H}^{d}(\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet})[d]\xrightarrow{\overline{t}_{Y}}K_{n,Y}.

Now consider the following diagram of complexes of sheaves

\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}[d]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}s_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}\vartheta_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{Y}^{d}[d]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(\varrho_{Y}\widetilde{f})_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k}^{d}[d]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}t_{X}}
\scriptstyle{(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{f}}}
\textstyle{(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{d}[d]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{t_{Y}}
\textstyle{(\varrho_{Y}\widetilde{f})_{*}\mathcal{H}^{d}(\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet})[d]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\tau_{\widetilde{f}}}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}\overline{t}_{X}}
\textstyle{(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\mathcal{H}^{d}(\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet})[d]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{t}_{Y}}

The unlabeled arrows are given by the natural quotient maps.
The front commutes by the definition of
\tau_{\widetilde{f}}
. The top commutes by the definition of
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}:(W_{n}f)_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{Y}^{d}
. The triangles in the right (resp. the left) side commute due to the definition of
\overline{t}_{Y}
and
s_{Y}
(resp.
\overline{t}_{X}
and
s_{X}
).
The back square commutes, because the trace map
\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{f}}
is functorial with respect to maps of residual complexes with the same associated filtration by
Proposition

1.2
(3).
We want to show that the bottom square commutes.
To this end, it suffices to show

(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{f}}:(\varrho_{Y}\widetilde{f})_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k}^{d}\rightarrow(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{d}

is compatible with

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}:(W_{n}f)_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{Y}^{d}

via
\vartheta_{X}
and
\vartheta_{Y}
.
Because the map

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}:(W_{n}f)_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{Y}^{d}

is determined by the degree
-d
part of the map

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}:(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X}\rightarrow K_{n,Y}
,
we are reduced to show compatibility of

(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{f}}:(\varrho_{Y}\widetilde{f})_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k}^{d}\rightarrow(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{d}

with

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}:(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X}\rightarrow K_{n,Y}
via
(W_{n}f)_{*}(s_{X}\circ\vartheta_{X}^{-1})
and
s_{Y}\circ\vartheta_{Y}^{-1}
.
By the commutativity of the left and right squares, this is reduced to the commutativity of the square on the back, which is known. Therefore the bottom square commutes as a result.
∎

The notation
\tau_{\widetilde{f}}
is only temporarily used in the lemma above. Later we will denote
\tau_{\widetilde{f}}
by
\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{f}}
.

Lemma 1.13

(
[
1
, 8.4(ii)]
)
.

Let
W_{n}\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet}
denote the relative de Rham-Witt complex defined by
[

]
.
The rest of the notations are the same as above.
