There is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{W_{n+1}\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{n}}
\textstyle{Z^{q}(\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{Y}^{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta_{Y}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{q}(\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k}^{\bullet}).}

Recall that any element in
W_{n}\Omega_{k[X_{1},...,X_{d}]}^{d}
is uniquely written as a sum of (
1.3.7
) and (
1.3.8
).

Lemma 1.14
.

Let

C^{\prime}=C_{n}^{\prime}:W_{n}\Omega_{k[X_{1},...,X_{d}]}^{d}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{k[X_{1},...,X_{d}]}^{d}.

be the map given by the
-d
-th cohomology of the level
n
Cartier operator for residual complexes (cf. (
1.2.3
)).
Let
\alpha=\sum_{j=0}^{n+v_{1}-1}V^{j}[\alpha_{j}]\in W_{n+v_{1}}k
with each
\alpha_{j}\in k
.
Let
\beta=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}V^{j}[\beta_{j}]\in W_{n}k
with each
\beta_{j}\in k
.

(1)

If
v_{1}=1-n
,

C^{\prime}\Big(dV^{-v_{1}}(\alpha[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}})\cdots dV^{-v_{r}}([X_{r}]^{h_{r}^{\prime}})\cdot F^{v_{r+1}}d[X_{r+1}]^{h_{r+1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{v_{d}}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}\Big)=0.

(2)

If
1-n<v_{1}<0
,
v_{r+1}=...=v_{r+s}=0
(
s
can be zero),

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\Big(dV^{-v_{1}}(\alpha[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}})\cdots dV^{-v_{r}}([X_{r}]^{h_{r}^{\prime}})\cdot F^{v_{r+1}}d[X_{r+1}]^{h_{r+1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{v_{d}}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle=dV^{1-v_{1}}(R(\widetilde{\alpha})[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}})\cdots dV^{1-v_{r}}[X_{r}]^{h_{r}^{\prime}}\cdot dV[X_{r+1}]^{h_{r+1}^{\prime}}\cdots dV[X_{r+s}]^{h_{r+s}^{\prime}}

\cdot F^{v_{r+s+1}-1}d[X_{r+s+1}]^{h_{r+s+1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{v_{d}-1}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}
.

Here

\widetilde{\alpha}=\sum_{j=0}^{n+v_{1}-1}V^{j}[\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}]_{n+v_{1}+1-j}\in W_{n+v_{1}+1}(W_{n}k),

where
\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}:=[\alpha_{j}]_{n}\in W_{n}k
is the Teichmüller lift of
\alpha_{j}\in k
.

(3)

If

v_{1}\geq 0
,
v_{1}=...=v_{s}=0
(
s
can be zero),

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\Big(\beta F^{v_{1}}d[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{v_{d}}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle=(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\beta)\cdot dV[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots dV[X_{s}]^{h_{s}^{\prime}}\cdot F^{v_{s+1}-1}d[X_{s+1}]^{h_{s+1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{v_{d}-1}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}.

Here

\widetilde{\beta}=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}V^{j}[\widetilde{\beta}_{j}]_{n+1-j}\in W_{n+1}(W_{n}k),

where
\widetilde{\beta}_{j}:=[\beta_{j}]_{n}\in W_{n}k
is the Teichmüller lifts of
\beta_{j}\in k
.

Proof.

Consider the map
W_{n}F_{X}:W_{n}X\rightarrow W_{n}X
with
X:=\mathbf{A}^{d}_{k}
. It is not a map of
W_{n}k
-schemes a priori, but after labeling the source by
W_{n}X:=W_{n}\mathbf{A}^{d}_{k_{1}}
and the target by
W_{n}Y:=W_{n}\mathbf{A}^{d}_{k_{2}}
, one can realize
W_{n}F_{X}
as a map of
W_{n}k_{2}
-schemes (the
W_{n}k_{2}
-scheme structure of
W_{n}X
is given by
W_{n}F_{X}\circ W_{n}\pi_{Y}
, where
\pi_{Y}:Y\rightarrow k_{2}
denotes the structure morphism of the scheme
Y
).
Write

\widetilde{X}=\mathbf{A}^{d}_{W_{n}k_{1}}=\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k_{1}[X_{1},\dots,X_{d}]\quad(\text{resp. $\widetilde{Y}=\mathbf{A}^{d}_{W_{n}k_{2}}=\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k_{2}[X_{1},\dots,X_{d}]$}),

and take the canonical lift
\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}
of
F_{X}
as in
Lemma

1.11
. Consider

(1.3.26)

\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X/k_{1}}^{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(\ref{(1.0.2)})}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}\vartheta_{X}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{X/k_{2}}^{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}F_{X}}}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}\vartheta_{X}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{Y/k_{2}}^{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta_{Y}}
\textstyle{(\varrho_{Y}\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}})_{*}\mathcal{H}^{d}(\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k_{1}}^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\varrho_{Y}\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}})_{*}\pi_{\widetilde{X}}^{\triangle}(\ref{(1.0.1)})}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(\varrho_{Y}\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}})_{*}\mathcal{H}^{d}(\Omega_{\widetilde{X}/W_{n}k_{2}}^{\bullet})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\varrho_{Y})_{*}(\operatorname{Tr}_{\widetilde{F}_{\widetilde{X}}})}
\textstyle{(\varrho_{Y})_{*}\mathcal{H}^{d}(\Omega_{\widetilde{Y}/W_{n}k_{2}}^{\bullet}).}

The composite map of the top row is
C^{\prime}
(cf. (
1.2.3
) and Ekedahl’s quasi-isomorphism
Proposition

1.3
). The composite of the bottom row is induced from
\varrho_{Y,*}
(
1.3.9
).
The right side commutes due to
Lemma

1.12
. The left side commutes by the naturality.
Hence we can decompose
C^{\prime}
in the following way:

C^{\prime}=\vartheta_{Y}^{-1}\circ(\ref{eq:trace of lift Frob on Coh})\circ\vartheta_{X}:W_{n}\Omega_{X/k_{1}}^{d}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{Y/k_{2}}^{d}.

Consider the first two cases. Suppose
v_{1}<0
and suppose there are
s
many
v_{j}
’s being zero,

v_{1}\leq\cdots\leq v_{r}<0=\cdots=0<v_{r+s+1}\leq\cdots\leq v_{d}.

Note that in
W_{n+1}\Omega_{(W_{n}k)[X_{1},...,X_{d}]/W_{n}k}^{1}
:

(1.3.27)

\displaystyle F^{n+v_{1}}d(\widetilde{\alpha}[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=F^{n+v_{1}}d\Big([X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}}\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{n+v_{1}-1}V^{j}[\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}]\Big)

\displaystyle=F^{n+v_{1}}d\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{n+v_{1}-1}V^{j}[\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}X_{1}^{h_{1}^{\prime}p^{j}}]\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n+v_{1}-1}F^{n+v_{1}-j}d[\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}X_{1}^{h_{1}^{\prime}p^{j}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n+v_{1}-1}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}X_{1}^{h_{1}^{\prime}p^{j}})^{p^{n+v_{1}-j}-1}d(\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}X_{1}^{h_{1}^{\prime}p^{j}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{n+v_{1}-1}h_{1}^{\prime}p^{j}\cdot\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}^{p^{n+v_{1}-j}}\cdot X_{1}^{h_{1}^{\prime}p^{n+v_{1}}-1}dX_{1}

Similarly we have

(1.3.28)

\sum_{j=0}^{n+v_{1}-1}p^{j}h_{1}^{\prime}\cdots h_{d}^{\prime}\cdot(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}^{p^{n+v_{1}-j}})X_{1}^{h_{1}^{\prime}p^{n+v_{1}-1}-1}dX_{1}=F^{n}dV^{1-v_{1}}(R(\widetilde{\alpha})[X_{1}]^{h_{1}}).

Here
R
is the restriction map
R:W_{n+v_{1}+1}(W_{n}k)\rightarrow W_{n+v_{1}}(W_{n}k)
.
Now according to the formula (
1.3.10
) and
Lemma

1.13
, we carry out the following calculations.

(1.3.29)

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\Big(dV^{-v_{1}}(\alpha[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}})\cdots dV^{-v_{r}}([X_{r}]^{h_{r}^{\prime}})\cdot F^{v_{r+1}}d[X_{r+1}]^{h_{r+1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{v_{d}}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{Y}^{-1}\circ(\ref{eq:trace of lift Frob on Coh})\circ F^{n}\Big(dV^{-v_{1}}(\widetilde{\alpha}[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}})\cdots dV^{-v_{r}}([X_{r}]^{h_{r}^{\prime}})\cdot F^{v_{r+1}}d[X_{r+1}]^{h_{r+1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{v_{d}}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{Y}^{-1}\circ(\ref{eq:trace of lift Frob on Coh})\Big(F^{n+v_{1}}d(\widetilde{\alpha}[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}})\cdots F^{n+v_{r}}d([X_{r}]^{h_{r}^{\prime}})\cdot F^{n+v_{r+1}}d[X_{r+1}]^{h_{r+1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{n+v_{d}}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{Y}^{-1}\circ(\ref{eq:trace of lift Frob on Coh})\Big(\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{n+v_{1}-1}h_{1}^{\prime}p^{j}\cdot\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}^{p^{n+v_{1}-j}}\cdot X_{1}^{h_{1}^{\prime}p^{n+v_{1}}-1}dX_{1}\Big)\cdot\Big(h_{2}^{\prime}\cdot X_{2}^{h_{2}^{\prime}p^{n+v_{2}}-1}dX_{2}\Big)\cdot

\cdots\Big(h_{d}^{\prime}\cdot X_{d}^{h_{d}^{\prime}p^{n+v_{d}}-1}dX_{d}\Big)\Big)

(
by (
1.3.27
)
)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{Y}^{-1}\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{n+v_{1}-1}p^{j}h_{1}^{\prime}\cdots h_{d}^{\prime}\cdot(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\widetilde{\alpha}_{j}^{p^{n+v_{1}-j}})X_{1}^{h_{1}^{\prime}p^{n+v_{1}-1}-1}dX_{1}\cdots X_{d}^{h_{d}^{\prime}p^{n+v_{d}-1}-1}dX_{d}\Big)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{Y}^{-1}\Big(F^{n}dV^{1-v_{1}}(R(\widetilde{\alpha})[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}})\cdots F^{n}dV^{1-v_{r}}[X_{r}]^{h_{r}^{\prime}}\cdot F^{n}dV[X_{r+1}]^{h_{r+1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{n}dV[X_{r+s}]^{h_{r+s}^{\prime}}

\cdot F^{n+v_{r+s+1}-1}d[X_{r+s+1}]^{h_{r+s+1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{n+v_{d}-1}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}
)

(
by (
1.3.28
)
)

If
v_{1}=1-n
,
(\ref{PartNonInt})=0
because

F^{n}dV^{1-v_{1}}(R(\widetilde{\alpha})[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}})=d(R(\widetilde{\alpha})[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}})=0

in
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{(W_{n}k)[X_{1},...,X_{d}]/W_{n}k})
.
If
v_{1}\neq 1-n
(hence
v_{j}>1-n
for all
j
),

\displaystyle(\ref{PartNonInt})

\displaystyle=dV^{1-v_{1}}(R(\widetilde{\alpha})[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}})\cdots dV^{1-v_{r}}[X_{r}]^{h_{r}^{\prime}}

\cdot dV[X_{r+1}]^{h_{r+1}^{\prime}}\cdots dV[X_{r+s}]^{h_{r+s}^{\prime}}\cdot F^{v_{r+s+1}-1}d[X_{r+s+1}]^{h_{r+s+1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{v_{d}-1}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}

and this is the same as what our lemma claims.

Now we check the third case.
If all the
v_{j}\geq 0
, suppose the first
s

v_{j}
’s are zero,

0=\cdots=0<v_{s+1}\leq\cdots\leq v_{d}.

Note that in
W_{n}k
,

(1.3.30)

\displaystyle(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(F^{n}(\widetilde{\beta}))=(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(F^{n}(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}V^{j}[\widetilde{\beta}_{j}]_{n+1-j}))=(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}F^{n-j}[\widetilde{\beta}_{j}]_{n+1-j}))

\displaystyle=(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\widetilde{\beta}_{j}^{p^{n-j}})=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\widetilde{\beta}_{j}^{p^{n-j-1}}=F^{n}\Big(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}V^{j}[(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}\widetilde{\beta}_{j}]_{n+1-j}\Big)=F^{n}\Big((W_{n+1}(W_{n}F_{k}))^{-1}(\beta)\Big).

We carry out the computation

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\Big(\beta F^{v_{1}}d[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{v_{d}}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{Y}^{-1}\circ(\ref{eq:trace of lift Frob on Coh})\circ F^{n}\Big(\widetilde{\beta}\cdot F^{v_{1}}d[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{v_{d}}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{Y}^{-1}\circ(\ref{eq:trace of lift Frob on Coh})\Big(F^{n}(\widetilde{\beta})\cdot F^{n+v_{1}}d[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{n+v_{d}}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{Y}^{-1}\circ(\ref{eq:trace of lift Frob on Coh})\Big(F^{n}(\widetilde{\beta})\cdot h_{1}^{\prime}\cdots h_{d}^{\prime}X_{1}^{h_{1}^{\prime}p^{n+v_{1}}-1}dX_{1}\cdots F^{n+v_{d}}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{Y}^{-1}\Big((W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(F^{n}(\widetilde{\beta}))\cdot h_{1}^{\prime}X_{1}^{h_{1}^{\prime}p^{n+v_{1}-1}-1}dX_{1}\cdots h_{d}^{\prime}X_{d}^{h_{d}^{\prime}p^{n+v_{d}-1}-1}dX_{d}\Big)

\displaystyle=\vartheta_{Y}^{-1}\Big(F^{n}\Big((W_{n+1}(W_{n}F_{k}))^{-1}(\beta)\Big)\cdot F^{n}dV[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{n}dV[X_{s}]^{h_{s}^{\prime}}\cdot

F^{n+v_{s+1}-1}d[X_{s+1}]^{h_{s+1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{n+v_{d}-1}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}

)

(
by (
1.3.30
))

\displaystyle=(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\beta)\cdot dV[X_{1}]^{h_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots dV[X_{s}]^{h_{s}^{\prime}}\cdot

F^{v_{s+1}-1}d[X_{s+1}]^{h_{s+1}^{\prime}}\cdots F^{v_{d}-1}d[X_{d}]^{h_{d}^{\prime}}

In the last equality we have used that

\vartheta_{Y}^{-1}\Big(F^{n}\Big((W_{n+1}(W_{n}F_{k}))^{-1}(\beta)\Big)\Big)=(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(\beta).

We hence proved the lemma.

∎

1.3.4.
Criterion for surjectivity of
C^{\prime}-1

The following proposition is proven in the smooth case by Illusie-Raynaud-Suwa
[
40
, 2.1]
. The proof presented here is due to Rülling.

Proposition 1.15

(Raynaud-Illusie-Suwa)
.

Let
k=\overline{k}
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
p>0
and let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over
k
.
Then for every
i
,

C^{\prime}-1
induces a surjective map on global cohomology groups

H^{i}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X}):=R^{i}\Gamma(W_{n}X,K_{n,X})\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}-1}H^{i}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X}).

In particular,

R^{i}\Gamma(W_{n}X,K_{n,X,log})\simeq H^{i}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X})^{C^{\prime}-1}.

Proof.

Take a Nagata compactification of
X
, i.e., an open immersion

j:X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}

such that
\overline{X}
is proper over
k
.
The boundary
\overline{X}\setminus X
is a closed subscheme in
\overline{X}
. By blowing up in
\overline{X}
one can assume
\overline{X}\setminus X
is the closed subscheme associated to an effective Cartier divisor
D
on
\overline{X}
. We can thus assume
j
is an affine morphism.
Therefore

W_{n}j:W_{n}X\hookrightarrow W_{n}\overline{X}

is also an affine morphism.

For any quasi-coherent sheaf
\mathcal{M}
on
W_{n}\overline{X}
, the difference between
\mathcal{M}
and
(W_{n}j)_{*}(W_{n}j)^{*}\mathcal{M}
are precisely those sections that have poles (of any order) at
{\operatorname{Supp}\,}D=W_{n}\overline{X}\setminus W_{n}X
.
Suppose that the effective Cartier divisor
D
is represented by
(U_{i},f_{i})_{i}
, where
\{U_{i}\}_{i}
is an affine cover of
\overline{X}
, and
f_{i}\in\Gamma(U_{i},\mathcal{O}_{X})
.
Recall that
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD)
denotes the line bundle on
\overline{X}
which is the inverse (as line bundles) of the
m
-th power of the ideal sheaf of
\overline{X}\setminus X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
. Locally, one has an isomorphism

\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD)\mid_{U_{i}}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}}\cdot\frac{1}{f_{i}^{m}}

for each
i
.
Denote by
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD)
the line bundle on
W_{n}\overline{X}
such that

W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD)\mid_{U_{i}}\simeq W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}}\cdot\frac{1}{[f_{i}]^{m}},

where
[-]=[-]_{n}
denotes the Teichmüller lift. Denote

\mathcal{M}(mD):=\mathcal{M}\otimes_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD).

The natural map

(1.3.31)

\mathcal{M}(*D):=\operatorname{colim}_{m}\mathcal{M}(mD)\xrightarrow{\simeq}(W_{n}j)_{*}(W_{n}j)^{*}(\mathcal{M}(mD))=(W_{n}j)_{*}(W_{n}j)^{*}\mathcal{M}

is an isomorphism of sheaves.
Here the inductive system on the left hand side is given by the natural map

\mathcal{M}(mD):=\mathcal{M}\otimes_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}\otimes_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}((m+1)D)

induced from the inclusion
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD)\hookrightarrow W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}((m+1)D)
, i.e., locally on
U_{i}
, this inclusion is the map

\displaystyle W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD)\mid_{U_{i}}

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}((m+1)D)\mid_{U_{i}}

\displaystyle\frac{a}{[f_{i}]^{m}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{a[f_{i}]}{[f_{i}]^{m+1}}.

where
a\in W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}}
. As a result,

(1.3.32)

\displaystyle H^{i}(W_{n}X,(W_{n}j)^{*}\mathcal{M})

\displaystyle=H^{i}(R\Gamma(W_{n}\overline{X},R(W_{n}j)_{*}(W_{n}j)^{*}\mathcal{M}))

\displaystyle=H^{i}(R\Gamma(W_{n}\overline{X},(W_{n}j)_{*}(W_{n}j)^{*}\mathcal{M}))\qquad\quad\text{($W_{n}j$ is affine)}

\displaystyle=H^{i}(R\Gamma(W_{n}\overline{X},\operatorname{colim}_{m}\mathcal{M}(mD))\qquad\qquad\text{(\ref{eq:ind lim M})}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{colim}_{m}H^{i}(W_{n}\overline{X},\mathcal{M}(mD)).

Apply this to the bounded complex
K_{n,\overline{X}}
of injective quasi-coherent
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}
-modules.
Taking into account
K_{n,X}\simeq(W_{n}j)^{*}K_{n,\overline{X}}
by
Proposition

1.1
(2),
(
1.3.31
) gives an isomorphism of complexes

(1.3.33)

K_{n,\overline{X}}(*D):=\operatorname{colim}_{m}K_{n,\overline{X}}(mD)\xrightarrow{\simeq}(W_{n}j)_{*}K_{n,X},

and (
1.3.32
) gives an isomorphism of
W_{n}k
-modules

\operatorname{colim}_{m}H^{i}(W_{n}\overline{X},K_{n,\overline{X}}(mD))=H^{i}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X}).

Via the projection formula
[
21
, II.5.6]

and tensoring

C^{\prime}:(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}K_{n,\overline{X}}\rightarrow K_{n,\overline{X}}

with
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD)
, one gets a map

\displaystyle(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(K_{n,\overline{X}}

\displaystyle\otimes_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(pmD))\simeq(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(K_{n,\overline{X}}\otimes_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}(W_{n}F_{X})^{*}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD))

\displaystyle\simeq((W_{n}F_{X})_{*}K_{n,\overline{X}})\otimes_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD)\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}\otimes{\operatorname{id}}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD)}}K_{n,\overline{X}}\otimes_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD).

Precomposing with the natural map

(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(K_{n,\overline{X}}\otimes_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD))\rightarrow(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(K_{n,\overline{X}}\otimes_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(pmD)),

and taking the global section cohomologies, one gets

C^{\prime}:H^{i}(W_{n}\overline{X},K_{n,\overline{X}}(mD))\rightarrow H^{i}(W_{n}\overline{X},K_{n,\overline{X}}(mD)).

To show the surjectivity of

C^{\prime}-1:H^{i}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X})\xrightarrow{}H^{i}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X}),

it suffices to show the surjectivity for

C^{\prime}-1:H^{i}(W_{n}\overline{X},K_{n,\overline{X}}(mD))\rightarrow H^{i}(W_{n}\overline{X},K_{n,\overline{X}}(mD)).

Because
\mathcal{H}^{q}(K_{n,\overline{X}})
are coherent sheaves on the proper scheme
\overline{X}
for all
q
,
\mathcal{H}^{q}(K_{n,\overline{X}}\otimes_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD))=\mathcal{H}^{q}(K_{n,\overline{X}})\otimes_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}(mD)

are also coherent, therefore the local-to-global spectral sequence implies that

M:=H^{i}(W_{n}\overline{X},K_{n,\overline{X}}(mD))

is a finite
W_{n}k
-module.
Now
M
is equipped with an endomorphism
C^{\prime}
which acts
p^{-1}
-linearly (cf.
Definition

A.4
). The proposition is then a direct consequence of
Proposition

A.6
.
∎

The following proposition is a corollary of
[
40
, Lemma 2.1]
. We restate it here as a convenient reference.

Proposition 1.16

(Raynaud-Illusie-Suwa)
.

Assume
k=\overline{k}
.
If
X
is separated smooth over
k
of pure dimension
d
,

C-1:W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X}

is surjective.

Proof.

Apply affine locally the
H^{-d}
-case of
Proposition

1.15
. Then Ekedahl’s quasi-isomorphism
W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X}[d]\simeq K_{n,X}
from
Proposition

1.3
together with compatibility of
C^{\prime}
and
C
from
Theorem

1.9
gives the claim.
∎

Remark 1.17
.

If
X
is Cohen-Macaulay of pure dimension
d
,
W_{n}X
is also Cohen-Macaulay by Serre’s
S_{k}
-criterion (
[
18
, (5.7.3)(i)]
) of the same pure dimension, and thus the complex
K_{n,X}
is concentrated at degree
-d
for all
n

[
9
, 3.5.1]
. Denote by
W_{n}\omega_{X}
the only nonzero cohomology sheaf of
K_{n,X}
in this case. Then the same reasoning as in
Proposition

1.16
shows that if
k=\overline{k}
and
X
is Cohen-Macaulay over
k
of pure dimension, the map

C^{\prime}-1:W_{n}\omega_{X}\rightarrow W_{n}\omega_{X}

is surjective.

1.3.5.
Comparison between
W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,log}
and
K_{n,X,log}

Let
X
be a
k
-scheme.
Denote by
d\log
the following map of abelian étale sheaves

\displaystyle d\log:(\mathcal{O}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}^{*})^{\otimes q}

\displaystyle\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}^{q},

\displaystyle a_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{q}

\displaystyle\mapsto d\log[a_{1}]_{n}\dots d\log[a_{q}]_{n},

where
a_{1},\dots,a_{q}\in\mathcal{O}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}^{*}
,
[-]_{n}:\mathcal{O}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
denotes the Teichmüller lift, and
d\log[a_{i}]_{n}:=\frac{d[a_{i}]_{n}}{[a_{i}]_{n}}
. We will denote its sheaf theoretic image by

W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
and call it the étale sheaf of log forms. We denote by
W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{X,log}:=W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{X,log,{\operatorname{Zar}}}:=\epsilon_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
, and call it the Zariski sheaf of log forms.

Lemma 1.18

(
[
8
, lemme 2]
,
[
16
, 1.6(ii)]
)
.

Let
X
be a separated smooth
k
-scheme. Then we have the following left exact sequences

(1.3.34)

0\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{X,log}^{q}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{q}\xrightarrow{1-\overline{F}}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{q}/dV^{n-1},

(1.3.35)

0\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{X,log}^{q}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{\prime q}\xrightarrow{C-1}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{q},

where
W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{\prime q}:=F(W_{n+1}\Omega_{X}^{q})
.
The right hand maps are also surjective if
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
.

The following proposition collects what we have done so far.

Proposition 1.19

(cf.
[
30
, Prop. 4.2]
)
.

Let
X
be a separated smooth scheme of pure dimension
d
over a perfect field
k
. Then

(1)

we have

\mathcal{H}^{-d}(K_{n,X,log})=W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,log}
, and
\mathcal{H}^{i}(K_{n,X,log})=0
for all
i\neq-d,-d+1
.

(2)

If
k=\overline{k}
, the natural map

W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,log}[d]\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X,log}

is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes of abelian sheaves.

Proof.

(1)

Since
C
is compatible with
C^{\prime}
by
Theorem

1.9
,
the natural map

\operatorname{Cone}(W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X}[d]\xrightarrow{C-1}W_{n}\Omega_{X}^{d}[d])[-1]\rightarrow K_{n,X,log}
is a quasi-isomorphism by the five lemma and the Ekedahl quasi-isomorphism
Proposition

1.3
. The claim thus follows from the exact sequence (
1.3.35
).

(2)

Proposition

1.16
+(1) above.

∎

1.4.
Localization triangle associated to
K_{n,X,log}

1.4.1.
Definition of
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f,log}

Proposition 1.20

(Proper pushforward, cf.
[
30
, (3.2.3)]
)
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a proper map between separated schemes of finite type over
k
. Then so is
W_{n}f:W_{n}X\rightarrow W_{n}Y
, and we have a map

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f,log}:(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X,log}\rightarrow K_{n,Y,log}

of complexes that fits into the following commutative diagram of complexes, where the two rows are distinguished triangles in
D^{b}(W_{n}X,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})

\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X,log}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f,log}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}-1}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y,log}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}-1}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
.

Moreover
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f,log}
is compatible with compositions and open restrictions.

This is the covariant functoriality of
K_{n,X,log}
with respect to proper morphisms. Thus we also denote
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f,log}
by
f_{*}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show the following diagrams commute.

\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}(W_{n}f)_{*}(\ref{(1.0.2)})}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}(\ref{(1.0.2)})}
\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}(W_{n}F_{Y})^{\triangle}K_{n,Y},}

\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}F_{X}}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}(W_{n}F_{Y})^{\triangle}K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}F_{Y}}}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y},}

where
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}
on the right of the first diagram and the left of the second diagram denote the trace map of the residual complex
(W_{n}F_{Y})^{\triangle}K_{n,Y}
:

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}:(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\simeq(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}f)^{\triangle}(W_{n}F_{Y})^{\triangle}K_{n,Y}\rightarrow(W_{n}F_{Y})^{\triangle}K_{n,Y}.

The commutativity of the first diagram is due to the functoriality of the trace map with respect to residual complexes with the same associated filtration (
Proposition

1.2
(3)). The commutativity of the second is because of the compatibility of the trace map with compositions of morphisms (
Proposition

1.2
(4)).
∎

1.4.2.
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f,log}
in the case of a nilpotent immersion

Proposition 1.21

(Rülling. Cf.
[
30
, 4.2]
)
.

Let
i:X_{0}\hookrightarrow X
be a nilpotent immersion (thus so is
W_{n}i:W_{n}(X_{0})\rightarrow W_{n}X
). Then the natural map

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i,log}:(W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,X_{0},log}\rightarrow K_{n,X,log}

is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

Put
{I_{n}}:=\operatorname{Ker}(W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow(W_{n}i)_{*}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}})
.
Applying
{\mathcal{H}om}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}}(-,K_{n,X})
to the sequence of
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules

(1.4.1)

0\rightarrow{I_{n}}\rightarrow W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow(W_{n}i)_{*}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}}\rightarrow 0,

we get again a short exact sequence of complexes of
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules

0\rightarrow(W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,X_{0}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i}}K_{n,X}\rightarrow Q_{n}:={\mathcal{H}om}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}}({I_{n}},K_{n,X})\rightarrow 0.

The first map is clearly
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i}
by duality. The restriction of the map
(W_{n}F_{X})^{*}:W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
to
{I_{n}}
gives a map

\displaystyle(W_{n}F_{X})^{*}\mid_{I_{n}}:\quad{I_{n}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}{I_{n}},

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}V([a_{i}])

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}V([a_{i}^{p}]).

Define

(1.4.2)

\displaystyle C^{\prime}_{{I_{n}}}:(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}Q_{n}

\displaystyle=(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}{\mathcal{H}om}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}}({I_{n}},K_{n,X})

\displaystyle\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}om}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}}((W_{n}F_{X})_{*}{I_{n}},(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}K_{n,X})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\simeq]{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(\ref{(1.0.2)})\circ}{\mathcal{H}om}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}}((W_{n}F_{X})_{*}{I_{n}},(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}F_{X}}\circ}{\mathcal{H}om}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}}((W_{n}F_{X})_{*}{I_{n}},K_{n,X})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{((W_{n}F_{X}^{*})\mid_{I_{n}})^{\vee}}{\mathcal{H}om}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}}({I_{n}},K_{n,X})=Q_{n}.

According to the definition of
C^{\prime}
in (
1.2.3
),
C^{\prime}
is compatible with
C^{\prime}_{I_{n}}
. Thus one has the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i}}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}Q_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}_{{I_{n}}}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i}}
\textstyle{K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Replacing
C^{\prime}
by
C^{\prime}-1
, and
C^{\prime}_{I_{n}}
by
C^{\prime}_{I_{n}}-1
, we arrive at the two lower rows of the following diagram.
Denote

Q_{n,log}:=\operatorname{Cone}(Q_{n}\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}_{{I_{n}}}-1}Q_{n})[-1].

Taking into account the shifted cones of
C^{\prime}-1
and
C^{\prime}_{I_{n}}-1
, we get the first row of the following diagram which is naturally a short exact sequence. Now we have the whole commutative diagram of complexes, where all the three rows are exact, and all the three columns are distinguished triangles in the derived category:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,X_{0},log}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i,log}}
\textstyle{K_{n,X,log}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Q_{n,log}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}-1}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i}}
\textstyle{K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}-1}
\textstyle{Q_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}_{{I_{n}}}-1}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,X_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i}}
\textstyle{K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\textstyle{Q_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\textstyle{0.}

We want to show that
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i,log}
is a quasi-isomorphism. By the exactness of the first row, it suffices to show that
Q_{n,log}
is an acyclic complex.
Because the right column is a distinguished triangle, it suffices to show that
C^{\prime}_{{I_{n}}}-1:Q_{n}\rightarrow Q_{n}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Actually it is even an isomorphism of complexes: since
(W_{n}F_{X})^{*}\mid_{I_{n}}:{I_{n}}\rightarrow(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}{I_{n}}
is nilpotent (because
I_{1}=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow i_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{0}})
is a finitely generated nilpotent ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
), the map
C^{\prime}_{{I_{n}}}:Q_{n}\rightarrow Q_{n}
is therefore nilpotent (because one can alter the order of the three labeled maps in (
1.4.2
) in the obvious sense), and
C^{\prime}_{{I_{n}}}-1
is therefore an isomorphism of complexes.
∎

1.4.3.
Localization triangles associated to
K_{n,X,log}

Let
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion with
j:U\hookrightarrow X
being its open complement. Recall

(1.4.3)

\underline{\Gamma}_{Z}(\mathcal{F}):=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal{F}\rightarrow j_{*}j^{-1}\mathcal{F})

for any abelian sheaf
\mathcal{F}
. Denote its
i
-th derived functor by
\mathcal{H}^{i}_{Z}(\mathcal{F})
.
Notice that

•

\Gamma_{Z^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})=\Gamma_{Z}(\mathcal{F})
for any nilpotent thickening
Z^{\prime}
of
Z
(e.g.
Z^{\prime}=W_{n}Z
),

•

\mathcal{F}\rightarrow j_{*}j^{-1}\mathcal{F}
is surjective whenever
\mathcal{F}
is flasque, and

•

flasque sheaves are
\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}
-acyclic (
[
22
, 1.10]
) and
f_{*}
-acyclic for any morphism
f
.

Therefore, for any complex of flasque sheaves
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
of
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-modules on
W_{n}X
,

0\rightarrow\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}\rightarrow(W_{n}j)_{*}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}|_{W_{n}U})\rightarrow 0

is a short exact sequence of complexes. Thus the induced triangle

(1.4.4)

\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}\rightarrow(W_{n}j)_{*}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}|_{W_{n}U})\xrightarrow{+1}

is a distinguished triangle in
D^{b}(W_{n}X,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
, whenever
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
is a flasque complex with bounded cohomologies. In particular, since
K_{n,X,log}
is a bounded complex of flasque sheaves, this is true for
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}=K_{n,X,log}
.

The following proposition is proven in the smooth case by Gros-Milne-Suwa
[
40
, 2.6]
. The proof presented here comes from an unpublished manuscript of Rülling.

Proposition 1.22

(Rülling)
.

Let
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion with
j:U\hookrightarrow X
its open complement. Then

(1)

(Purity)
The map

(W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,Z,log}=\underline{\Gamma}_{Z}((W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,Z,log})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i,log}}\underline{\Gamma}_{Z}(K_{n,X,log})

is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes of sheaves.

(2)

(Localization triangle)
The following

(1.4.5)

(W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,Z,log}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i,log}}K_{n,X,log}\rightarrow(W_{n}j)_{*}K_{n,U,log}\xrightarrow{+1}

is a distinguished triangle in
D^{b}(W_{n}X,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
.

Note that we are working on the Zariski site and abelian sheaves on
W_{n}X
can be identified with abelian sheaves on
X
canonically. Thus we can replace
(W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,Z,log}
by
i_{*}K_{n,Z,log}
, and
(W_{n}j)_{*}K_{n,U,log}
by
j_{*}K_{n,U,log}
freely.

Proof.

(1)

Let
I_{n}
be the ideal sheaf associated to the closed immersion
W_{n}i:W_{n}Z\hookrightarrow W_{n}X
, and let
Z_{n,m}
be the closed subscheme of
W_{n}X
determined by
m
-th power ideal
I_{n}^{m}
. In particular,
Z_{n,1}=W_{n}Z
. Denote by
i_{n,m}:Z_{n,m}\hookrightarrow W_{n}X
and by
j_{n,m}:W_{n}Z\hookrightarrow Z_{n,m}
the associated closed immersions.
In this way, for each
m
, one has a decomposition of
W_{n}i
as maps of
W_{n}k
-schemes:

\textstyle{W_{n}Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{n,m}}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}\pi_{Z}}
\textstyle{Z_{n,m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{n,m}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{Z_{n,m}}}
\textstyle{W_{n}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}\pi_{X}}
\textstyle{W_{n}k.}

Denote
K_{Z_{n,m}}:=(\pi_{Z_{n,m}})^{\triangle}(W_{n}k)
, where
\pi_{Z_{n,m}}:Z_{n,m}\rightarrow W_{n}k
is the structure morphism.
We have a canonical isomorphism

(1.4.6)

i_{n,m,*}\mathcal{H}^{i}(K_{Z_{n,m}})\simeq{\mathcal{E}xt}^{i}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}}(i_{n,m,*}\mathcal{O}_{Z_{n,m}},K_{n,X})

by
Proposition

1.1
(4) and
Proposition

1.1
(1) associated to the closed immersion
i_{n,m}
.
The trace maps associated to the closed immersions

Z_{n,m}\hookrightarrow Z_{n,m+1}

for different
m
make the left hand side of (
1.4.6
) an inductive system.
The right hand side also lies in an inductive system when
m
varies: the canonical surjections

i_{n,m+1,*}\mathcal{O}_{Z_{n,m+1}}\rightarrow i_{n,m,*}\mathcal{O}_{Z_{n,m}}

induce the maps

(1.4.7)

{\mathcal{H}om}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}}(i_{n,m,*}\mathcal{O}_{Z_{n,m}},K_{n,X})\rightarrow{\mathcal{H}om}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}}(i_{n,m+1,*}\mathcal{O}_{Z_{n,m+1}},K_{n,X})

whose
i
-th cohomologies are the connecting homomorphisms of the inductive system. By duality, the map (
1.4.7
) is the trace map associated to the closed immersion
Z_{n,m}\hookrightarrow Z_{n,m+1}
, and thus is compatible with the inductive system on the left hand side of (
1.4.6
).

Consider the trace map associated to the closed immersion
i_{n,m}:Z_{n,m}\hookrightarrow W_{n}X
, i.e., the evaluation-at-1 map

{\mathcal{H}om}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}}(i_{n,m,*}\mathcal{O}_{Z_{n,m}},K_{n,X})\rightarrow K_{n,X}.

Its image naturally lies in
\Gamma_{W_{n}Z}(K_{n,X})
.
It induces an isomorphism on cohomology sheaves after taking the colimit on
m

\operatorname{colim}_{m}{\mathcal{E}xt}^{i}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}}(i_{n,m,*}\mathcal{O}_{Z_{n,m}},K_{n,X})\xrightarrow[\simeq]{{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{i}_{Z}(K_{n,X})

by
[
21
, V.4.3]
.

Now we consider

(1.4.8)

\displaystyle\operatorname{colim}_{m}i_{n,m,*}\mathcal{H}^{i}(K_{Z_{n,m}})

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{colim}_{m}{\mathcal{E}xt}^{i}_{W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}}(i_{n,m,*}\mathcal{O}_{Z_{n,m}},K_{n,X})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\simeq]{{\mathrm{ev}}_{1}}\mathcal{H}^{i}_{Z}(K_{n,X}).

The composite map of (
1.4.8
) is
\operatorname{colim}_{m}\operatorname{Tr}_{i_{n,m}}
.
On the other hand, consider the log trace associated to the closed immersion
i_{n,m}

(cf.
Proposition

1.20
)

(1.4.9)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}_{i_{n,m},log}:\mathcal{H}^{i}(i_{n,m,*}K_{Z_{n,m},log})=\mathcal{H}^{i}(\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}

\displaystyle(i_{n,m,*}K_{Z_{n,m},log}))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{}\mathcal{H}^{i}(\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}(K_{n,X,log}))=\mathcal{H}^{i}_{Z}(K_{n,X,log}).

The maps (
1.4.8
), (
1.4.9
) give the vertical maps in the following diagram (due to formatting reason we omit
i_{n,m,*}
from every term of the first row) which are automatically compatible by
Proposition

1.20
:

\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{i-1}(K_{Z_{n,m}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}-1}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{i_{n,m}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{i-1}(K_{Z_{n,m}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{i_{n,m}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{i}(K_{Z_{n,m},log})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{i_{n,m},log}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{i}(K_{Z_{n,m}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}-1}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{i_{n,m}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{i}(K_{Z_{n,m}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{i_{n,m}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{i-1}_{Z}(K_{n,X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}-1}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{i-1}_{Z}(K_{n,X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{i}_{Z}(K_{n,X,log})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{i}_{Z}(K_{n,X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}-1}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{i}_{Z}(K_{n,X}).}

Taking the colimit with respect to
m
, the five lemma immediately gives that
\operatorname{colim}_{m}\operatorname{Tr}_{i_{n,m},log}
is an isomorphism. Then
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i,log}
, which is the composition of

(W_{n}i)_{*}\mathcal{H}^{i}(K_{n,Z,log})\xrightarrow[\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{Kato's complex thickening iso}},\simeq]{\operatorname{colim}_{m}\operatorname{Tr}_{j_{n,m},log}}\operatorname{colim}_{m}i_{n,m,*}\mathcal{H}^{i}(K_{Z_{n,m},log})\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\operatorname{colim}_{m}\operatorname{Tr}_{i_{n,m},log}}\mathcal{H}^{i}_{Z}(K_{n,X,log}),

is an isomorphism. This proves the statement.

(2)

Since
\underline{\Gamma}_{Z}(K_{n,X,log})\rightarrow K_{n,X,log}\rightarrow(W_{n}j)_{*}K_{n,U,log}\xrightarrow{+1}
is a distinguished triangle, the second part follows from the first part.

∎

1.5.
Functoriality

The push-forward functoriality of
K_{n,X,log}
has been done in
Proposition

1.20
for proper
f
. Now we define the pullback map for an étale morphism
f
. Since
W_{n}f
is then also étale, we have an isomorphism of functors
(W_{n}f)^{*}\simeq(W_{n}f)^{\triangle}
by
Proposition

1.1
(2). Define a chain map of complexes of
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules

(1.5.1)

f^{*}:K_{n,Y}\xrightarrow{{\mathrm{adj}}}(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}f)^{*}K_{n,Y}\simeq(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}f)^{\triangle}K_{n,Y}\simeq(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X}.

Here
{\mathrm{adj}}
stands for the adjunction map of the identity map of
(W_{n}f)^{*}K_{n,Y}
.

Proposition 1.23

(Étale pullback)
.

Suppose
f:X\rightarrow Y
is an étale morphism. Then

f^{*}:K_{n,Y,log}\rightarrow(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X,log},

defined by termwise applying (
1.5.1
), is a chain map between complexes of abelian sheaves.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
C^{\prime}
is compatible with
f^{*}
defined above. Consider the following diagram in the category of complexes of
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{Y}
-modules

\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a)}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{adj}}}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{(1.0.2)})}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}(W_{n}F_{Y})^{\triangle}K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d)}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{adj}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}F_{Y}}}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{adj}}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}f)^{*}(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b)}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{(1.0.2)})}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}f)^{*}(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}(W_{n}F_{Y})^{\triangle}K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e)}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}F_{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}f)^{*}K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(W_{n}f)^{*}K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c)}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{(1.0.2)})}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(W_{n}f)^{*}(W_{n}F_{Y})^{\triangle}K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{(1.0.2)})}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}F_{X}}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X}.}

In this diagram we use shortened notations for the maps due to formatting reasons, e.g. we write
(\ref{(1.0.2)})
instead of
(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(\ref{(1.0.2)})
, etc..
The maps labelled
\alpha
and
\beta
are base change maps, and they are isomorphisms because
W_{n}f
is flat (actually
W_{n}f
is étale because
f
is étale)
[
21
, II.5.12]
.
The composite of the maps on the very left and very right are
(W_{n}F_{Y})_{*}(f^{*})
and
f^{*}
(where
f^{*}
is as defined in (
1.5.1
)). The composite of the maps on the very top and very bottom are
C^{\prime}_{Y}
and
(W_{n}f)_{*}C^{\prime}_{X}
.
Diagrams
a),b),c),d)
commute due to naturality.
Diagram
e)
commutes, because we have a cartesian square

\textstyle{W_{n}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}F_{X}}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}f}
\textstyle{W_{n}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}f}
\textstyle{W_{n}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{W_{n}F_{Y}}
\textstyle{W_{n}Y}

by
Remark

1.8
(2), and then the base change formula of the Grothendieck trace map as given in
Proposition

1.2
(5) gives the result.
∎

Lemma 1.24
.

Consider the following cartesian diagram

\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y}

with
g
being proper, and
f
being étale.
Then we have a commutative diagram of residual complexes

\textstyle{(W_{n}g)_{*}K_{n,Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}g)_{*}(W_{n}f^{\prime})_{*}K_{n,W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}g^{\prime})_{*}K_{n,W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X}.}

Proof.

We decompose the diagram into the following two diagrams and show their commutativity one by one. First we consider

\textstyle{(W_{n}g)_{*}K_{n,Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{adj}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}g)_{*}(W_{n}f^{\prime})_{*}(W_{n}f^{\prime})^{*}K_{n,Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}g^{\prime})_{*}(W_{n}f^{\prime})^{*}(W_{n}g)^{\triangle}K_{n,Y}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}f)^{*}(W_{n}g)_{*}(W_{n}g)^{\triangle}K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathrm{adj}}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}f)^{*}K_{n,Y}.}

Here
\alpha
denotes the base change map, it is an isomorphism because
W_{n}f
is flat
[
21
, II.5.12]
. This diagram commutes by the naturality. Next we consider

\textstyle{(W_{n}g)_{*}(W_{n}f^{\prime})_{*}(W_{n}f^{\prime})^{*}K_{n,Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n}g)_{*}(W_{n}f^{\prime})_{*}K_{n,W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}g^{\prime})_{*}(W_{n}f^{\prime})^{*}(W_{n}g)^{\triangle}K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}g^{\prime})_{*}K_{n,W}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}f)^{*}(W_{n}g)_{*}(W_{n}g)^{\triangle}K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}f)^{*}K_{n,Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X}.}

The top part commutes by the naturality. The bottom part commutes by the base change formula of the Grothendieck trace maps with respect to étale morphisms (
Proposition

1.2
(5)).
∎

Since both
f^{*}
for log complexes in
Proposition

1.23
and
g_{*}:=\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g,log}
are defined termwise, we arrive immediately the following compatibility as a consequence of
Lemma

1.24
.

Proposition 1.25
.

Notations are the same as
Lemma

1.24
. One has a commutative diagram of complexes

\textstyle{(W_{n}g)_{*}K_{n,Z,log}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime*}}
\scriptstyle{g_{*}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}g)_{*}(W_{n}f^{\prime})_{*}K_{n,W,log}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}(W_{n}g^{\prime})_{*}K_{n,W,log}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}_{*}}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y,log}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X,log}.}

1.6.
Étale counterpart
K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

Let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over
k
of dimension
d
.
In this subsection we will use
t={\operatorname{Zar}},{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
to distinguish objects, morphisms on different sites. If
t
is omitted, it means
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
unless otherwise stated.

Denote the structure sheaf on the small étale site
(W_{n}X)_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
by
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
. Denote

(\epsilon_{*},\epsilon^{*}):((W_{n}X)_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})\rightarrow((W_{n}X)_{\operatorname{Zar}},W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X})

the module-theoretic functors.
Recall that every étale
W_{n}X
-scheme is of the form
W_{n}g:W_{n}U\rightarrow W_{n}X
, where
g:U\rightarrow X
is an étale
X
-scheme by
Remark

1.8
(1).
Now let
\mathcal{F}
be a
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
-module on
(W_{n}X)_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
. Consider the following map (cf.
[
30
, p. 264]
)

(1.6.1)

\tau:(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}\mathcal{F}\xrightarrow{}\mathcal{F},

which is defined to be

\displaystyle((W_{n}F_{X})_{*}\mathcal{F})(W_{n}U\xrightarrow{W_{n}g}W_{n}X)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(W_{n}X\times_{W_{n}F_{X},W_{n}X}W_{n}U\xrightarrow{pr_{1}}W_{n}X)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\simeq]{W_{n}F_{U/X}^{*}}\mathcal{F}(W_{n}U\xrightarrow{W_{n}g}W_{n}X)

for any étale map
W_{n}g:W_{n}U\rightarrow W_{n}X
(here we use
pr_{1}
to denote the first projection map of the fiber product). This is an automorphism of
\mathcal{F}
as an abelian étale sheaf, but it changes the
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
-module structure of
\mathcal{F}
.

Define

K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}:=\epsilon^{*}K_{n,X}

to be the complex of étale
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
-modules associated to the Zariski complex
K_{n,X}
of
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules. This is still a complex of quasi-coherent sheaves with coherent cohomologies. For a proper map
f:X\rightarrow Y
of
k
-schemes, define

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}:(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}=\epsilon^{*}((W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X})\xrightarrow{\epsilon^{*}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}}K_{n,Y,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

to be the étale map of
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{Y,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
-modules associated to the Zariski map
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}:K_{n,X}\rightarrow K_{n,X}
of
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules.
Define the Cartier operator
C^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
for étale complexes to be the composite

\displaystyle{C^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}:K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\tau^{-1}}(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}=\epsilon^{*}((W_{n}F_{X})_{*}K_{n,X})\xrightarrow{\epsilon^{*}(\ref{C'_n def})}K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}.

Define

K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}:=\operatorname{Cone}(K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-1}K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})[-1].

We also have the sheaf-level Cartier operator. Let
X
be a smooth
k
-scheme. Recall that by definition,
C_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
is the composition of the inverse of (
1.6.1
) with the module-theoretic etalization of the
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-linear map (
1.3.6
) (it has appeared in
Lemma

1.18
before):

C_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}:W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\tau^{-1}}(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}=\epsilon^{*}((W_{n}F_{X})_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X})\xrightarrow{\epsilon^{*}(\ref{level n cartier def, top degree})}W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}.

Proposition 1.26

(cf.
Theorem

1.9
)
.

C^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
is the natural extension of
C^{\prime}
to the small étale site, i.e.,

\epsilon_{*}{C^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}={C^{\prime}}:K_{n,X}\rightarrow K_{n,X}.

If
X
is smooth,
C_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
is the natural extension of
C
to the small étale site

\epsilon_{*}{C_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}={C}:W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X}.

And one has compatibility

{C_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}=\mathcal{H}^{-d}({C^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}).

Proof.

The first two claims are clear. The last claim follows from the compatibility of
C
and
C^{\prime}
in the Zariski case (
Theorem

1.9
).
∎

Proposition 1.27

(cf.
Proposition

1.15
)
.

Let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over
k
with
k=\overline{k}
. Then

H^{i}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}):=R^{i}\Gamma(W_{n}X,K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-1}H^{i}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})

is surjective for every
i
. In particular,

R^{i}\Gamma(W_{n}X,K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})\simeq H^{i}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})^{C^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-1}.

Proof.

The quasi-coherent descent from the étale site to the Zariski site gives

R\Gamma((W_{n}X)_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})=R\Gamma((W_{n}X)_{{\operatorname{Zar}}},K_{n,X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}).

Taking the
i
-th cohomology groups, the desired surjectivity then follows from the compatibility of
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
(
Proposition

1.26
) and the Zariski case (
Proposition

1.15
).
∎

In the étale topology and for any perfect field
k
, the surjectivity of

C_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-1:W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

is known without the need of
Proposition

1.27
(cf.
Lemma

1.18
).
For the same reasoning as in
Proposition

1.19
, we have

Proposition 1.28

(cf.
Proposition

1.19
)
.

Assume
X
is smooth of pure dimension
d
over a perfect field
k
. Then the natural map

W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}[d]\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes of abelian sheaves.

We go back to the general non-smooth case. The proper pushforward property in the étale setting is very similar to the Zariski case.

Proposition 1.29

(Proper pushforward, cf.
Proposition

1.20
)
.

For
f:X\rightarrow Y
proper, we have a well-defined map of complexes of étale sheaves

(1.6.2)

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}:(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

given by applying
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
termwise.

Proof.

The map
\tau^{-1}
is clearly functorial with respect to any map of abelian sheaves. The rest of the proof goes exactly as in
Proposition

1.20
.
∎

Proposition 1.30

(cf.
Proposition

1.21
)
.

Let
i:X_{0}\hookrightarrow X
be a nilpotent immersion. Then the natural map

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}:(W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,X_{0},log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

This is is a direct consequence of the functoriality of the map
\tau^{-1}
and
Proposition

1.21
.
∎

Let
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion with
j:U\hookrightarrow X
being the open complement as before. Define

\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}(\mathcal{F}):=\operatorname{Ker}(\mathcal{F}\rightarrow j_{*}j^{-1}\mathcal{F})

for any étale abelian sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
X
, just as in the Zariski case (cf. (
1.4.3
)). Replacing
Z
(resp.
X
) by a nilpotent thickening will define the same functor as
\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}(-)
, because the definition of the functor
\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}
only depends on the pair
(X,U)
. Recall that if
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{I}
is an injective
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-sheaf,

0\rightarrow\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}(\mathcal{I})\rightarrow\mathcal{I}\rightarrow j_{*}j^{-1}\mathcal{I}\rightarrow 0

is exact. In fact, because
j_{!}\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
is a subsheaf of the constant sheaf
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
on
X
,
the map
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n},\mathcal{I})\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(j_{!}\mathbb{Z}/p^{n},\mathcal{I})
is surjective.
Since
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(j_{!}\mathbb{Z}/p^{n},\mathcal{I})=\operatorname{Hom}_{U}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n},j^{-1}\mathcal{I})=\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n},j_{*}j^{-1}\mathcal{I})
,
the map
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n},\mathcal{I})\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n},j_{*}j^{-1}\mathcal{I})
is surjective, and hence we have the claim.
This implies that for any complex
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
of étale
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-sheaves with bounded cohomologies,

(1.6.3)

R\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}(\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}\rightarrow j_{*}j^{-1}\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{+1}

is a distinguished triangle in
D^{b}(X,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
(cf. (
1.4.4
)).

Proposition 1.31

(cf.
Proposition

1.22
)
.

Let
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion with open complement
j:U\hookrightarrow X
, as before. Then

(1)

(Purity)
We can identify canonically the functors

(W_{n}i)_{*}=R\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}\circ(W_{n}i)_{*}:D^{b}((W_{n}Z)_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})\rightarrow D^{b}((W_{n}X)_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}).

The composition of this canonical identification with the trace map

(W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,Z,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}=R\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}((W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,Z,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}}R\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}(K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})

is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes of étale
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-sheaves.

(2)

(Localization triangle)

(W_{n}i)_{*}K_{n,Z,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow{}(W_{n}j)_{*}K_{n,U,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow{+1}

is a distinguished triangle in
D^{b}((W_{n}X)_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
.

Proof.

(1)

One only needs to show that
(W_{n}i)_{*}=R\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}\circ(W_{n}i)_{*}
, and then the rest of the proof is the same as in
Proposition

1.22
(1). Let
\mathcal{I}
be an injective étale
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-sheaf on
W_{n}Z
. Since
\operatorname{Hom}_{W_{n}X}(-,(W_{n}i)_{*}\mathcal{I})=\operatorname{Hom}_{W_{n}Z}((W_{n}i)^{-1}(-),\mathcal{I})
and
(W_{n}i)^{-1}
is exact, we know
(W_{n}i)_{*}\mathcal{I}
is an injective abelian sheaf on
(W_{n}X)_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
. This implies that
R(\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}\circ(W_{n}i)_{*})=R\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}\circ(W_{n}i)_{*}
by the Leray spectral sequence, and thus
(W_{n}i)_{*}=R(W_{n}i)_{*}=R(\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}\circ(W_{n}i)_{*})=R\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}\circ(W_{n}i)_{*}
.

(2)

Note that
(W_{n}j)_{*}K_{n,U,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}=R(W_{n}j)_{*}K_{n,U,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
. In fact, the terms of
K_{n,U,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
are quasi-coherent
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
-modules which are
(W_{n}j)_{*}
-acyclic in the étale topology (because
R^{i}f_{*}(\epsilon^{*}\mathcal{F})=\epsilon^{*}(R^{i}f_{*}\mathcal{F})
for any quasi-coherent Zariski sheaf
\mathcal{F}
and any quasi-compact quasi-separated morphism
f

[
39
,
Tag 071N
]
.). Now the first part and the distinguished triangle (
1.6.3
) imply the claim.

∎

2.
Bloch’s cycle complex
\mathbb{Z}_{X,t}^{c}

Let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over
k
of dimension
d
.
Let

\Delta^{i}=\operatorname{Spec}k[T_{0},\dots,T_{i}]/(\sum T_{j}-1).

Define
z_{0}(X,i)
to be the free abelian group generated by closed integral subschemes
Z\subset X\times\Delta^{i}
that intersect all faces properly and
\dim Z=i.

We say two closed subschemes
Z_{1},Z_{2}
of a scheme
Y

intersect properly
if for every irreducible component
W
of the schematic intersection
Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}:=Z_{1}\times_{Y}Z_{2}
, one has

(2.0.1)

\dim W\leq\dim Z_{1}+\dim Z_{2}-\dim Y

(cf.
[
15
, A.1]
).
A subvariety of
X\times\Delta^{i}
is called a
face
if it is determined by some
T_{j_{1}}=T_{j_{2}}=\dots=T_{j_{s}}=0
(
0\leq j_{1}<\dots<j_{s}\leq i
). Note that a face is Zariski locally determined by a regular sequence of
X\times\Delta^{i}
. Therefore the given inequality condition (
2.0.1
) in the definition of
z_{0}(X,i)
is equivalent to the equality condition
[
15
, (53)]
.

The above definition defines a sheaf
z_{0}(-,i)
in both the Zariski and the étale topology on
X
(
[
3
, p.270]
. See also
[
12
, Lemma 3.1]
).
Define the complex of sheaves

\rightarrow z_{0}(-,i)\xrightarrow{d}z_{0}(-,i-1)\rightarrow\dots z_{0}(-,0)\rightarrow 0

with differential map

d(Z)=\sum_{j}(-1)^{j}[Z\cap V(T_{j})].

Here we mean by
V(T_{j})
the closed integral subscheme determined by
T_{j}
and by
[Z\cap V(T_{j})]
the linear combination of the reduced irreducible components of the scheme theoretic intersection
Z\cap V(T_{j})
with coefficients being intersection multiplicities.
z_{0}(X,\bullet)
is then a homological complex concentrated in degree
[0,\infty)
.
Labeling cohomologically, we set

(\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X})^{i}=z_{0}(-,-i).

This complex is nonzero in degrees

(-\infty,0].

Define the higher Chow group

\displaystyle{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,i)

\displaystyle:=H_{i}(z_{0}(X,\bullet))=H^{-i}(\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}(X))

for any
i
.
The higher Chow groups with coefficients in an abelian group
A
will be denoted

\displaystyle{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,i;A)

\displaystyle:=H^{-i}(\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}A).

The complex
\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,t}
, with either
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
or
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
, has the following functoriality properties (cf.
[
3
, Prop. 1.3]
). If
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a proper morphism, then there is a chain map
f_{*}:f_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{Y}
by the pushforward of cycles. If
f:X\rightarrow Y
is a quasi-finite flat morphism, then there is a chain map
f^{*}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{Y}\rightarrow f_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}
by the pullback of cycles.

3.
Kato’s complex of Milnor
K
-theory
{C^{M}_{X,t}}

Recall that given a field
L
, the
q
-th Milnor
K
-group
K^{M}_{q}(L)
of
L
is defined to be the
q
-th graded piece of the graded commutative ring

\bigoplus_{q\geq 0}K^{M}_{q}(L)=\frac{\bigoplus_{q\geq 0}(L^{*})^{\otimes q}}{(a\otimes(1-a)\mid a,1-a\in L^{*})},

where
(a\otimes(1-a)\mid a,1-a\in L^{*})
denotes the two-sided ideal of the graded commutative ring
\bigoplus_{q\geq 0}(L^{*})^{\otimes q}
generated by elements of the form
a\otimes(1-a)
with
a,1-a\in L^{*}
.
The image of an element
a_{1}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{q}\in(L^{*})^{\otimes q}
in
K^{M}_{q}(L)
is denoted by
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{q}\}
.

If
L
is a discrete valuation field with valuation
v
and residue field
k(v)
,
the group homomorphism

\partial_{v}:K^{M}_{q}(L)\rightarrow K^{M}_{q-1}(k(v)),\quad\partial_{v}(\{\pi_{v},u_{1},\dots,u_{q-1}\})=\{\overline{u}_{1},\dots,\overline{u}_{q-1}\}

is called the map of the
tame symbol
.
Here
\pi_{v}
is a local parameter with respect to
v
,
u_{1},\dots u_{q-1}
are units in the valuation ring of
v
, and
\overline{u}_{1},\dots,\overline{u}_{q-1}
are the images of
u_{1},\dots u_{q-1}
in the residue field
k(v)
. This is consistent with the sign convention in
[
38
, p.328]
.

For every natural number
q
and every finite field extension
L^{\prime}/L
, there exists a unique group homomorphism

\operatorname{Nm}_{L^{\prime}/L}:K^{M}_{q}(L^{\prime})\rightarrow K^{M}_{q}(L)

such that

(1)

For any field extensions
L\subset L^{\prime}\subset L^{\prime\prime}
, one has
\operatorname{Nm}_{L/L}=id
and
\operatorname{Nm}_{L^{\prime}/L}\circ\operatorname{Nm}_{L^{\prime\prime}/L^{\prime}}=\operatorname{Nm}_{L^{\prime\prime}/L}
;

(2)

Let
L(T)
be the function field of
\mathbf{A}^{1}_{k}
. For every
x\in K^{M}_{q}(L(T))
one has

\sum_{v}\operatorname{Nm}_{L(v)/L}(\partial_{v}(x))=0,

where
v
runs over all discrete valuations of
L(T)
, and
L(v)
denotes the residue field at valuation
v
.

The map
\operatorname{Nm}_{L^{\prime}/L}
is called the
norm map
associated to the finite field extension
L^{\prime}/L
.

Recall the definition of a Milnor
K
-sheaf on a point
X=\operatorname{Spec}L
, where
L
is any field.

\mathcal{K}^{M}_{\operatorname{Spec}L,q,{\operatorname{Zar}}}
is the constant sheaf associated to the abelian group
K^{M}_{q}(L)
(without the assumption that
L
is an infinite field, cf.
[
32
, Prop. 10(4)]
), and
\mathcal{K}^{M}_{\operatorname{Spec}L,q,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
is the étale sheaf associated to the presheaf

L^{\prime}\mapsto K^{M}_{q}(L^{\prime});\quad L^{\prime}/L\text{ finite separable.}

Choose a separable closure
L^{\mathrm{sep}}
of
L
. Then the geometric stalk at the geometric point
\operatorname{Spec}L^{\mathrm{sep}}
over
\operatorname{Spec}L
is
\operatorname{colim}_{L\subset L^{\prime}\subset L^{\mathrm{sep}}}K^{M}_{q}(L^{\prime})
, which is equal to
K^{M}_{q}(L^{\mathrm{sep}})
because the filtered colimit commutes with the tensor product and the quotient. Now by Galois descent of the étale sheaf condition, the sheaf
\mathcal{K}^{M}_{\operatorname{Spec}L,q,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
is precisely

L^{\prime}\mapsto K^{M}_{q}(L^{\mathrm{sep}})^{\operatorname{Gal}(L^{\mathrm{sep}}/L^{\prime})};\quad L^{\prime}/L\text{ finite separable.}

Here the Galois action is given on each factor.

Let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over
k
of dimension
d
.
Now with the topology
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
or
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
, we have the corresponding Gersten complex of Milnor
K
-theory, denote by
C^{M}_{X,t}
(the differentials
d^{M}
will be introduced below):

(3.0.1)

\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(d)}}\iota_{x*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,d,t}\xrightarrow{d^{M}}\dots\xrightarrow{d^{M}}\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(1)}}\iota_{x*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,1,t}\xrightarrow{d^{M}}\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(0)}}\iota_{x*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,0,t},

where
\iota_{x}:\operatorname{Spec}k(x)\hookrightarrow X
the natural inclusion map.
As part of the convention,

(C^{M}_{X,t})^{i}=\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(-i)}}\iota_{x,*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,-i,t}.

In other words, (
3.0.1
) sits in degrees

[-d,0].

It remains to introduce the differential maps.

If
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
, the differential map
d^{M}
in (
3.0.1
) is defined in the following way.
Let
x\in X_{(q)}
be a dimension
q
point, and
\rho:X^{\prime}\rightarrow\overline{\{x\}}
be the normalization of
\overline{\{x\}}
with generic point
x^{\prime}
. Define

(d^{M})^{x}_{y}:K^{M}_{q}(x)=K^{M}_{q}(x^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sum\partial^{x^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}}\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}|y}K^{M}_{q-1}(y^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\sum\operatorname{Nm}_{y^{\prime}/y}}K^{M}_{q-1}(y).

Here we have used the shortened symbol

K^{M}_{q}(x):=K^{M}_{q}(k(x)).

The notation
y^{\prime}|y
means that
y^{\prime}\in{X^{\prime}}^{(1)}
is in the fiber of
y
.

(3.0.2)

\partial^{x^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}:K^{M}_{q}(x^{\prime})\rightarrow K^{M}_{q-1}(y^{\prime})

is the Milnor tame symbol of the discrete valuation field
k(x^{\prime})
with valuation defined by
y^{\prime}
. And

(3.0.3)

\operatorname{Nm}_{y^{\prime}/y}:K^{M}_{q-1}(y^{\prime})\rightarrow K^{M}_{q-1}(y)

is the Milnor norm map of the finite field extension
k(y)\subset k(y^{\prime})
.
The differential
d^{M}
of this complex is given by

d^{M}:=\sum_{x\in X_{(q)}}\sum_{y\in X_{(q-1)}\cap\overline{\{x\}}}(d^{M})^{x}_{y}:\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(q)}}K^{M}_{q}(x)\rightarrow\bigoplus_{y\in X_{(q-1)}}K^{M}_{q-1}(y).

If
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
, set
x\in X_{(q)}
,
y\in X_{(q-1)}\cap\overline{\{x\}}
. Denote by
\rho:X^{\prime}\rightarrow\overline{\{x\}}
the normalization map and denote by
x^{\prime}
the generic point of
X^{\prime}
. One can canonically identify the étale abelian sheaves

\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,q,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
and
\rho_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x^{\prime},q,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

on
\overline{\{x\}}
(here
\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,q,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
on
\overline{\{x\}}
means the pushforward of the sheaf
\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,q,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
on the point
\operatorname{Spec}k(x)
via
\operatorname{Spec}k(x)\rightarrow\overline{\{x\}}
),
and thus identify

\iota_{x,*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,q,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
and
\iota_{x,*}\rho_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x^{\prime},q,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

on
X
.
Let
y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime(1)}
such that
\rho(y^{\prime})=y
. Then the componentwise differential map

(d^{M})^{x}_{y}:\iota_{x,*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,q,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow\iota_{y,*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{y,q-1,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

is defined to be the composition

(d^{M})^{x}_{y}=\iota_{y,*}(\operatorname{Nm})\circ\rho_{*}(\partial).

Here
\partial:=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime(1)}\cap\rho^{-1}(y)}\partial^{x^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}
, where

(3.0.4)

\partial^{x^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}:\iota_{x^{\prime},*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x^{\prime},q,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow\iota_{y^{\prime},*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{y^{\prime},q-1,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

on
X^{\prime}
is defined to be the sheafification of the tame symbol on the presheaf level. Indeed, the tame symbol is a map of étale presheaves by
[
38
, R3a]
.
And
\operatorname{Nm}:=\sum_{y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime(1)}\cap\rho^{-1}(y)}\operatorname{Nm}_{y^{\prime}/y}
, where

(3.0.5)

\operatorname{Nm}_{y^{\prime}/y}:\rho_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{y^{\prime},q-1,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}^{M}_{y,q-1,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

on
y
is defined to be the sheafification of the norm map on the presheaf level. The norm map is a map of étale presheaves by
[
38
, R1c]
.

The complex
C^{M}_{X,t}
, either
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
or
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
, is covariant for proper morphisms and contravariant for quasi-finite flat morphisms (
[
38
, (4.6)(1)(2)]
). The pushforward map associated to a proper morphism is induced by the Milnor norm map, and the pullback map associated to a quasi-finite flat morphism is induced by the pullback map of the structure sheaves.

4.
Kato-Moser’s complex of logarithmic de Rham-Witt sheaves
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}

Kato first defined the Gersten complex of the logarithmic de Rham-Witt sheaves in
[
28
, §1]
. Moser in
[
36
, (1.3)-(1.5)]
sheafified Kato’s construction on the étale site and studied its dualizing properties. We will adopt here the sign conventions in
[
38
]
.

Let
Y
be a
k
-scheme. Let
q\in\mathbb{N}
be an integer. Recall that in
Section

1.3.5
, we have defined
W_{n}\Omega_{Y,log,t}^{q}
, with either
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
or
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
, to be the abelian subsheaf of
W_{n}\Omega_{Y,t}^{q}
étale locally generated by log forms.
We will freely use
W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{L,log,t}
for
W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{\operatorname{Spec}L,log,t}
below.

Now let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over
k
of dimension
d
. Define the Gersten complex
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}
, in the topology
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
or
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
, to be the complex of
t
-sheaves isomorphic to
C^{M}_{X,t}/p^{n}
via the Bloch-Gabber-Kato isomorphism
[
4
, 2.8]
:

(4.0.1)

0\rightarrow\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(d)}}\iota_{x,*}W_{n}\Omega_{k(x),log,t}^{d}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(1)}}\iota_{x*}W_{n}\Omega_{k(x),log}^{1}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(0)}}\iota_{x,*}W_{n}\Omega_{k(x),log,t}^{0}\rightarrow 0.

Here
\iota_{x}:\operatorname{Spec}k(x)\rightarrow X
is the natural map. We will still denote by
\partial
the reduction of the tame symbol
\partial
mod
p^{n}
(cf. (
3.0.2
)(
3.0.4
)), but denote by
\mathrm{tr}
the reduction of Milnor’s norm
\operatorname{Nm}
mod
p^{n}
(cf. (
3.0.3
)(
3.0.5
)). The reason for the later notation will be clear from
Lemma

5.3
. As part of the convention,

\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}^{i}=\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(-i)}}\iota_{x*}W_{n}\Omega_{k(x),log,t}^{-i},

i.e.
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X}
is concentrated in degrees

[-d,0].

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion with
j:U\hookrightarrow X
its open complement.
We have the following short exact sequence for
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{i_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,Z,{\operatorname{Zar}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,U,{\operatorname{Zar}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

For
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
, one has the localization triangle

i_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,Z,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow Rj_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,U,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow{+1}.

Proof.

\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}
is a complex of flasque sheaves (therefore
Rj_{*}(\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{Zar}}})=j_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}
), and one has the sequence being short exact in this case.
If
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
, the purity theorem holds
[
36
, Corollary on p.130]
, i.e.,
i_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,Z,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}=\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}(\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})\xrightarrow{\simeq}R\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}(\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})
.
We are done with the help of the distinguished triangle (
1.6.3
) in the étale topology.
∎

Functoriality of
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}
is the same as that of
C^{M}_{X,t}
via
d\log
. We omit the statement.

Part II
The maps

5.
Construction of the chain map
\zeta_{n,X,log,t}:{C^{M}_{X,t}}\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X,log,t}

5.1.
Construction of the chain map
\zeta_{n,X,t}:{C^{M}_{X,t}}\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X,t}

Let
x\in X_{(q)}
be a dimension
q
point.
\iota_{x}:\operatorname{Spec}k(x)\rightarrow X
is the canonical map and
i_{x}:\overline{\{x\}}\hookrightarrow X
the closed immersion.
At degree
i=-q
, and over a point
x
, we define the degree
i
map to be
\zeta_{n,X,t}^{i}:=\sum_{x\in X_{(q)}}\zeta_{n,x,t}^{i}
, with

(5.1.1)

\displaystyle\zeta_{n,x,t}^{i}:(W_{n}\iota_{x})_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,q,t}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{d\log}(W_{n}\iota_{x})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{k(x),log,t}^{q}\subset(W_{n}\iota_{x})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{k(x),t}^{q}

\displaystyle=(W_{n}i_{x})_{*}K_{n,\overline{\{x\}},t}^{i}\xrightarrow{(-1)^{i}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i_{x}}}K_{n,X,t}^{i}.

We will use freely the notation
\zeta_{n,X,t}^{i}
with some of its subscript or superscript dropped.

It is worth noticing that all the maps of étale sheaves involved here are given by the sheafification of the respective Zariski maps on the étale presheaf level. So to check commutativity of a composition of such maps between étale sheaves, it suffices to check on the
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
level. Keeping the convention as before, we usually omit the subscript
{\operatorname{Zar}}
if we are working with the Zariski topology.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over
k
with
k
being a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
. For
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
and
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
, the map

\zeta_{n,X,t}:{C^{M}_{X,t}}\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X,t},

as defined termwise in (
5.1.1
), is a chain map of complexes of sheaves on the site
(W_{n}X)_{t}
.

Note that we have a canonical identification
(W_{n}X)_{t}=X_{t}
for both
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
and
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
. We use
(W_{n}X)_{t}
just for the convenience of describing the
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-structure of residual complexes appearing later.

Proof.

To check
\zeta_{n,X,t}
is a map of complexes, it suffices to check that the diagram

\textstyle{(C^{M}_{X,t})^{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{M}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{n,X,t}^{i}}
\textstyle{(C^{M}_{X,t})^{i+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{n,X,t}^{i+1}}
\textstyle{(K_{n,X,t})^{i}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{X}}
\textstyle{(K_{n,X,t}^{i+1})^{i+1}}

commutes for
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
. To this end, it suffices to show: for each
x\in X_{(q)}
, and
y\in X_{(q-1)}
which is a specialization of
x
, the diagram

(5.1.2)

\textstyle{(W_{n}\iota_{x})_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(d_{X}^{M})^{x}_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{n,x}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}\iota_{y})_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{y,q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{n,y}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}i_{y,x})_{*}K_{n,\overline{\{y\}}}^{-q+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i_{y,x}}}
\textstyle{K_{n,\overline{\{x\}}}^{-q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{\overline{\{x\}}}}
\textstyle{K_{n,\overline{\{x\}}}^{-q+1}}

commutes. Here
i_{y,x}:\overline{\{y\}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\{x\}}
denotes the canonical closed immersion.

Since the definition of the differential maps in
{C^{M}_{X}}
involves normalization, consider the normalization
\rho:X^{\prime}\rightarrow\overline{\{x\}}
of
\overline{\{x\}}
, and form the cartesian square

\textstyle{\overline{\{y\}}\times_{\overline{\{x\}}}X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}=\overline{\{x^{\prime}\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\overline{\{y\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{y,x}}
\textstyle{\overline{\{x\}}.}

Denote the generic point of
X^{\prime}
by
x^{\prime}
.
Suppose
y^{\prime}
is one of the generic points of the irreducible components of
\overline{\{y\}}\times_{\overline{\{x\}}}X^{\prime}
, and denote by
Y^{\prime}
the irreducible component corresponding to
y^{\prime}
.
In particular,
y^{\prime}
is a codimension 1 point in the normal scheme
X^{\prime}
,
thus is regular. Because the base field
k
is perfect,
y^{\prime}
is also a smooth point in
X^{\prime}
. According to
Remark

1.4
, the degree
[-q,-q+1]
terms of
K_{n,X^{\prime}}
are of the form

(W_{n}\iota_{x^{\prime}})_{*}H^{0}_{x^{\prime}}(W_{n}\Omega_{X^{\prime}}^{q})\xrightarrow{\delta}\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}_{(q-1)}}(W_{n}\iota_{y^{\prime}})_{*}H^{1}_{y^{\prime}}(W_{n}\Omega_{X^{\prime}}^{q})\xrightarrow{}\dots,

where
\delta
denotes the differential map of the residual complex
K_{n,X}
.
After localizing at a single
y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime(1)}
in the Zariski sense, one gets

(W_{n}\iota_{x^{\prime}})_{*}H^{0}_{x^{\prime}}(W_{n}\Omega_{X^{\prime}}^{q})\xrightarrow{\delta_{y^{\prime}}}(W_{n}\iota_{y^{\prime}})_{*}H^{1}_{y^{\prime}}(W_{n}\Omega_{X^{\prime}}^{q})\xrightarrow{}\dots.

Consider the following diagrams.
Write
\iota_{x^{\prime}}:\operatorname{Spec}k(x^{\prime})\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}
,
\iota_{y^{\prime}}:\operatorname{Spec}k(y^{\prime})\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}
the inclusions,
i_{y^{\prime},x^{\prime}}:Y^{\prime}=\overline{\{y^{\prime}\}}\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}
