the closed immersion, we have a diagram

(5.1.3)

\textstyle{(W_{n}\iota_{x^{\prime}})_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x^{\prime},q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial^{x^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{(W_{n}\iota_{y^{\prime}})_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{y^{\prime},q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{(W_{n}\iota_{x^{\prime}})_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{k(x^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{y^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}\iota_{y^{\prime}})_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{q-1}_{k(y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}(i_{y^{\prime},x^{\prime}})}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}\iota_{y^{\prime}})_{*}H_{y^{\prime}}^{1}(W_{n}\Omega_{X^{\prime}}^{q}).}

For any
y^{\prime}\in\rho^{-1}(y)\subset X^{\prime(1)}
, we have a diagram

(5.1.4)

\textstyle{(W_{n}\rho)_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{y^{\prime},q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Nm}_{y^{\prime}/y}}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}^{M}_{y,q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{(W_{n}\rho)_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{q-1}_{k(y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\rho}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega^{q-1}_{k(y)}.}

Write
i_{y^{\prime},x^{\prime}}:Y^{\prime}=\overline{\{y^{\prime}\}}\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}
,
i_{y,x}:\overline{\{y\}}\hookrightarrow\overline{\{x\}}
, we have a diagram

(5.1.5)

\textstyle{(W_{n}\rho)_{*}(W_{n}\iota_{y^{\prime}})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{k(y^{\prime})}^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}(i_{y^{\prime},x^{\prime}})}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}\iota_{y})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{k(y)}^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}(i_{y,x})}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}\rho)_{*}(W_{n}\iota_{y^{\prime}})_{*}H_{y^{\prime}}^{1}(W_{n}\Omega_{X^{\prime}}^{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\rho}}
\textstyle{K_{n,\overline{\{x\}}}^{-(q-1)},}

and a diagram

(5.1.6)

\textstyle{(W_{n}\iota_{x^{\prime}})_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{k(x^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{X^{\prime}}=\sum\delta_{y^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}\in\rho^{-1}(y)}(W_{n}\iota_{y^{\prime}})_{*}H^{1}_{y^{\prime}}(W_{n}\Omega^{q-1}_{X^{\prime}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\rho}}
\textstyle{K_{n,\overline{\{x\}}}^{-q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{\overline{\{x\}}}}
\textstyle{K_{n,\overline{\{x\}}}^{-(q-1)}.}

All the trace maps above are trace maps of residual complexes at a certain degree.
(
5.1.5
) is the degree
q-1
part of the diagram

\textstyle{(W_{n}\rho)_{*}(W_{n}i_{y^{\prime},x^{\prime}})_{*}K_{n,Y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}(i_{y^{\prime},x^{\prime}})}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}i_{y,x})_{*}K_{n,\overline{\{y\}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}(i_{y,x})}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}\rho)_{*}K_{n,X^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\rho}}
\textstyle{K_{n,\overline{\{x\}}}}

(the trace map on top is the trace map of the restriction of
W_{n}\rho
to
W_{n}Y^{\prime}
),
and thus is commutative by the functoriality of the Grothendieck trace map with respect to composition of morphisms (
Proposition

1.2
(4)).
(
5.1.6
) is simply the degree
-q
to
-q+1
terms of the trace map
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\rho}:(W_{n}\rho)_{*}K_{n,X^{\prime}}\rightarrow K_{n,\overline{\{x\}}}
, thus is also commutative.
It remains to check the commutativity of (
5.1.3
) and (
5.1.4
). And these are
Lemma

5.2
and
Lemma

5.3
.

One notices that diagram (
5.1.2
) decomposes into the four diagrams (
5.1.3
)-(
5.1.6
):

\textstyle{(W_{n}\rho)_{*}(W_{n}\iota_{x^{\prime}})_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x^{\prime},q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}|y}\partial^{x^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}|y}(W_{n}\rho)_{*}(W_{n}\iota_{y^{\prime}})_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{y^{\prime},q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{y^{\prime}/y}\operatorname{Nm}_{y^{\prime}/y}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}\iota_{y})_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{y,q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{(W_{n}\rho)_{*}(W_{n}\iota_{x^{\prime}})_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{k(x^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\rho}}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{circle 2,3})}
\scriptstyle{\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}|y}\delta_{y^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}|y}(W_{n}\rho)_{*}(W_{n}\iota_{y^{\prime}})_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{q-1}_{k(y^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}|y}-\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}(i_{y^{\prime},x^{\prime}})}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\rho}}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{circle 4})}
\textstyle{(W_{n}\iota_{y})_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{q-1}_{k(y)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}(i_{y,x})}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}|y}(W_{n}\rho)_{*}(W_{n}\iota_{y^{\prime}})_{*}H_{y^{\prime}}^{1}(W_{n}\Omega_{X^{\prime}}^{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\rho}}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{circle 6})}
\textstyle{K_{n,\overline{\{x\}}}^{-(q-1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K_{n,\overline{\{x\}}}^{-q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{\overline{\{x\}}}}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{circle 5})}
\textstyle{K_{n,\overline{\{x\}}}^{-(q-1)}}

Here by symbol
y^{\prime}|y
we mean that
y^{\prime}\in\rho^{-1}(y)
. Notice that we have added a minus sign to both vertical arrows of (
5.1.5
) in the corresponding square above, but this does not affect its commutativity.
Since one can canonically identify

(W_{n}\rho)_{*}(W_{n}\iota_{x^{\prime}})_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x^{\prime},q}\text{\quad with \quad}(W_{n}\iota_{x})_{*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,q},

to show the commutativity of the diagram (
5.1.2
), it only remains to show
Lemma

5.2
and
Lemma

5.3
.
∎

Lemma 5.2
.

For an integral normal scheme
X^{\prime}
, with
x^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
being the generic point and
y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime(1)}
being a codimension 1 point, the diagram (
5.1.3
) is commutative.

Proof.

Given a
y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime(1)}
lying over
y
,
the abelian group
K^{M}_{q}(x^{\prime})
is generated by

\{\pi^{\prime},u_{1},\dots,u_{q-1}\}\text{ and }\{v_{1},\dots,v_{q-1},v_{q}\},

where
u_{1},\dots,u_{q-1},v_{1},\dots,v_{q-1},v_{q}\in\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime},y^{\prime}}^{*}
, and
\pi^{\prime}
is a chosen uniformizer of the discrete valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime},y^{\prime}}
. It suffices to check the commutativity for these generators.

In the first case, the left-bottom composition gives

\displaystyle(\delta_{y^{\prime}}\circ d\log)(\{\pi^{\prime},u_{1},\dots,u_{q-1}\})

\displaystyle=\delta_{y^{\prime}}(d\log[\pi^{\prime}]_{n}d\log[u_{1}]_{n}\dots d\log[u_{q-1}]_{n})

\displaystyle=\left[\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-7.69838pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces
$d[\pi^{\prime}]_{n}d\log[u_{1}]_{n}\dots d\log[u_{q-1}]_{n}$}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$[\pi^{\prime}]_{n}$
}\crcr}}}}\right].}}

The last equality above is given by
[
6
, A.1.2]
.
Here we have used the fact that
[\pi^{\prime}]
is a regular element in
W_{n}X^{\prime}
, since
\pi^{\prime}
is regular in
X^{\prime}
.
The top-right composition gives

\displaystyle(-\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}(i_{y^{\prime},x^{\prime}})}\circ

\displaystyle d\log\circ\partial^{x^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}})(\{\pi^{\prime},u_{1},\dots,u_{q-1}\})

\displaystyle=(-\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}(i_{y^{\prime},x^{\prime}})}\circ d\log)\{\overline{u}_{1},\dots,\overline{u}_{q-1}\}

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}(i_{y^{\prime},x^{\prime}})}(d\log[\overline{u}_{1}]_{n}\dots d\log[\overline{u}_{q-1}]_{n})

\displaystyle=\left[\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-7.69838pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces
$d[\pi^{\prime}]_{n}d\log[\overline{u}_{1}]_{n}\dots d\log[\overline{u}_{q-1}]_{n}$}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$[\pi^{\prime}]_{n}$
}\crcr}}}}\right].}}

The last equality is given by
[
6
, A.2.12]
.
So the diagram (
5.1.3
) is commutative in this case.

In the second case, since
\partial^{x^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}(\{v_{1},\dots,v_{q}\})=0
, we need to check the left-bottom composite also gives zero. In fact,

\displaystyle(\delta_{y^{\prime}}\circ d\log)(\{v_{1},\dots,v_{q}\})

\displaystyle=\delta_{y^{\prime}}(d\log[v_{1}]_{n}\dots d\log[v_{q}]_{n})

\displaystyle=\left[\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-7.69838pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces
$[\pi^{\prime}]_{n}\cdot d\log[v_{1}]_{n}\dots d\log[v_{q}]_{n}$}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces$[\pi^{\prime}]_{n}$
}\crcr}}}}\right]}}

\displaystyle=0.

The second equality is due to
[
6
, A.1.2]
. The last equality is because, in a small neighborhood
V
of
y^{\prime}
, the element
[\pi^{\prime}]_{n}\cdot d\log[v_{1}]_{n}\dots d\log[v_{q}]_{n}\in W_{n}\Omega_{V}^{q}
lies in the
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{V}
-submodule
[\pi^{\prime}]_{n}\cdot W_{n}\Omega_{V}^{q}
.
∎

Lemma 5.3

(Compatibility of Milnor norm and Grothendieck trace)
.

Let
F/E
be a finite field extension with both fields
E
and
F
being of transcendence degree
q-1
over
k
. Suppose there exists a finite morphism
g
between integral separated finite type
k
-schemes, such that
F
is the function field of the source of
g
and
E
is the function field of the target of
g
, and the field extension
F/E
is induced via the map
g
. Then the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{K^{M}_{q-1}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Nm}_{F/E}}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{q-1}(E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{F}^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{E}^{q-1}.}

Here the norm map
\operatorname{Nm}_{F/E}
denotes the norm map from Milnor
K
-theory, and
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}
denotes the Grothendieck trace map associated to the finite morphism
g
.

Remark 5.4
.

The compatibility of the trace map with the norm and the pushforward of cycles in various settings has been known by the experts, and many definitions and properties of the trace map in the literature reflect this viewpoint.
But since we have not found a proof of the compatibility of the Milnor norm with the trace map defined via the Grothendieck duality theory, we include a proof here.

Proof.

We start the proof by some reductions.
Since both
\operatorname{Nm}_{F/E}
and
\operatorname{Tr}_{F/E}
are independent of the choice of towers of simple field extensions, without loss of generality, one can suppose
F
is a finite simple field extension over
E
. Now
F=E(a)=\frac{E[T]}{f(T)}
for some monic irreducible polynomial
f(T)\in E[T]
with
a\in F
being one of its roots. This realizes
\operatorname{Spec}F
as an
F
-valued point
P
of
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}
, namely,

\textstyle{\operatorname{Spec}F=P\,}
\scriptstyle{i_{P}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Spec}E.}

All the three morphisms on above are morphisms of finite type (although not between schemes of finite type over
k
), so it makes sense to talk about the associated trace maps for residual complexes.
But for the particular residual complexes we are interested in, we need to enlarge the schemes involved to schemes of finite type over
k
, while preserving the morphism classes (e.g., closed immersion, smooth morphism, etc) of the morphisms between them.

To this end, take
Y
to be any separated smooth connected scheme of finite type over
k
with
E
being the function field.
Since
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}
is the generic fiber of
Y\times_{k}\mathbf{P}^{1}_{k}
,
by possibly shrinking
Y
to an affine neighborhood
\operatorname{Spec}B
of
pr_{1}(P)
(here
pr_{1}:Y\times_{k}\mathbf{P}^{1}_{k}\rightarrow Y
is the first projection map) one can extend the above diagram to the following:

\textstyle{\operatorname{Spec}F\in W}
\scriptstyle{i_{W}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{Y=\operatorname{Spec}B\ni\operatorname{Spec}E.}

Here
W:=\overline{\{P\}}^{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{Y}}
is the closure of the point
P
in
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{Y}
. This is a commutative diagram of finite type
k
-schemes.
In particular, it makes sense to talk about the residual complexes
K_{n,Y},K_{n,W}
and
K_{n,\mathbf{P}^{1}_{Y}}
.

Now it remains to show the commutativity of the following diagram

(5.1.7)

\textstyle{K^{M}_{q-1}(E(a))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Nm}_{E(a)/E}}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{q-1}(E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{E(a)}^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{E}^{q-1},}

where
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}
denotes the trace map for residual complexes
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}:(W_{n}g)_{*}K_{n,W}\rightarrow K_{n,Y}
at degree
-(q-1)
.

We do induction on
[E(a):E]
.
If
[E(a):E]=1
, then both the Grothendieck trace
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}:W_{n}\Omega^{q-1}_{E(a)/k}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega^{q-1}_{E/k}
and the norm map
\operatorname{Nm}_{E(a)/E}:K^{M}_{q-1}(E(a))\rightarrow K^{M}_{q-1}(E)
are the identity, therefore the claim holds. Now the induction step. Suppose the diagram (
5.1.7
) commutes for
[E(a):E]\leq r-1
. We will need to prove the commutativity for
[E(a):E]=r
.

First note that
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}:(W_{n}g)_{*}K_{n,W}\rightarrow K_{n,Y}

naturally decomposes into

(5.1.8)

(W_{n}g)_{*}K_{n,W}\xrightarrow{(W_{n}\pi)_{*}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i_{P}W}}(W_{n}\pi)_{*}K_{n,\mathbf{P}^{1}_{Y}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\pi}}K_{n,Y}.

by
Proposition

1.2
(4).

H^{1}_{P}(W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{Y}})
is a direct summand of the degree
-(q-1)
part of
K_{n,\mathbf{P}^{1}_{Y}}
. One can canonically identify

(5.1.9)

H^{1}_{P}(W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{Y}})=H^{1}_{P}(W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}}),

via pulling back along the natural map
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{P}^{1}_{Y}
.
Thus on degree
-(q-1)
and at the point
P
, the map (
5.1.8
) is canonically identified with

W_{n}\Omega_{E(a)}^{q-1}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i_{P}W}}H^{1}_{P}(W_{n}\Omega_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}}^{q})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\pi}}W_{n}\Omega_{E}^{q-1}.

Consider the diagram

\textstyle{K^{M}_{q}(E(T))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{P}}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{q-1}(E(a))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Nm}_{E(a)/E}}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{q-1}(E)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{E(a)}^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i_{P}W}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{E(T)}^{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{P}}
\textstyle{H^{1}_{P}(W_{n}\Omega_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}}^{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\pi}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{E}^{q-1}.}

We have used the identification (
5.1.9
) in this diagram. We have seen that the left square is commutative up to sign
-1
, as a special case of
Lemma

5.2
(i.e. take normal scheme
X^{\prime}=\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}
and
y^{\prime}:=P=\operatorname{Spec}F
).
Since
\partial_{P}
is surjective, to show the commutativity of the trapezoid on the right, it suffices to show that the composite square is commutative up to
-1
.
For any element

s:=\{s_{1},\dots,s_{q-1}\}\in K^{M}_{q-1}(E(a)),

one can always find a lift

\widetilde{s}:=\{f,\widetilde{s}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{s}_{q-1}\}\in K^{M}_{q}(E(T)),

such that each of the
s_{i}=s_{i}(T)
is a polynomial of degree
\leq r-1

(e.g. decompose
E(a)
as a
r
-dimensional
E
-vector space
E(a)=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{r-1}Ea^{j}
and suppose
s_{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}b_{i,j}a^{j}
with
b_{i,j}\in E
, then
\widetilde{s}_{i}=\widetilde{s}_{i}(T)=\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}b_{i,j}T^{j}
satisfies the condition),
and
\partial_{P}(\widetilde{s})=s
. Denote by

y_{i,1},\dots,y_{i,a_{i}}\quad(1\leq i\leq q-1)

the closed points of
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}
corresponding to the irreducible factors of the polynomials
\widetilde{s}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{s}_{q-1}
. Note that the local section
\widetilde{s}_{i,l}
cutting out
y_{i,l}
is by definition an irreducible factor of
\widetilde{s}_{i}
, and therefore
\deg\widetilde{s}_{i,l}<r
for all
i
and all
l
.

We claim that

(5.1.10)

\sum_{y\in(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E})_{(0)}}(\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\pi})_{y}\circ\delta_{y}=0:W_{n}\Omega_{E(T)/k}^{q}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{E/k}^{q-1}.

In fact,

(5.1.11)

0\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}}^{q}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{E(T)}^{q}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{y\in(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E})_{(0)}}(W_{n}\iota_{y})_{*}H^{1}_{y}(W_{n}\Omega_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}}^{q})\rightarrow 0

is an exact sequence
[
7
, 1.5.9]
, where
\iota_{y}:y\hookrightarrow\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}
is the natural inclusion of the point
y
. Taking the long exact sequence with respect to the global section functor, one arrives at the following diagram with the row being a complex

\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{E(T)}^{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{y\in(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E})_{(0)}}H^{1}_{y}(W_{n}\Omega_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}}^{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sum_{y}(\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\pi})_{y}}
\textstyle{H^{1}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E},W_{n}\Omega_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}}^{q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\pi}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{E/k}^{q-1}.}

The trace maps on left of the above are induced from the degree
0
part of
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\pi}:(W_{n}\pi)_{*}K_{n,\mathbf{P}^{1}_{Y}}\rightarrow K_{n,Y}
.
The trace map on the right of the above is induced also by
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\pi}:(W_{n}\pi)_{*}K_{n,\mathbf{P}^{1}_{Y}}\rightarrow K_{n,Y}
, while the global cohomology group is calculated via (
5.1.11
),
i.e., one uses the last two terms of (
5.1.11
) as an injective resolution of the sheaf
W_{n}\Omega_{\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E}}^{q}
, and then
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\pi}:(W_{n}\pi)_{*}K_{n,\mathbf{P}^{1}_{Y}}\rightarrow K_{n,Y}
induces the map of complexes (sitting in degrees
[-1,0]
) on global sections,
and then the map of cohomologies on degree
0
gives our trace map
H^{1}(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{E},W_{n}\Omega_{E}^{q})\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega^{q-1}_{E}
on the right.
From the construction of these trace maps, the diagram on above is by definition commutative. Therefore (
5.1.10
) holds.

One notices that
\delta_{y}\circ d\log(\widetilde{s})=0
unless
y\in\{p,y_{1,1},\dots,y_{q-1,a_{q-1}},\infty\}
. Now we calculate

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}

\displaystyle\circ d\log)(s)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}g}\circ d\log\circ\partial_{P})(\widetilde{s})

\displaystyle=-((\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\pi})_{P}\circ\delta_{P}\circ d\log)(\widetilde{s})\quad\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{Compatibility of the Tame symbol and the residue symbols zhiqiande lemma})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{y\in\{y_{1,1},\dots,y_{q-1,a_{q-1}},\infty\}}((\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\pi})_{y}\circ\delta_{y}\circ d\log)(\widetilde{s})\text{\quad(\ref{sum is zero delta_P circ Tr})}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{y\in\{y_{1,1},\dots,y_{q-1,a_{q-1}},\infty\}}(d\log\circ\operatorname{Nm}_{E(k(y))/E}\circ\partial_{y})(\widetilde{s})

(induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle=(d\log\circ\operatorname{Nm}_{E(a)/E}\circ\partial_{P})(\widetilde{s})\text{\quad(\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Rost-ChowCoeff}{}{}, 2.2 (RC)]})}

\displaystyle=(d\log\circ\operatorname{Nm}_{E(a)/E})(s).

This finishes the induction.
∎

5.2.
Functoriality of
\zeta_{n,X,t}:{C^{M}_{X,t}}\rightarrow K_{n,X,t}

Let
k
denote a perfect field of positive characteristic
p
.

Proposition 5.5

(Proper pushforward)
.

\zeta
is compatible with proper pushforward. I.e., for
f:X\rightarrow Y
a proper map, the following diagram is commutative

\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}{C^{M}_{X,t}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{n,X,t}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{C_{Y,t}^{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{n,Y,t}}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y,t}.}

Here
f_{*}
on the left denotes the pushforward map for Kato’s complex of Milnor
K
-theory (cf.
Section

3
), and
f_{*}
on the right denotes the Grothendieck trace map
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f,t}
for residual complexes.

Proof.

We only need to prove the proposition for
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
and for degree
i\in[-d,0]
. Then by the very definition of the
\zeta
map and the compatibility of the trace map with morphism compositions
Proposition

1.2
(4), it suffices to check the commutativity at points
x\in X_{(q)}
,
y\in Y_{(q)}
, where
q=-i
:

\textstyle{K^{M}_{q}(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{k(x)}^{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{q}(y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{k(y)}^{q}.}

(1)

If
y\neq f(x)
, both pushforward maps are zero maps, therefore we have the desired commutativity.

(2)

If
y=f(x)
, by definition of
\zeta
and the pushforward maps,
we need to show the commutativity of the following diagram for the finite field extension
k(y)\subset k(x)

\textstyle{K^{M}_{q}(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Nm}_{k(x)/k(y)}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{k(x)}^{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}}
\textstyle{K^{M}_{q}(y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{k(y)}^{q}.}

This is precisely
Lemma

5.3
.

∎

Proposition 5.6

(Étale pullback)
.

\zeta
is compatible with étale pullbacks. I.e., for
f:X\rightarrow Y
an étale morphism, the following diagram is commutative

\textstyle{C_{Y,t}^{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{n,Y,t}}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}{C^{M}_{X,t}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{n,X,t}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X,t}.}

Here
f^{*}
on the left denotes the pullback map for Kato’s complex of Milnor
K
-theory (cf.
Section

3
), and
f^{*}
on the right denotes the pullback map for residual complexes (
1.5.1
).

Proof.

It suffices to prove the proposition for
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
. Take
y\in Y_{(q)}
. Consider the cartesian diagram

\textstyle{X\times_{Y}\overline{\{y\}}=:W}
\scriptstyle{f|_{W}}
\scriptstyle{i_{W}}
\textstyle{\overline{\{y\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{y}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y.}

Then the desired diagram at point
y
decomposes in the following way at degree
-q
:

\textstyle{K^{M}_{q}(y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{k(y)}^{q}=K_{n,\overline{\{y\}}}^{-q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(f|_{W})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i_{y}}}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y}^{-q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{x\in W_{(q)}}K^{M}_{q}(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{x\in W_{(q)}}W_{n}\Omega_{k(x)}^{q}=K_{n,W}^{-q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i_{W}}}
\textstyle{K_{n,X}^{-q}.}

The left square commutes because both
f^{*}
and
(f\mid_{W})^{*}
are induced by the natural map
f^{*}:\mathcal{O}_{Y}\rightarrow f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
The right square commutes due to
Lemma

1.24
.
∎

5.3.
Extend to
K_{n,X,log,t}

Recall the complex
K_{n,X,log,t}:=\operatorname{Cone}(K_{n,X,t}\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}_{t}-1}K_{n,X,t})[-1]
, i.e.,

K_{n,X,log,t}^{i}=K_{n,X,t}^{i}\oplus K_{n,X,t}^{i-1}.

Notice that

(5.3.1)

K_{n,X,t}\rightarrow K_{n,X,log,t},\quad a\mapsto(a,0)

is not a chain map. Nevertheless,

Proposition 5.7
.

We keep the same assumptions as in
Proposition

5.1
. The chain map
\zeta_{n,X,t}:{C^{M}_{X,t}}\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X,t}

composed with (
5.3.1
) gives a chain map

\zeta_{n,X,log,t}:=(\ref{K to Klog map of graded algebras})\circ\zeta_{n,X,t}:{C^{M}_{X,t}}\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X,log,t}

of complexes of abelian sheaves on
(W_{n}X)_{t}
.

We will also use the shortened notation
\zeta_{log,t}
for
\zeta_{n,X,log,t}
. If
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
, the subscript
{\operatorname{Zar}}
will also be omitted.

Proof.

Given
x\in X_{(q)}
, we prove commutativity of the following diagram

\textstyle{\iota_{x*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,q,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{\iota_{x*}W_{n}\Omega_{k(x),log,t}^{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i_{x},t}}
\textstyle{K_{n,X,t}^{-q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}_{X,t}-1}
\textstyle{K_{n,X,t}^{-q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\iota_{x*}\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,q,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d\log}
\textstyle{\iota_{x*}W_{n}\Omega_{k(x),t}^{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C^{\prime}_{\overline{\{x\}},t}-1}
\textstyle{(i_{x,*}K_{n,\overline{\{x\}},t})^{-q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i_{x},t}}
\textstyle{K^{-q}_{n,X,t}.}

The left square naturally commutes. The right square also commutes, because
C^{\prime}
is compatible with the Grothendieck trace map
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i_{x}}
(the proofs of
Proposition

1.20
and
Proposition

1.29
give the case for
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
and
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
, respectively).
Now because
C^{\prime}_{\overline{\{x\}},t}-1:W_{n}\Omega_{k(x),t}^{q}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{k(x),t}^{q}
, which is identified with
C_{\overline{\{x\}},t}-1
as a result of
Theorem

1.9
and
Proposition

1.26
, annihilates
W_{n}\Omega_{k(x),log,t}^{q}
, the composite of the second row is zero. Thus the composite of the first row is zero.
This yields a unique chain map

\zeta_{n,X,log,t}:{C^{M}_{X,t}}\rightarrow K_{n,X,log,t},

i.e., on degree
i=-q
, we have
\zeta_{n,X,log,t}=\sum_{x\in X_{(q)}}\zeta_{n,x,log,t}
with

\displaystyle\zeta_{n,x,log,t}^{i}:\mathcal{K}^{M}_{x,q,t}

\displaystyle\rightarrow K_{n,X,log,t}^{i}=K_{n,X,t}^{i}\oplus K_{n,x,t}^{i-1},

\displaystyle s=\{s_{1}\dots,s_{q}\}

\displaystyle\mapsto(\zeta_{n,X,t}^{i}(s),0).

∎

As a direct corollary of
Proposition

5.5
and
Proposition

5.6
, one has the following proposition.

Proposition 5.8

(Functoriality)
.

(1)

\zeta_{log,t}
is compatible with proper pushforward. I.e., for
f:X\rightarrow Y
a proper map, the following diagram of complexes is commutative

\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}{C^{M}_{X,t}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{n,X,log,t}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{C_{Y,t}^{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{n,Y,log,t}}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y,log,t}.}

Here
f_{*}
on the left denotes the pushforward map for Kato’s complex of Milnor
K
-theory (cf.
Section

3
), and
f_{*}
on the right denotes
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f,log,t}
as defined in
Proposition

1.20
and
Proposition

1.29
.

(2)

\zeta_{log,t}
is compatible with étale pullbacks. I.e., for
f:X\rightarrow Y
an étale morphism, the following diagram of complexes is commutative

\textstyle{C_{Y,t}^{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{n,Y,log,t}}
\textstyle{K_{n,Y,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}{C^{M}_{X,t}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{n,X,log,t}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}f)_{*}K_{n,X,log,t}.}

Here
f^{*}
on the left denotes the pullback map for Kato’s complex of Milnor
K
-theory (cf.
Section

3
), and
f^{*}
on the right denotes the pullback map defined in
Proposition

1.23
.

5.4.
The map
\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}:{C^{M}_{X,t}}/p^{n}\simeq\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X,log,t}
is a quasi-isomorphism

Since
\zeta_{n,X,t}
is termwise defined via the
d\log
map, it annihilates
p^{n}C^{M}_{X,t}
. Therefore
\zeta_{n,X,log,t}
annihilates
p^{n}C^{M}_{X,t}
as well, and it induces a chain map

\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}:{C^{M}_{X,t}}/p^{n}\rightarrow K_{n,X,log,t}.

Since the
d\log
map induces an isomorphism of complexes
{C^{M}_{X,t}}/p^{n}\simeq\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}
, to show
\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}
is a quasi-isomorphism, it is equivalent to show

\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}\rightarrow K_{n,X,log,t}

is a quasi-isomorphism.

Lemma 5.9
.

Suppose
X
is separated smooth over the perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
. Then for any level
n
,

\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}};

is a quasi-isomorphism. If we moreover have
k=\overline{k}
, then

\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{Zar}}}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{Zar}}}

is also a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

This is a local problem, thus it suffices to prove the statement for each connected component of
X
. Therefore we assume
X
is of pure dimension
d
over
k
. Then for any level
n
, we have a quasi-isomorphism (
[
17
, Cor 1.6]
)

W_{n}\Omega_{X,log,t}^{d}[d]\xrightarrow{\simeq}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}.

We also have

\displaystyle W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}[d]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\quad\text{(by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{log qis complex log et}), and}

\displaystyle W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,log,{\operatorname{Zar}}}[d]

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{Zar}}}\quad\text{if $k=\overline{k}$ (by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{log qis complex log}).}

On degree
-d
, we have a diagram

\textstyle{\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}^{-d}=\bigoplus_{x\in X^{(0)}}(W_{n}\iota_{x})_{*}W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{k(x),log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\zeta}_{n,x,log,t}^{-d}}
\textstyle{\displaystyle K_{n,X,log,t}^{-d}=\bigoplus_{x\in X^{(0)}}(W_{n}\iota_{x})_{*}H_{x}^{0}(W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,t})}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{d}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega^{d}_{X,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

which is naturally commutative, due to the definition of
\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}
. It induces quasi-isomorphisms as stated in the lemma.
∎

Theorem 5.10
.

Let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over a perfect field
k
of characteristic
p>0
. Then the chain map

\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

is a quasi-isomorphism. Moreover if
k=\overline{k}
,

\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{Zar}}}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{Zar}}}

is also a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

One can assume that
X
is reduced. In fact, the complex
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}
is defined to be the same complex as
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X_{\mathrm{red}},t}
(see (
4.0.1
)), and we have a quasi-isomorphism
K_{n,X_{\mathrm{red}},log,t}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,t}
given by the trace map, according to
Proposition

1.21
and
Proposition

1.30
. One notices that
\overline{\zeta}_{n,X_{\mathrm{red}},log,t}
is compatible with
\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}
because of the functoriality of the map
\zeta_{log,t}
with respect to proper maps
Proposition

5.8
(1). As long as we have a quasi-isomorphism

\overline{\zeta}_{n,X_{\mathrm{red}},log,t}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X_{\mathrm{red}},t}\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X_{\mathrm{red}},log},

we will get automatically that

\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X_{\mathrm{red}},t}=\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}\xrightarrow{\overline{\zeta}_{n,X_{\mathrm{red}},log,t}}K_{n,X_{\mathrm{red}},log}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,t}

is a quasi-isomorphism.

Now we do induction on the dimension of the reduced scheme
X
. Suppose
X
is of dimension
d
, and suppose
\overline{\zeta}_{n,Y,log,t}
is a quasi-isomorphism for schemes of dimension
\leq d-1
. Now decompose
X
into the singular part
Z
and the smooth part
U

U\xhookrightarrow{j}X\xhookleftarrow{i}Z.

Then
Z
has dimension
\leq d-1
.
Consider the following diagram in the derived category of complexes of
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-modules

(5.4.1)

\textstyle{i_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,Z,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}\overline{\zeta}_{n,Z,log,t}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}}
\textstyle{Rj_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,U,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Rj_{*}\overline{\zeta}_{n,U,log,t}}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\textstyle{i_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,Z,t}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}\overline{\zeta}_{n,Z,log,t}[1]}
\textstyle{i_{*}K_{n,Z,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i,log}}
\textstyle{K_{n,X,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Rj_{*}K_{n,U,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\textstyle{i_{*}K_{n,Z,log,t}[1],}

where the two rows are distinguished triangles coming from
Proposition

1.22
,
Proposition

1.31
and
Proposition

4.1
.
We show that the three squares in (
5.4.1
) are commutative in the derived category. The left square is commutative because of
Proposition

5.8
(1).
The middle square is induced from the diagram

(5.4.2)

\textstyle{\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}}
\textstyle{j_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,U,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{*}\overline{\zeta}_{n,U,log,t}}
\textstyle{K_{n,X,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{*}K_{n,U,log,t}}

of chain complexes. Let
x\in X_{(q)}
. If
x\in X_{(q)}\cap U
, both
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}\rightarrow j_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,U,t}
and
K_{n,X,log,t}\rightarrow j_{*}K_{n,U,log,t}
give identity maps at
x
, therefore the square (
5.4.2
) commutes in this case.
If
x\in X_{(q)}\cap Z
, both of these give the zero map at
x
, therefore the square (
5.4.2
) is also commutative. The right square of (
5.4.1
) can be decomposed in the following way (cf. (
1.4.4
) and (
1.6.3
)):

\textstyle{Rj_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,U,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Rj_{*}\overline{\zeta}_{n,U,log,t}}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\textstyle{R\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}(\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t})[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}(\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t})[1]}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces i_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,Z,t}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}\overline{\zeta}_{n,Z,log,t}[1]}
\textstyle{Rj_{*}K_{n,U,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\textstyle{R\uline{\Gamma}_{Z}(K_{n,X,log,t})[1]}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces i_{*}K_{n,Z,log,t}[1].}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}

The map
i_{*}
on the first row is induced by the norm map of Milnor
K
-theory. It is clearly an isomorphism of complexes if
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
. It is a quasi-isomorphism if
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
due to the purity theorem
[
36
, p.130 Cor.]
. The map
i_{*}
on the second row is induced from
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i,log,t}
as defined in
Proposition

1.20
and
Proposition

1.29
, and it is an isomorphism due to
Proposition

1.22
(1) if
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
, and
Proposition

1.31
if
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
.
The first square commutes by the naturality of the
+1
map. The second commutes because of the compatibility of
\zeta_{log,t}
with the proper pushforward
Proposition

5.8
(1). We thus deduce that the right square of (
5.4.1
) commutes.

Now consider over any perfect field
k
for either of the two cases:

(1)

t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
and
k
is a perfect field, or

(2)

t={\operatorname{Zar}}
and
k=\overline{k}
.

The left vertical arrow of (
5.4.1
) is a quasi-isomorphism because of the induction hypothesis. The third one counting from the left is also a quasi-isomorphism because of
Lemma

5.9
. Thus so is the second one.
∎

6.
Combine
\psi_{X,t}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,t}\rightarrow{C^{M}_{X,t}}
with
\zeta_{n,X,log,t}:{C^{M}_{X,t}}\xrightarrow{}K_{n,X,log,t}

6.1.
The map
\psi_{X,t}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,t}\rightarrow C^{M}_{X,t}

In
[
44
, 2.14]
, Zhong constructed a map of abelian groups
\psi_{X,t}(X):\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}(X)\rightarrow C^{M}_{X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}(X)
based on the Nesterenko-Suslin-Totaro isomorphism
[
37
, Thm. 4.9]
[
42
]
.
It is straightforward to check that Zhong’s construction induces a well-defined map of complexes
\psi:=\psi_{X,t}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,t}\rightarrow C^{M}_{X,t}
of sheaves for both
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
and
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
.
Zhong in
[
44
, 2.15]
proved that
\psi
is covariant with respect to proper morphisms, and contravariant with respect to quasi-finite flat morphisms.

6.2.
The map
\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}\circ\overline{\psi}_{X,t}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,t}/p^{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,t}
is a quasi-isomorphism

In
[
44
, 2.16]
Zhong proved that

\psi_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

combined with the Bloch-Gabber-Kato isomorphism
[
4
, 2.8]
, gives a quasi-isomorphism

\overline{\psi}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}.

In the proof, Zhong actually showed that these two complexes of sheaves on each section of the big Zariski site over
X
are quasi-isomorphic. Therefore by restriction to the small Zariski site, we have

\overline{\psi}_{X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}/p^{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}.

Combining Zhong’s quasi-isomorphism with
Theorem

5.10
:

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over
k
with
k
being a perfect field of positive characteristic
p
.
Then the following composition

\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\circ\overline{\psi}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},

is a quasi-isomorphism. If moreover
k=\overline{k}
, then the following composition

\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{Zar}}}\circ\overline{\psi}_{X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}/p^{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{Zar}}},

is also a quasi-isomorphism.

Remark 6.2
.

From the construction of the maps
\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}
and
\overline{\psi}_{X,t}
, we can describe explicitly their composite map.
We write here only the Zariski case, and the étale case is just given by the Zariski version on the small étale site and then doing the étale sheafification.

Let
U
be a Zariski open subset of
X
. Let
Z\in(\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{Zar}}})^{i}(U)=z_{0}(U,-i)
be a prime cycle.

•

If
i\in[-d,0]
and
\dim p_{U}(Z)=-i
, set
q=-i
. Then
Z
as a cycle of dimension
q
in
U\times\Delta^{q}
, is dominant over some
u=u(Z)\in U_{(q)}
under the projection
p_{U}:U\times\Delta^{q}\rightarrow U
.
By slight abuse of notation, we denote by

T_{0},\dots,T_{q}\in k(Z)
the pullbacks of the corresponding coordinates via
Z\hookrightarrow U\times\Delta^{q}
.
Since
Z

intersects all faces properly,
T_{0},\dots,T_{q}\in k(Z)^{*}
.
Thus
\{\frac{-T_{0}}{T_{q}},\dots,\frac{-T_{q-1}}{T_{q}}\}\in K^{M}_{q}(k(Z))
is well-defined.
Take the Zariski closure of
\operatorname{Spec}k(Z)
in
U\times\Delta^{q}
, and denote it by
Z^{\prime}
. Then
p_{U}
maps
Z^{\prime}
to
\overline{\{u\}}^{U}=\overline{\{u\}}^{X}\cap U
.
Denote by
i_{u}:\overline{\{u\}}^{X}\hookrightarrow X
the closed immersion, and denote the composition

Z^{\prime}\xrightarrow{p_{U}}\overline{\{u\}}^{U}\hookrightarrow\overline{\{u\}}^{X}\xhookrightarrow{i_{u}}X

by
h
.
The map
h
is clearly generically finite, then there exists an open neighborhood
V
of
u
in
X
such that the restriction
h:h^{-1}(V)\rightarrow V
is finite. Then
W_{n}h:W_{n}(h^{-1}(V))\rightarrow W_{n}V
is also finite. Therefore it makes sense to consider the trace map
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}h}
near the generic point of
Z^{\prime}
. Similarly, it makes sense to consider the trace map
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}p_{U}}
near the generic point of
Z^{\prime}
.
Then we calculate

\displaystyle\zeta_{log}(\psi(Z))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i_{u}}(d\log(\operatorname{Nm}_{k(Z)/k(u(Z))}\{\frac{-T_{0}}{T_{q}},\dots,\frac{-T_{q-1}}{T_{q}}\}))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i_{u}}(\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}p_{U}}d\log\{\frac{-T_{0}}{T_{q}},\dots,\frac{-T_{q-1}}{T_{q}}\})\quad(\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{Compatibility of Milnor norm and Grothendieck trace}})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}h}(\frac{T_{q}dT_{0}-T_{0}dT_{q}}{T_{0}T_{q}}\cdots\frac{T_{q}dT_{q-1}-T_{q-1}dT_{q}}{T_{q-1}T_{q}})

Here in the last step we have used the functoriality of the trace map with respect to composition of morphisms (
Proposition

1.2
(4)).

•

If
i\notin[-d,0]
or
\dim p_{U}(Z)\neq-i
, we have
\zeta_{log}(\psi(Z))=0
.

Combining the functoriality of Zhong’s map
\psi
with
Proposition

5.8
, one arrives at the following proposition.

Proposition 6.3

(Functoriality)
.

The composition
\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}\circ\overline{\psi}_{X,t}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,t}/p^{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,t}
is covariant with respect to proper morphisms, and contravariant with respect to étale morphisms for both
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
and
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
.

Part III
Applications

7.
De Rham-Witt analysis of
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}
and
K_{n,X,log,t}

Let
X
be a separated scheme of finite type over
k
of dimension
d
. In this section we will use terminologies as defined in
[
7
, §1]
, such as Witt residual complexes, etc.

Recall that Ekedahl defined a map of complexes of
W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules (cf.
[
7
, Def. 1.8.3]
)

\uline{p}:=\uline{p}_{\{K_{n,X}\}_{n}}:R_{*}K_{n-1,X,t}\rightarrow K_{n,X,t}.

By abuse of notations, we denote by
R:W_{n-1}X\hookrightarrow W_{n}X
the closed immersion induced by the restriction map on the structure sheaves
R:W_{n}\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow W_{n-1}\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

The map
\uline{p}:R_{*}K_{n-1,X,t}\rightarrow K_{n,X,t}
induces a map of complexes of abelian sheaves

(7.0.1)

\uline{p}:K_{n-1,X,log,t}\rightarrow K_{n,X,log,t}

by applying
\uline{p}
on each summand.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
C^{\prime}_{t}:K_{n,X,t}\rightarrow K_{n,X,t}
commutes with
\uline{p}
for both
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
and
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
.
For
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
,
C^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
is the composition of
\tau^{-1}:K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
and
\epsilon^{*}(C^{\prime}_{\operatorname{Zar}}):(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
. Since
\tau^{-1}
is functorial with respect to any map of abelian sheaves, we know that

\textstyle{R_{*}K_{n-1,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\uline{p}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}R_{*}K_{n-1,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}}
\scriptstyle{\uline{p}}
\textstyle{K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{-1}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}}

is commutative, thus it suffices to prove the proposition for
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
.
That is, it suffices to show the diagrams (
7.0.2
) and (
7.0.3
) commute:

(7.0.2)

\textstyle{R_{*}K_{n-1,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{R_{*}(\ref{(1.0.2)})}
\scriptstyle{\uline{p}}
\textstyle{R_{*}(W_{n-1}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{{n-1},X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\uline{p}_{\{(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\}_{n}}}
\textstyle{K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{(1.0.2)})}
\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X},}

(7.0.3)

\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}R_{*}(W_{n-1}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{{n-1},X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}\uline{p}_{\{(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\}_{n}}}
\textstyle{R_{*}(W_{n-1}F_{X})_{*}(W_{n-1}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{{n-1},X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{*}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n-1}F_{X}}}
\textstyle{R_{*}K_{{n-1},X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\uline{p}}
\textstyle{(W_{n}F_{X})_{*}(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}F_{X}}}
\textstyle{K_{n,X}.}

Here
\uline{p}:=\uline{p}_{\{K_{n,X}\}_{n}}
is the lift-and-multiplication-by-
p
map associated to the Witt residual complex
\{K_{n,X}\}_{n}
, while
\uline{p}_{\{(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\}_{n}}
denotes the one associated to Witt residual system
\{(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\}_{n}
(cf.
[
7
, 1.8.7]
). By definition, the map

\uline{p}_{\{(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\}_{n}}:R_{*}(W_{n-1}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n-1,X}\rightarrow(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}

is given by the adjunction map of

(W_{n-1}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n-1,X}\xrightarrow[\simeq]{(W_{n-1}F_{X})^{\triangle}(^{a}\uline{p})}(W_{n-1}F_{X})^{\triangle}R^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\simeq R^{\triangle}(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X},

where
{}^{a}\uline{p}
is the adjunction of
\uline{p}
for residual complexes (cf.
[
7
, Def. 1.8.3]
). The second diagram (
7.0.3
) commutes because the trace map
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}F_{X}}
induces a well-defined map between Witt residual complexes
[
7
, Lemma 1.8.9]
.

It remains to show the commutativity of (
7.0.2
). According to the definition of
\uline{p}_{(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}}
in
[
7
, 1.8.7]
, we are reduced to show the adjunction square commutes:

\textstyle{R^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R^{\triangle}(\ref{(1.0.2)})}
\textstyle{R^{\triangle}(W_{n}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n-1}F_{X})^{\triangle}R^{\triangle}K_{n,X}}
\textstyle{K_{n-1,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{a}\uline{p}}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{(1.0.2)})}
\textstyle{(W_{n-1}F_{X})^{\triangle}K_{n-1,X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n-1}F_{X})^{\triangle}(^{a}\uline{p})}

And this is
(W_{n-1}\pi)^{\triangle}
applied to the following diagram

\textstyle{R^{\triangle}W_{n}k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R^{\triangle}(\ref{(1.0.1)})}
\textstyle{R^{\triangle}(W_{n}F_{k})^{\triangle}W_{n}k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{(W_{n-1}F_{k})^{\triangle}R^{\triangle}W_{n}k}
\textstyle{W_{n-1}k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{}^{a}\uline{p}}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{(1.0.1)})}
\textstyle{(W_{n-1}F_{k})^{\triangle}W_{n-1}k\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{(W_{n-1}F_{k})^{\triangle}(^{a}\uline{p})}

We are reduced to show its commutativity.
Notice that this diagram is over
\operatorname{Spec}W_{n-1}k
, where the only possible filtration is the one-element set consisting of the unique point of
\operatorname{Spec}W_{n-1}k
.
This means that the Cousin functor associated to this filtration sends any dualizing complex to itself, and the map
{}^{a}\uline{p}
in the sense of a map either between residual complexes
[
7
, Def. 1.8.3]
or between dualizing complexes
[
7
, Def. 1.6.3]
actually agree.

Now we start the computation.
Formulas for (
1.2.1
) and for
{}^{a}\uline{p}
(in the sense of a map between dualizing complexes) are explicitly given in
Section

1.2
and
[
7
, 1.6.4(1)]
, respectively.
Label the source and the target of
W_{n}F_{k}
by
\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k_{1}
and
\operatorname{Spec}k_{2}
respectively.
Take
a\in W_{n-1}k_{1}
.
Denote
\overline{W_{n}F_{k}}:(\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k_{1},W_{n}k_{1})\rightarrow(\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k_{2},(W_{n}F_{k})_{*}(W_{n}k_{1}))
, and
\overline{R}:(\operatorname{Spec}W_{n-1}k_{i},W_{n-1}k_{i})\rightarrow(\operatorname{Spec}W_{n}k_{i},R_{*}W_{n-1}k_{i})

(i=1,2)
the natural maps of ringed spaces.
Now the down-right composition
((W_{n-1}F_{k})^{\triangle}(^{a}\uline{p}))\circ(\ref{(1.0.1)})
equals to the Cousin functor
E_{(W_{n-1}F_{k})^{\triangle}R^{\triangle}Z^{\bullet}(W_{n}k)}
applied to the following composition

\displaystyle W_{n-1}k_{1}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\simeq]{(\ref{(1.0.1)})}\overline{W_{n-1}F_{k}}^{*}\operatorname{Hom}_{W_{n-1}k_{2}}((W_{n-1}F_{k})_{*}(W_{n-1}k_{1}),W_{n-1}k_{2})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\simeq]{{}^{a}\uline{p}}\overline{W_{n-1}F_{k}}^{*}\operatorname{Hom}_{W_{n-1}k_{2}}((W_{n-1}F_{k})_{*}(W_{n-1}k_{1}),\overline{R}^{*}\operatorname{Hom}_{W_{n}k_{2}}(R_{*}W_{n-1}k_{2},W_{n}k_{2})),

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto[(W_{n-1}F_{k})_{*}1\mapsto(W_{n-1}F_{k})^{-1}(a)]

\displaystyle\mapsto[(W_{n-1}F_{k})_{*}1\mapsto[R_{*}1\mapsto\uline{p}(W_{n-1}F_{k})^{-1}(a)]].

And
R^{\triangle}(\ref{(1.0.1)})\circ(^{a}\uline{p})
equals to the Cousin functor
E_{(W_{n-1}F_{k})^{\triangle}R^{\triangle}Z^{\bullet}(W_{n}k)}
applied the following composition

\displaystyle W_{n-1}k_{1}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[\simeq]{{}^{a}\uline{p}}\overline{R}^{*}\operatorname{Hom}_{W_{n}k_{1}}(R_{*}W_{n-1}k_{1},W_{n}k_{1})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{(\ref{(1.0.1)})}\overline{R}^{*}\operatorname{Hom}_{W_{n}k_{1}}(R_{*}W_{n-1}k_{1},\overline{W_{n}F_{k}}^{*}\operatorname{Hom}_{W_{n}k_{2}}((W_{n}F_{k})_{*}(W_{n}k_{1}),W_{n}k_{2})),

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mapsto[R_{*}1\mapsto\uline{p}(a)]

\displaystyle\mapsto[R_{*}1\mapsto[(W_{n}F_{k})_{*}1\mapsto(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}\uline{p}(a)]].

It remains to identify
\uline{p}((W_{n-1}F_{k})^{-1}a)
and
(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}\uline{p}(a)
. And this is straightforward: write
a=\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}V^{i}[a_{i}]\in W_{n-1}k_{1}
,

(7.0.4)

\displaystyle(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}\uline{p}(a)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}\uline{p}(V^{i}[a_{i}])=\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}(W_{n}F_{k})^{-1}(V^{i+1}[a_{i}^{p}])

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}(V^{i+1}[a_{i}])=\uline{p}\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}(V^{i}[a_{i}^{1/p}])=\uline{p}((W_{n-1}F_{k})^{-1}a).

Hence we finish the proof.
∎

However we don’t naturally have a restriction map
R
between residual complexes. Nevertheless, we can use the quasi-isomorphism
\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,t}
to build up a map

(7.0.5)

R:K_{n,X,log,t}\rightarrow K_{n-1,X,log,t}

in the derived category
D^{b}(X,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
. For this we will need to show that
\uline{p}
and
R
induce chain maps for
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}
. This should be well-known to experts, we add here again due to a lack of reference.

Lemma 7.2
.

\uline{p}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}\rightarrow\widetilde{\nu}_{n+1,X,t},\quad R:\widetilde{\nu}_{n+1,X,t}\rightarrow\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}

given by
\uline{p}
and
R
termwise, are well defined maps of complexes for both
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
and
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove for
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
.
Let
x\in X_{(q)}
be a point of dimension
q
. Let
\rho:X^{\prime}\rightarrow\overline{\{x\}}
be the normalization of
\overline{\{x\}}
. Let
x^{\prime}
be the generic point of
X^{\prime}
and
y^{\prime}\in X^{\prime(1)}
be a codimension
1
point. Denote
y:=\rho(y^{\prime})
.
It suffices to check the commutativity of the following diagrams in (1) and (2).

(1)

Firstly,

\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{x^{\prime},log}^{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{\uline{p}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{y^{\prime},log}^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\uline{p}}
\textstyle{W_{n+1}\Omega_{x^{\prime},log}^{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{W_{n+1}\Omega_{y^{\prime},log}^{q-1},}

\textstyle{W_{n+1}\Omega_{x^{\prime},log}^{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{W_{n+1}\Omega_{y^{\prime},log}^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{x^{\prime},log}^{q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{y^{\prime},log}^{q-1}.}

Notice that
p=\uline{p}\circ R
. Suppose
\pi^{\prime}
is a uniformizer of discrete valuation ring
\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime},y^{\prime}}
and
u_{1},\dots,u_{q}
are invertible elements in
\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime},y^{\prime}}
. Calculate

\displaystyle\uline{p}(\partial(d\log[\pi^{\prime}]_{n}

\displaystyle d\log[u_{2}]_{n}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n}))

\displaystyle=\uline{p}(d\log[u_{2}]_{n}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n})

\displaystyle=p(d\log[u_{2}]_{n+1}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n+1})

\displaystyle=p(\partial(d\log[\pi^{\prime}]_{n+1}d\log[u_{2}]_{n+1}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n+1}))

\displaystyle=\partial(p(d\log[\pi^{\prime}]_{n+1}d\log[u_{2}]_{n+1}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n+1}))

\displaystyle=\partial(\uline{p}(d\log[\pi^{\prime}]_{n}d\log[u_{2}]_{n}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n})),

and

\displaystyle\uline{p}(\partial(d\log[u_{1}]_{n}

\displaystyle d\log[u_{2}]_{n}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n}))

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle=p(\partial(d\log[u_{1}]_{n+1}d\log[u_{2}]_{n+1}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n+1}))

\displaystyle=\partial(p(d\log[u_{1}]_{n+1}d\log[u_{2}]_{n+1}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n+1}))

\displaystyle=\partial(\uline{p}(d\log[u_{1}]_{n}d\log[u_{2}]_{n}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n})).

This proves the first diagram. Now the second.

\displaystyle R(\partial(d\log[\pi^{\prime}]_{n+1}

\displaystyle d\log[u_{2}]_{n+1}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n+1}))

\displaystyle=R(d\log[u_{2}]_{n+1}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n+1})

\displaystyle=d\log[u_{2}]_{n}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n}

\displaystyle=\partial(d\log[\pi^{\prime}]_{n}d\log V[u_{2}]_{n}\dots d\log V[u_{q}]_{n})

\displaystyle=\partial(R(d\log[\pi^{\prime}]_{n+1}d\log[u_{2}]_{n+1}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n+1})),

and

\displaystyle R(\partial(d\log[u_{1}]_{n+1}

\displaystyle d\log[u_{2}]_{n+1}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n+1}))

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle=\partial(d\log[u_{1}]_{n}d\log[u_{2}]_{n}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n})

\displaystyle=\partial(R(d\log[u_{1}]_{n+1}d\log[u_{2}]_{n+1}\dots d\log[u_{q}]_{n+1})).

(2)

Secondly,

\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{y^{\prime},log}^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{tr}}
\scriptstyle{\uline{p}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{y,log}^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\uline{p}}
\textstyle{W_{n+1}\Omega_{y^{\prime},log}^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{tr}}
\textstyle{W_{n+1}\Omega_{y,log}^{q-1},}

\textstyle{W_{n+1}\Omega_{y^{\prime},log}^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{tr}}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{W_{n+1}\Omega_{y,log}^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{y^{\prime},log}^{q-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{tr}}
\textstyle{W_{n}\Omega_{y,log}^{q-1}.}

Notice that
\rho:X^{\prime}\rightarrow\overline{\{x\}}^{X}
can be restricted to a map from
\overline{\{y^{\prime}\}}^{X^{\prime}}
to
\overline{\{y\}}^{X}
(
\overline{\{x\}}^{X}
denotes the closure of
x
in
X
, and similarly for
\overline{\{y^{\prime}\}}^{X^{\prime}}
,
\overline{\{y\}}^{X}
). Furthermore,
y^{\prime}
(resp.
y
) belongs to the smooth locus of
\overline{\{y^{\prime}\}}^{X^{\prime}}
(resp.
\overline{\{y\}}^{X}
), and there
\uline{p}
and
R
come from the restriction of the
\uline{p}
and
R
on the respective smooth locus.
The map
\mathrm{tr}
, induced by Milnor’s norm map, agrees with the Grothendieck trace map
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}\rho}
due to
Lemma

5.3
. And according to compatibility of the Grothendieck trace map with the Witt system structure (i.e. de Rham-Witt system structure with zero differential) on canonical sheaves
[
7
, 4.1.4(6)]
, we arrive at the desired commutativity.

∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Assume either

•

t={\operatorname{Zar}}
and
k=\overline{k}
, or

•

t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
and
k
being a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
.

Then we have the following short exact sequence

(7.0.6)

0\rightarrow\widetilde{\nu}_{i,X,t}\xrightarrow{\uline{p}^{j}}\widetilde{\nu}_{i+j,X,t}\xrightarrow{R^{i}}\widetilde{\nu}_{j,X,t}\rightarrow 0,

in the category of complexes of sheaves over
X_{t}
, and a distinguished triangle

(7.0.7)

K_{i,X,log,t}\xrightarrow{\uline{p}^{j}}K_{i+j,X,log,t}\xrightarrow{R^{i}}K_{j,X,log,t}\xrightarrow{+1}

in the derived category
D^{b}(X_{t},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
.

Proof.

(1)

Because of
Lemma

7.2
, it suffices to show

0\rightarrow W_{i}\Omega^{q}_{x,log,t}\xrightarrow{\uline{p}^{j}}W_{i+j}\Omega^{q}_{x,log,t}\xrightarrow{R^{i}}W_{j}\Omega^{q}_{x,log,t}\rightarrow 0

is short exact for any given point
x\in X_{(q)}
. And this is true for
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
because of
[
8
, Lemme 3]
. And for
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
, one further needs
R^{1}\epsilon_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{x,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}^{q}=0
for any
x\in X_{(q)}
if
k=\overline{k}
, which is proved in
[
40
, Cor. 2.3]
.

(2)

Now it suffices to show that
\uline{p}
and
R
for the system
\{K_{n,X,log,t}\}_{n}
are compatible with
\uline{p}
and
R
of the system
\{\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}\}_{n}
, via the quasi-isomorphism
\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}
. The compatibility for
R
is clear by definition. It remains to check the compatibility for
\uline{p}
. Because
\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,log,t}=(\ref{K to Klog map of graded algebras})\circ\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,t}
, it suffices to check compatibility of
\uline{p}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n-1,X,t}\rightarrow\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}
with
\uline{p}:K_{n-1,X,t}\rightarrow K_{n,X,t}
via
\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,t}
. At a given degree
-q
and given point
x\in X_{(q)}
, the map
\overline{\zeta}_{n,X,t}:\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}\rightarrow K_{n,X,t}
factors as

(W_{n}\iota_{x})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{x,log,t}^{q}\xrightarrow{}(W_{n}i_{x})_{*}W_{n}\Omega_{x,t}^{q}=(W_{n}i_{x})_{*}K^{q}_{n,\overline{\{x\}},t}\xrightarrow{(-1)^{q}\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}i_{x},t}}K^{q}_{n,X,t}.

The first arrow is the inclusion map and is naturally compatible with
\uline{p}
. The compatibility of
\uline{p}
via the trace map is given in
[
7
, Lemma 1.8.9]
.

∎

8.
Higher Chow groups of zero cycles

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
. In the whole
Section

8
,
X
denotes a separated scheme of finite type over
k
of dimension
d
with structure map
\pi:X\rightarrow k
.

8.1.
First properties

Proposition 8.1
.

There is a distinguished triangle

\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n}\rightarrow K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-1}K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow{+1}

in the derived category
D^{b}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
.
If
k=\overline{k}
, one also has the Zariski counterpart. Namely, we have a distinguished triangle

(8.1.1)

\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n}\rightarrow K_{n,X}\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}-1}K_{n,X}\xrightarrow{+1}

in the derived category
D^{b}(X,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
.

In particular, if
k=\overline{k}
and
X
is Cohen-Macaulay of pure dimension
d
, then
\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n}
is concentrated at degree
-d
, and the triangle (
8.1.1
) becomes

\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n}\rightarrow W_{n}\omega_{X}[d]\xrightarrow{C^{\prime}-1}W_{n}\omega_{X}[d]\xrightarrow{+1}

in this case. Here
W_{n}\omega_{X}
is the only non-vanishing cohomology sheaf of
K_{n,X}
(if
n=1
,
W_{1}\omega_{X}=\omega_{X}
is the usual dualizing sheaf on
X
).

Proof.

This is direct from the main result
Theorem

6.1
and
Remark

1.17
.
∎

Proposition 8.2
.

Assume
k=\overline{k}
. Then higher Chow groups of zero cycles equal the
C^{\prime}
-invariant part of the cohomology groups of Grothendieck’s coherent dualizing complex, i.e.,

{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,q;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})=H^{-q}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X})^{C^{\prime}-1}.

Proof.

This follows directly from the
Proposition

1.15

and the main result
Theorem

6.1
.
∎

Corollary 8.3

(Relation with
p
-torsion Poincaré duality)
.

There is an isomorphism

K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\simeq R\pi^{!}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})

in
D^{b}(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
,
where
R\pi^{!}
is the extraordinary inverse image functor defined in
[
41
, Exposé XVIII, Thm 3.1.4]
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the main theorem
Theorem

5.10
and
[
27
, Thm. 4.6.2]
.
∎

Corollary 8.4

(Affine vanishing)
.

Suppose
X
is affine and Cohen-Macaulay of pure dimension
d
. Then

(1)

If
t={\operatorname{Zar}}
and
k=\overline{k}
,

{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,q,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})=0

for
q\neq d
.

(2)

If
t={\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
,

R^{-q}\Gamma(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n})=0

for
q\neq d,d-1
. If one further assumes
k=\overline{k}
or smoothness, the possible non-vanishing occurs only in degree
q=d
.

Proof.

If
X
is Cohen-Macaulay of pure dimension
d
,
W_{n}X
is also Cohen-Macaulay of pure dimension
d
by Serre’s
S_{k}
-criterion, and
K_{n,X,t}
is concentrated at degree
-d
for all
n

[
9
, 3.5.1]
. Now Serre’s affine vanishing theorem implies
H^{-q}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X,t})=0
for
q\neq d
. This implies that
R^{-q}\Gamma(W_{n}X,K_{n,X,log,t})=0
unless
q=d,d-1
. With the given assumptions,
Theorem

6.1
implies that
{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,q,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})=R^{-q}\Gamma(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n})=0
unless
q=d,d-1
.
If one also assumes
k=\overline{k}
,
Proposition

8.2
gives the vanishing result for
q=d-1
.

If
X
is smooth,
C_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}-1:W_{n}\Omega_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}^{d}\rightarrow W_{n}\Omega_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}^{d}
is surjective by
[
16
, 1.6(ii)]
(see (
1.3.35
)). By the compatibility of
C_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}
and
C^{\prime}_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}

Proposition

1.26
, one deduces that
C^{\prime}-1:\mathcal{H}^{-d}(K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})\penalty\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{-d}(K_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})
is surjective.
∎

Generalizing Bass’s finiteness conjecture for
K
-groups (cf.
[
43
, IV.6.8]
), the finiteness of higher Chow groups in various arithmetic settings appears in the literature.
The following result was first proved by Geisser
[
13
, §5, eq. (12)]
using the finiteness result from the étale cohomology theory, and here we deduce it as a corollary of our main theorem, which essentially relies on the finiteness of coherent cohomologies on a proper scheme. We remark that Geisser’s result is more general than ours in that he allows arbitrary torsion coefficients.

Corollary 8.5

(Finiteness, Geisser)
.

Assume
k=\overline{k}
. Let
X
be proper over
k
. Then for any
q
,

{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,q;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})\quad

is a finite
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-module.

Proof.

According to
Theorem

6.1
,
R^{-q}\Gamma(X,\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n})=R^{-q}\Gamma(X,K_{n,X,log})
.
Thus it suffices to show that for every
q
,
R^{-q}\Gamma(X,K_{n,X,log})
is a finite
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-module.
First of all, since the cohomology group
R^{-q}\Gamma(X,K_{n,X,log})
is the
C^{\prime}
-invariant part of
R^{-q}\Gamma(X,K_{n,X})
by
Proposition

1.15
and
Proposition

1.27
,
R^{-q}\Gamma(X,K_{n,X,log})
is a module over the invariant ring
(W_{n}k)^{1-W_{n}F_{X}^{-1}}=\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
.
Because
X
is proper,
R^{-q}\Gamma(X,K_{n,X})
is a finite
W_{n}k
-module by the local-to-global spectral sequence. Then
Proposition

A.7
gives us the result.

Alternatively, we can also do induction on
n
.
In the
n=1
case, because
R^{-q}\Gamma(X,K_{X,log})
is the
C^{\prime}
-invariant part of the finite dimensional
k
-vector space
H^{-q}(X,K_{X})
again by
Proposition

1.15
and
Proposition

1.27
, it is a finite
\mathbf{F}_{p}
-module by
p^{-1}
-linear algebra
Proposition

A.3
.
The desired result then follows from the long exact sequence associated to (
7.0.7
) by induction on
n
.
∎

We refer to
Definition

A.4
and
Remark

A.5
(2) for the definition of the semisimplicity and the notation
(-)_{\mathrm{ss}}
in this context.

Corollary 8.6

(Semisimplicity)
.

Assume
k=\overline{k}
. Let
X
be proper over
k
. Then for any
q
,

H^{-q}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X})_{\mathrm{ss}}={\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,q;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}}W_{n}k.

Proof.

Since
X
is proper,
H^{-q}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X})
is a finite
W_{n}k
-module for any
q
.
Then according to
Proposition

A.8
,

H^{-q}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X})_{\mathrm{ss}}=H^{-q}(W_{n}X,K_{n,X})^{C^{\prime}-1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}}W_{n}k.

The claim now follows from
Proposition

8.2
.
∎

8.2.
Étale descent

The results
Proposition

8.7
,
Proposition

8.8
in this subsection are well-known to experts.

Proposition 8.7

(Gros-Suwa)
.

Assume
k=\overline{k}
. Then one has a canonical isomorphism

\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}=\epsilon_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}R\epsilon_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}

in the derived category
D^{b}(X,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
.

Proof.

If
k=\overline{k}
, terms of the étale complex
\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}
are
\epsilon_{*}
-acyclic according to
[
16
, 3.16]
.
∎

The étale descent of Bloch’s cycle complex with
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficients is shown in
[
13
, Thm 3.1]
, assuming the Beilinson-Lichtenbaum conjecture which is now proved by Rost and Voevodsky. Hence the étale descent holds conjecture-free.
Note that one can also deduce the mod
p^{n}
version as a corollary of
Proposition

8.7
via Zhong’s quasi-isomorphism in
Section

6.2
(which is dependent on the main result of Geisser-Levine
[
14
, 1.1]
).

Proposition 8.8

(Geisser-Levine)
.

Assume
k=\overline{k}
. Then one has a canonical isomorphism

\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}/p^{n}=\epsilon_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n}\xrightarrow{\simeq}R\epsilon_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n}.

in the derived category
D^{b}(X,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
.
As a result,

{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,q;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})\simeq R^{-q}\Gamma(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n}).

Proof.

Clearly, we have the compatibility

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}/p^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{R\epsilon_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R\epsilon_{*}\psi_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{R\epsilon_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}.}

Thus

\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}/p^{n}\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\overline{\psi}_{X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{Zar}}}=\epsilon_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow[\simeq]{\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{etale descent tilde vnX}}}R\epsilon_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\xrightarrow[\simeq]{R\epsilon_{*}\overline{\psi}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}}R\epsilon_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n}.

∎

Corollary 8.9
.

Assume
k=\overline{k}
. Suppose
X
is affine and Cohen-Macaulay of pure dimension
d
. Then

R^{i}\epsilon_{*}(\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n})=R^{i}\epsilon_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}=0,\quad i\neq-d.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of
Proposition

8.8
,
Proposition

8.7
and
Corollary

8.4
.
∎

8.3.
Birational geometry and rational singularities

Recall the following definition of
resolution-rational singularities
, which are more often called
rational singularities
before in the literature, but here we follow the terminology from
[
33
]
(see also
Remark

8.11
(1) and
[
33
, (9.12.1)]
).

Definition 8.10

(cf.
[
33
, 9.1]
)
.

An integral
k
-scheme
X
is said to have
resolution-rational singularities
, if

(1)

there exists a birational proper morphism
f:\widetilde{X}\rightarrow X
with
\widetilde{X}
smooth (such a
f
is called a
resolution of singularities
or simply a
resolution
of
X
), and

(2)

R^{i}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}}=R^{i}f_{*}\omega_{\widetilde{X}}=0
for
i\geq 1
. And
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

Such a map
f:\widetilde{X}\rightarrow X
is called a
rational resolution
of
X
.

Note that the cohomological condition (
2
) is equivalent to the following condition

(2’)

\mathcal{O}_{X}\simeq Rf_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}}
,
f_{*}\omega_{\widetilde{X}}\simeq Rf_{*}\omega_{\widetilde{X}}
in the derived category of abelian Zariski sheaves.

Remark 8.11
.

(1)

According to
[
33
, 8.2]
, on integral
k
-schemes of pure dimension, our definitions for resolution-rational singularities and for rational resolutions are the same as the ones in
[
33
, 9.1]
.

(2)

Necessary conditions for an integral
k
-scheme to have resolution-rational singularities,
are that the scheme is normal and Cohen-Macaulay.
The normality statement follows from the equality
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\widetilde{X}}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
, and the Cohen-Macaulay statement is a standard result, see e.g.
[
33
, 8.3]
.

(3)

According to
[
33
, 9.6]
, resolution-rational singularities are pseudo-rational. By definition
[
33
, 1.2]
, a
k
-scheme
X
is said to have
pseudo-rational singularities
, if it is normal Cohen-Macaulay, and for every normal scheme
X^{\prime}
, every projective birational morphism
f:X^{\prime}\rightarrow X
, the composition
f_{*}\omega_{X^{\prime}}\rightarrow Rf_{*}\omega_{X^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}_{f}}\omega_{X}
is an isomorphism.

Corollary 8.12
.

Let
X
and
Y
be integral
k
-schemes of pure dimensions which have pseudo-rational singularities.
Suppose there are proper birational
k
-morphisms
f:Z\rightarrow X
and
g:Z\rightarrow Y
where
Z
is a normal Cohen-Macaulay scheme. Then we have

R^{-q}\Gamma(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{\widetilde{X},{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n})=R^{-q}\Gamma(Y_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{Y,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n})

for all
q
and all
n\geq 1
.
If we assume furthermore
k=\overline{k}
, we also have

{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,q,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})={\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(Y,q,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})

for all
q
and all
n\geq 1
.

Remark 8.13
.

(1)

In particular, since for any rational resolution of singularities
f:\widetilde{X}\rightarrow X
,
\widetilde{X}
and
X
are properly birational as
k
-schemes (i.e., take
Z
to be
\widetilde{X}
), one can compute the higher Chow groups of zero cycles of
X
via those of
\widetilde{X}
.

(2)

Deleting the pseudo-rational singularities assumption (in particular, we relax the Cohen-Macaulay assumptions on
X
and
Y
), the proof still passes through with the following assumption:
X
and
Y
are linked by a chain of proper birational maps and each of these maps has its trace map being a quasi-isomorphism between the residual complexes. Such a proper birational map is called a
cohomological equivalence
in
[
33
, 8.4]
.

(3)

If normal Macaulayfications for integral varieties exist (e.g., conjecture
[
33
, 1.13]
is true), the assumption of
Corollary

8.12
can be weakened to the following:
Let
X
and
Y
be integral
k
-schemes of pure dimensions which have pseudo-rational singularities and are properly birational, i.e., there are proper birational
k
-morphisms
f:Z\rightarrow X
and
g:Z\rightarrow Y
with
Z
being some integral scheme.

In fact, we can replace
Z
by a normal Cohen-Macaulay scheme by the following process.
Using Chow’s Lemma
[
33
, 4.1]
, we know that there exist projective birational morphisms
f^{\prime}:Z_{1}\rightarrow Z
and
g^{\prime}:Z_{2}\rightarrow Z
such that the compositions
Z_{1}\xrightarrow{f^{\prime}}Z\xrightarrow{f}X
and
Z_{2}\xrightarrow{g^{\prime}}Z\xrightarrow{g}Y
are also birational and projective. Let
U\subset Z
be an open dense subset such that
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
restricted to the preimage of
U
are isomorphisms. Take
Z^{\prime}
be the Zariski closure of the image of the diagonal of
U
in
Z_{1}\times_{Z}Z_{2}
with the reduced scheme structure. Then the two projections
Z^{\prime}\rightarrow Z_{1}
and
Z^{\prime}\rightarrow Z_{2}
are also projective and birational.
This means that by replacing
Z^{\prime}
with
Z
,
f
with
Z^{\prime}\rightarrow X
and
g
with
Z^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
, we can assume our
f:Z\rightarrow X
,
g:Z\rightarrow Y
to be projective birational and our
Z
to be integral.
Using normal Macaulayfication
[
33
, 1.13]
we can additionally assume that
Z
is normal Cohen-Macaulay.

In particular, since the conjecture
[
33
, 1.13]
is known to be true for varieties of dimension at most 4 over algebraically closed fields (cf.
[
33
, 1.14(iii)]
), one can state
Corollary

8.12
with this weakened assumption in this case.

Proof.

Note that
f
and
g
are pseudo-rational modifications by
[
33
, 9.7]
.
Suppose that
X
is of pure dimension
d
. Then so is
Z
. Now
[
33
, 8.6]
implies that the trace map of
f
induces an isomorphism

\operatorname{Tr}_{f}:Rf_{*}K_{Z,t}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{X,t}

in
D^{b}(X_{t},\mathbb{Z}/p)
. Thus

\operatorname{Tr}_{f,log}:Rf_{*}K_{Z,log,t}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{X,log,t}

is also an isomorphism in
D^{b}(X_{t},\mathbb{Z}/p)
. Consider the diagram

(8.3.1)

\textstyle{f_{*}K_{Z,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\uline{p}^{n-1}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{f,log}}
\textstyle{f_{*}K_{n,Z,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f,log}}
\textstyle{f_{*}K_{n-1,Z,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n-1}f,log}}
\textstyle{f_{*}K_{Z,log,t}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Tr}_{f,log}[1]}
\textstyle{K_{X,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\uline{p}^{n-1}}
\textstyle{K_{n,X,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{K_{n-1,X,log,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\textstyle{K_{X,log,t}[1]}

in
D^{b}(X_{t},\mathbb{Z}/p)
. The first row is
Rf_{*}
applied to the triangle (
7.0.7
) on
Z
. The second row is the triangle (
7.0.7
) on
X
