. The left square commutes on the level of complexes by the compatibility of the trace map with
\uline{p}

[
7
, 1.8.9]
. To prove commutativity of the middle square in the derived category, it suffices to show the square

\textstyle{f_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n,Z,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{f_{*}\widetilde{\nu}_{n-1,Z,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\nu}_{n,X,t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\nu}_{n-1,X,t}}

commutes on the level of complexes. Since the vertical maps
f_{*}
for Kato-Moser complexes are
\mathrm{tr}

(cf. §
4
), which are by definition the reduction of the norm maps for Milnor
K
-theory, they agree with the Grothendieck trace maps
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f}
,
\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n-1}f}
by
Lemma

5.3
.
And according to the compatibility of
R
with the Grothendieck trace maps
[
7
, 4.1.4(6)]
, we arrive at the desired commutativity.
The right square in (
8.3.1
) commutes by the naturality of the "
+1
" map.
With all these commutativities we conclude that the vertical maps in (
8.3.1
) define a map of triangles. By induction on
n
we deduce that

\operatorname{Tr}_{W_{n}f,log}:Rf_{*}K_{n,Z,log,t}\xrightarrow{\simeq}K_{n,X,log,t}

is an isomorphism in
D^{b}(X_{t},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
for every
n
. The main result
Theorem

6.1
thus implies

R^{-q}\Gamma(Z_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{\widetilde{X},{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n})=R^{-q}\Gamma(X_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n})

for all
q
and
n
. If
k=\overline{k}
, the same theorem also implies that

{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(Z,q,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})={\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,q,\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})

for all
q
and
n
.

Now replacing
f
by
g
everywhere in the above argument and we get the result.
∎

8.4.
Galois descent

Corollary 8.14
.

Let
X
and
Y
be separated schemes of finite type over
k
of dimension
d
.
Let
f:Y\rightarrow X
be a finite étale Galois map with Galois group
G
. Then

R^{-d}\Gamma(X_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n})=R^{-d}\Gamma(Y_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{Y}/p^{n})^{G}.

If
k=\overline{k}
, we also have

{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,d;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})={\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(Y,d;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})^{G}.

Proof.

The pullback
f^{*}
induces two canonical maps

f^{*}:\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow(f_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{Y,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})^{G},\quad f^{*}:K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow(f_{*}K_{n,Y,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})^{G}.

Both of them are isomorphisms of complexes, because each term of these complexes is an étale sheaf.
Because of the contravariant functoriality with respect to étale morphisms (
Proposition

6.3
and
[
44
, 2.15]
),
\overline{\zeta}_{log}\circ\overline{\psi}
is
G
-equivariant. That is, the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\zeta}_{log}\circ\overline{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{(f_{*}\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{Y,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}/p^{n})^{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\zeta}_{log}\circ\overline{\psi}}
\textstyle{(f_{*}K_{n,Y,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})^{G}}

commutes.

Apply
R^{-d}\Gamma(X_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},-)
to the isomorphism
f^{*}:K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}\rightarrow(f_{*}K_{n,Y,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})^{G}
, one gets

R^{-d}\Gamma(X_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},K_{n,X,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})=R^{-d}\Gamma(X_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},(f_{*}K_{n,Y,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})^{G}).

Consider the local-to-global spectral sequence associated to the right hand side of this equality, there is only one non-zero term in the
E_{\infty}
-page with total degree
-d
(which is a term in the
E_{2}
-page), thus we have

R^{-d}\Gamma(X_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},(f_{*}K_{n,Y,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})^{G})=H^{0}(X_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\mathcal{H}^{-d}((f_{*}K_{n,Y,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})^{G})).

Because
(-)^{G}
commutes with taking kernels and with
H^{0}
, we have

H^{0}(X_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\mathcal{H}^{-d}((f_{*}K_{n,Y,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})^{G}))=H^{0}(X_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\mathcal{H}^{-d}(f_{*}K_{n,Y,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}))^{G}.

Because
f_{*}
preserves kernels, we have

H^{0}(X_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\mathcal{H}^{-d}(f_{*}K_{n,Y,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}))^{G}=H^{0}(Y_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathcal{H}^{-d}(K_{n,Y,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}))^{G}.

Again by the observation from the spectral sequence, this means

H^{0}(Y_{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}},\mathcal{H}^{-d}(K_{n,Y,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}}))^{G}=R^{-d}\Gamma(Y_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},K_{n,Y,log,{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}})^{G}.

Since
\overline{\zeta}_{log}\circ\overline{\psi}
is
G
-equivariant, the main theorem
Theorem

6.1
implies

R^{-d}\Gamma(X_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{X}/p^{n})=R^{-d}\Gamma(Y_{{\operatorname{\acute{e}t}}},\mathbb{Z}^{c}_{Y}/p^{n})^{G}.

If
k=\overline{k}
,
Proposition

8.8
implies

{\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(X,d;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})={\operatorname{CH}}_{0}(Y,d;\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})^{G}.

∎

Appendix

Appendix A
Semilinear algebra

Definition A.1
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of positive characteristic
p
, and
V
be a finite dimensional
k
-vector space. A
p
-linear map
(resp.
p^{-1}
-linear map) on
V
is a map
T:V\rightarrow V
, such that

\displaystyle T(v+w)=T(v)+T(w),\quad T(cv)=c^{p}T(v),\quad v,w\in V,c\in k.

(resp.

\displaystyle T(v+w)=T(v)+T(w),\quad T(cv)=c^{-p}T(v),\quad v,w\in V,c\in k.\text{)}

We say a map
T:V\rightarrow V
is
semilinear
if it is either
p
-linear or
p^{-1}
-linear. A semilinear map
T:V\rightarrow V
is called
semisimple
, if
{\operatorname{Im}}T=V
.

Remark A.2
.

Let
T
be a semilinear map.

(1)

Note that

\{c\in k\mid c^{p}=c\}=\mathbf{F}_{p}=\{c\in k\mid c^{-p}=c\}.

The fixed points of
T

V^{1-T}:=\{v\in V\mid T(v)=v\}

is a
\mathbf{F}_{p}
-vector space.

(2)

There is a descending chain of
k
-vector subspaces of
V

{\operatorname{Im}}T\supset{\operatorname{Im}}T^{2}\supset\dots\supset{\operatorname{Im}}T^{n}\supset\dots.

Since
V
is finite dimensional, it becomes stationary for some large
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
Define

V_{\mathrm{ss}}:=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}{\operatorname{Im}}(T^{n})={\operatorname{Im}}(T^{N})={\operatorname{Im}}(T^{N+1})=\dots.

Obviously,

(a)

V_{\mathrm{ss}}
is a
k
-vector subspace of
V
that is stable under
T
.

T
is semisimple on
V_{\mathrm{ss}}
.

(b)

V^{1-T}\subset V_{\mathrm{ss}}.

The proof of the following result is given in
[
20
]
for
p
-linear maps, but an analogous proof also works for
p^{-1}
-linear maps.

Proposition A.3

(
[
20
, Exposé XXII, Cor. 1.1.10, Prop. 1.2]
)
.

Suppose
k
is a separably closed field of positive characteristic
p
.
Then

1-T:V\rightarrow V

is surjective. And

V_{\mathrm{ss}}\simeq V^{1-T}\otimes_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}k,

which in particular means
V^{1-T}
is a finite dimensional
\mathbf{F}_{p}
-vector space with
\dim_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}V^{1-T}=\dim_{k}V_{\mathrm{ss}}
.

We generalize the definition of a semilinear map.

Definition A.4
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of positive characteristic
p
, and
let
W_{n}k
be the ring of the
n
-th truncated Witt vectors of
k
. Let
M
be a finitely generated
W_{n}k
-module.
A
p
-linear map
(resp.
p^{-1}
-linear map
) on
M
is a map
T:M\rightarrow M
, such that

\displaystyle T(m+m^{\prime})=T(m)+T(m^{\prime}),\quad T(cm)=W_{n}F_{k}(c)T(m),\quad m,m^{\prime}\in M,c\in W_{n}k.

(resp.

\displaystyle T(m+m^{\prime})=T(m)+T(m^{\prime}),\quad T(cm)=W_{n}F_{k}^{-1}(c)T(m),\quad m,m^{\prime}\in M,c\in W_{n}k.\text{)}

Here
F_{k}
denotes the
p
-th power Frobenius on the field
k
. We say that
T
is
semilinear
if it is either
p
-linear or
p^{-1}
-linear in this sense.
A semilinear map
T:V\rightarrow V
is called
semisimple
, if
{\operatorname{Im}}T=V
.

Remark A.5
.

Let
T
be a semilinear map in the sense of
Definition

A.4
.

(1)

Write
\sigma=W_{n}F_{k}
(resp.
\sigma=W_{n}F_{k}^{-1}
). Then

(W_{n}k)^{1-\sigma}:=\{c\in W_{n}k\mid\sigma(c)=c\}=\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}

for both cases.
The fixed points of
T

M^{1-T}:=\{m\in M\mid T(m)=m\}

is a
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-module.

(2)

As in the case of vector spaces,

{\operatorname{Im}}T\supset{\operatorname{Im}}T^{2}\supset\dots\supset{\operatorname{Im}}T^{n}\supset\dots

is a descending chain of
W_{n}k
-submodules of
M
. It becomes stationary for some large
N\in\mathbb{N}
, because
M
as a finitely generated
W_{n}k
-module is artinian.
Define the
W_{n}k
-submodule of
M

M_{\mathrm{ss}}:=\bigcap_{n\geq 1}\mathrm{Im}(T^{n})=\mathrm{Im}(T^{N})=\mathrm{Im}(T^{N+1})=\dots.

Then

(a)

M_{\mathrm{ss}}
is a
W_{n}k
-submodule of
M
that is stable under
T
.
T
is semisimple on
M_{\mathrm{ss}}
.

(b)

M^{1-T}\subset M_{\mathrm{ss}}
.

(c)

(M/p)_{\mathrm{ss}}=M_{\mathrm{ss}}/p\subset M/p
.

Proposition A.6
.

Let
k
be a separably closed field of positive characteristic
p
.
Then

1-T:M\rightarrow M

is surjective.

Proof.

Take
m\in M
. Because
M
is finitely generated as a
W_{n}k
-module,
M/pM
is a finite dimensional
k
-vector space. Then
Proposition

A.3
implies that there exists a
m^{\prime}\in M
, such that

(1-T)(m^{\prime})-m\in pM
. That is, there exists a
m_{1}\in M
such that

(1-T)(m^{\prime})=m+pm_{1}.

Do the same process with
m_{1}
instead of
m
, one gets a
m^{\prime}_{1}\in M
and a
m_{2}\in M
such that

(1-T)(m^{\prime}_{1})=m_{1}+pm_{2}.

Thus

(1-T)(m^{\prime}-pm^{\prime}_{1})=m-p^{2}m_{2}.

Repeat this process. After finitely many times, because
p^{n}=0
in
W_{n}k
,

(1-T)(m^{\prime}-pm^{\prime}_{1}+\dots+(-1)^{n-1}p^{n-1}m^{\prime}_{n-1})=m.

∎

Proposition A.7
.

Let
k
be a separably closed field of positive characteristic
p
.
Then

(1)

M^{1-T}/(pM)^{1-T}=(M/p)^{1-T}.

(2)

M^{1-T}
is a finite
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-module.

Proof.

Since
W_{n}k
is of
p^{n}
-torsion, we know that
p^{m}M=0
for some
m\leq n
. Do induction on the smallest number
m
such that
p^{m}M=0
. If
m=1
, the first claim is trivial, and
M=M/p
is actually a finite dimensional
k
-vector space, thus the second claim follows from
Proposition

A.3
.

Now we assume
m>1
.
Note that
T
induces a semilinear map on
pM
and
pM
is a finite
W_{n}k
-module, so by
Proposition

A.6
the map
1-T:pM\rightarrow pM
is surjective.
Now we have the two rows on the bottom of the following diagram being exact:

\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{1-T}/(pM)^{1-T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M/p\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1-T}
\textstyle{M/p\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M^{1-T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1-T}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(pM)^{1-T}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{pM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1-T}
\textstyle{pM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The vertical maps between the last two rows are natural inclusions, and the first row is the cokernels of these inclusion maps. The snake lemma implies that the first row is exact, which means that

M^{1-T}/(pM)^{1-T}=(M/p)^{1-T}.

This is a finite
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-module by the case
m=1
.
On the other hand, since
p^{m-1}\cdot pM=0
, the induction hypothesis applied to the
W_{n}k
-module
pM
gives
(pM)^{1-T}
is a finite
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-module. Now the vertical exact sequence on the left gives that
M^{1-T}
is a finite
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-module.
∎

Proposition A.8
.

Let
k
be a separably closed field of positive characteristic
p
. Then we have an identification of
W_{n}k
-modules

M_{\mathrm{ss}}\simeq M^{1-T}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}}W_{n}k.

Proof.

For the finite dimensional
k
-vector space
M/p
,
Proposition

A.3
tells us that

(M/p)_{\mathrm{ss}}\simeq(M/p)^{1-T}\otimes_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}k

In other words, there exists
m_{1},\dots,m_{d}\in M
(
d=\dim_{\mathbf{F}_{p}}M/p
), such that
m_{1}+pM,\dots,m_{d}+pM\in(M/p)^{1-T}
generate
(M/p)_{\mathrm{ss}}
as a
k
-vector space.
Because of
Proposition

A.7
(1), one can choose
m_{1},...,m_{d}\in M^{1-T}
.
Since
M
is a finite generated
W_{n}k
-module,
M_{\mathrm{ss}}
as a submodule is also finitely generated over
W_{n}k
.
Note moreover that
(M/p)_{\mathrm{ss}}=M_{\mathrm{ss}}/p
.
Apply Nakayama’s lemma,
m_{1},...,m_{d}\in M^{1-T}
generate
M_{\mathrm{ss}}
as an
W_{n}k
-module.
∎
